# vsms — project context

A2P SMS gateway for Cameroon (MTN + Orange). OTP and notification delivery, provider abstraction over HTTP APIs and SMPP, Next.js admin console.

**Read [`docs/architecture.md`](docs/architecture.md) before making design decisions.** It is the spec, and every claim in it was verified against the real toolchain rather than assumed. This file is the subset that must be in context for *every* session.

## Status

**Milestone 0 done.**

- `schemas/vsms.cstack` — 23 models (as of #64's `RouteValidation`; this line had drifted to "20" since #68 — actual count was already 22 before this PR, from #72's `ConsentRecord`), 13 enums, 7 procedures. Parses, emits DDL, and expands through `include_server_schema!`.
- `backends/migrations/postgres/0001_init/` — generated by `cratestack migrate diff`. **Never hand-edit.** Regenerated wholesale on every schema change at this pre-production stage — see below.
- `backends/migrations/postgres/0002_bootstrap/` — generated from §2.10 of the design doc by `cargo xtask bootstrap-sql` (see the "Repo automation: `xtask`" section below — this was `ci/gen-bootstrap-sql.py` before that PR). **Edit the doc, then regenerate.** CI diffs them.
- `backends/crates/sms-encoding/` and `backends/crates/sms-msisdn/` — done: pure, no framework dependency, `cargo test --workspace` green including the French-corpus gate.
- `backends/crates/sms-api/` — **the schema expands.** `include_server_schema!`, the `Principal → CratestackContext` projection, the seven-procedure registry, router assembly, `SM001`/`23505` error mapping. 87+ generated routes.
- `backends/apps/sms-gateway/` — the binary: `serve` and `routes`.
- `OperatorPrefixRule` — models `sms_msisdn::OperatorPrefixTable`'s backing store, seeded with 14 rows of current best evidence (MTN `67x`/`650`–`654`, Orange `69x`/`655`–`659`, `68x` contested, Camtel `62x` unverified). `previewMessage` still reports `operator: unknown` — the table exists and is seeded, but no procedure queries it yet. That's real work, not a gap in this milestone: `previewMessage` is synchronous and pure today, and querying the DB means giving it a cache and a refresh policy. Natural fit is milestone 2 or 5, alongside the routing code that has the same need.

`previewMessage` is the one live procedure; the other six return an error naming the milestone that will build them.

**Milestone 1 done.** The auth shape is decided ([#6](https://github.com/vymalo/vsms/issues/6)): `authkestra-op` stays, pinned `=0.3.3`, and machine callers authenticate with **`private_key_jwt`** — no shared client secret exists anywhere in this system. The `GrantType` `#[serde(untagged)]` bug that shaped the original design is **fixed in 0.3.2** (verified by round-tripping every variant; see §4.2). Schema and migrations for it have landed: `OauthClient` lost `secretHash` and gained `tokenEndpointAuthMethod` + `jwks`, plus new `OauthSigningKey` and `ClientAssertion` models. `/token` rate limiting is explicitly out of scope — it is a reverse-proxy concern, and with no Argon2 at the token endpoint the original urgency is gone.

Three things about that schema are load-bearing and easy to undo by accident:

- **`tokenEndpointAuthMethod` has no `@default` on purpose.** A `@default` would drop it from `CreateOauthClientInput`, and a *missing* method is how authkestra spells "accepts a secret from either transport, refuses assertions" — the one state `private_key_jwt` must never reach. `backends/crates/sms-api/tests/create_inputs.rs` turns its disappearance into a compile error.
- **`@sensitive` on `OauthSigningKey.privateKeyPem` is not confidentiality.** It redacts audit snapshots only; the model's `hasRole('system')` read policy is the entire control. `system` is synthetic and no issued token may carry it — an `AuthProvider` that mints `system` for HTTP callers hands out the key that signs every token, through generated CRUD.
- **`ClientAssertion` is insert-only, written as `create` + catching `23505`.** authkestra requires `record_jti` to be atomic; a read-then-write is the TOCTOU race the table exists to prevent.

`#19`'s `SmsClientStore`/`SmsClientAssertionStore` landed. `#20` (OIDC provider) and `#21` (real `AuthProvider`) landed together in #97, superseding a stale earlier attempt (#85) whose own review (B1–B5, P1–P4) is the source-of-truth for what got fixed: `backends/crates/sms-auth/src/op.rs` — RS256 key management with overlap-window rotation, `CompositeOpStore` assembly, `OpConfig`; `backends/crates/sms-api/src/auth.rs`'s `GatewayAuth` replaces `DenyAll`; `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/op.rs` mounts the OP's three routes plus a `rotate-signing-key` CLI subcommand — deliberate, per §4.2 the OP's signing key is not a schema default, so an operator must run it once before `/token` can issue anything (**it fails at process start** if no active key exists, not lazily on the first `/token` request — an earlier revision of this file claimed the opposite, which is wrong: `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/main.rs`'s `serve` arm calls `sms_auth::op::load_signing_keys(...)?` before binding the listener, so the `?` propagates straight out of `main`. Found while ordering a compose file's startup dependencies in #139, which is why the deploy runbook uses `docker compose run --rm` for the rotate step rather than `exec` against a gateway that could never have come up). Found live, not by reading source: leaving `jsonwebtoken::Validation::set_audience` uncalled does **not** skip audience validation the way it does inside `authkestra_resource::jwt::JwtStrategy`'s own construction — the library still requires an explicit match once a token carries an `aud` claim, which every `client_credentials` token does; fixed with an explicit `validation.validate_aud = false`. Also found live: `GET /oauth_signing_keys` under role `"app"` returns zero rows, not a `403` — CrateStack's list-route policy denial is row-level filtering to an empty array, and `backends/crates/sms-auth/tests/oidc_flow_live.rs` asserts that real shape rather than the assumed one. Scope cut, explicit in `sms_auth::op`'s module doc: no authorization-code/human-login flow (no admin console exists yet to need it) and no scope/permission-layer (§4.6 Layer 2) enforcement — machine callers only, tracked separately as #23/#24/#25.

#98 closed a gap found while closing out #18's own checklist after #97, not caught by #97's bot review: the discovery document at `/.well-known/openid-configuration` never advertised `private_key_jwt` in `token_endpoint_auth_methods_supported`, even though `/token` had accepted it since #97 merged. `authkestra_axum::op::axum_discovery_handler` builds the document straight from `OidcDiscovery::from_config` with no hook to chain `authkestra_op::handlers::discovery::OidcDiscovery::with_private_key_jwt()` onto the result, so `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/op.rs` now hand-rolls `discovery_handler`, the same pattern the file already uses for `jwks_handler` (the library handler can only ever publish one signing key). `oidc_flow_live.rs` duplicates `op.rs`'s route assembly — `backends/apps/sms-gateway` is a binary crate with no `lib.rs`, so its modules aren't importable from a test in `sms-auth` — and carries a matching permanent assertion that fetches the live discovery document over real HTTP and checks the advertised methods, not just a unit test of `OidcDiscovery` in isolation.

M1 closed with #23/#24/#25 landing as three separate PRs (#111/#112/#113), which closed epic #18.

`#23` (`provisionAppClient`, #111): both the schema's own `ProvisionClientResult { clientSecret: String }` and the issue's own text ("returning the secret once", "`client_secret_hash` must be `NOT NULL`") predated the `private_key_jwt` decision two paragraphs up and still described a shared-secret flow — caught before writing any implementation by cross-checking `backends/crates/sms-auth/src/lib.rs`'s existing `client_secret_hash: None` hardcode and its own comment ("no column can hold one... this is the only value that could ever be correct here"). Resolved by changing `ProvisionClientResult` to return `privateKeyPem` instead of a secret: the procedure generates a 2048-bit RSA keypair server-side (mirroring `rotate_signing_key`'s own pattern in `backends/crates/sms-auth/src/op.rs`), persists only the derived public JWK (`OauthClient.jwks` = `{"keys": [<JWK>]}`, one key, `kid` = `clientId`), and returns the private key exactly once — never stored anywhere. `AppClient` + `OauthClient` write in one `serializable` transaction. Client retirement with an overlap window (the issue's third bullet) was explicitly cut: `OauthClient.update` is still `hasRole('system')`-only, no owner/admin write path exists yet, and a true overlap-window rotation would need a per-client key-history model shaped like `OauthSigningKey`'s; `AppClient.active`/`retiredAt` are the documented, coarser fallback for now.

`#24` (RBAC layers 1 and 2, #112): Layer 1 (`@@allow`) was already mostly in place; this ticket's Layer-1 contribution is regression coverage — `policy_golden_list_live_postgres.rs` and `field_expansion.rs` — for the two silent-failure modes §2.0 warns about (a misspelled `@@allow` action, or `@@use` instead of `@use`). Layer 2 is new: `sms_api::require_permission(ctx, required)` checks `perms` (human/role vocabulary) or `scope` (service-account vocabulary) per §5.2, failing closed on anything missing or non-matching. Wired into `Procedures::send` (`sms:send`) and, via a Tower layer (`rbac::enforce_route_permission`, `router::PROVIDER_WRITE_ROUTES`), onto `PATCH /providers/{id}`. That route's permission literal shipped as `provider:write` in the first draft — caught by Lightbridge's review as matching nothing in §5.2's actual vocabulary (`operator` holds `provider:read/update`, never `provider:write`), which would have permanently denied a legitimate operator token the moment one could exist; fixed to `provider:update` before merge. Real, documented limitation: `GatewayAuth` never mints anything but `role: "app"`/`"system"` for a genuine token (no human-login flow exists yet — same scope cut as #97/#98), so Layer 2's *allow* case on that route has no live token to prove it with; Layer 1 alone already closes the route to everything this deployment can currently issue, and Layer 2 becomes load-bearing rather than defense-in-depth the moment a role-bearing token exists.

`#25` (the M1 acceptance gate, #113): the one test in this workspace that spawns two genuinely separate `sms-gateway serve` OS processes (`std::process::Command` + `CARGO_BIN_EXE_sms-gateway`, `kill()` + `wait()` between them) rather than building the router in-process like every other live suite — the issue's own reason: "an in-memory-only test... is worthless here." Provisions via `Procedures::provision_app_client` directly rather than over HTTP against the first process, since `provisionAppClient` requires `owner`/`admin` and no real token in this deployment can carry that role (same gap `#24` already documented). What does cross the process boundary over real HTTP, against the second, freshly spawned process: the `/token` exchange and a protected mutation — the actual persistence claim this gate exists to prove. Its RBAC-denial half hand-signs a test-only `developer`-role token with the real active OP signing key (there is no OAuth flow in this deployment that could mint one) — a deliberate, documented stand-in for a future human-login path, not a real issuance route.

**`aws-lc-rs` enters this tree from authkestra alone — cratestack selects `ring` throughout.** An earlier revision of this file claimed four independent paths including cratestack and sqlx; that was **wrong**, and read off a misinterpreted `cargo tree -i aws-lc-rs`. That command lists everything depending on the shared `rustls`, not everything *asking for* `aws-lc-rs`; because Cargo unifies features, one `rustls` in the graph carries both providers' feature flags regardless of who requested which. Read `cargo tree -e features -i rustls` instead, which shows the actual feature edges.

What the sources say: `cratestack-sqlx` selects `sqlx-core`'s `_tls-rustls-ring-webpki`, and `cratestack-client-rust` selects `reqwest`'s `rustls-tls`, which in reqwest 0.12 expands to `rustls-tls-webpki-roots` → `__rustls-ring`. Both hard-select **ring**. The `aws-lc-rs` edge is `reqwest 0.13`'s *default* features via `authkestra-engine` — exactly what [marcjazz/authkestra#179](https://github.com/marcjazz/authkestra/pull/179) (in 0.3.3) made opt-out-able.

Confirmed empirically rather than argued: setting `default-features = false, features = ["rustls-no-provider"]` on the four authkestra pins makes `cargo tree -i aws-lc-rs` report *"did not match any packages"* — it leaves the graph entirely. **So a static musl/`scratch` build or a `cargo-deny` policy banning `aws-lc-rs` is achievable today, with no upstream blocker.** Not done here because `rustls-no-provider` panics at `reqwest::Client::new()` unless the application installs a `CryptoProvider` in `main` first, and that runtime path is unverified — do that work deliberately, with a live TLS test, not as a drive-by.

**That deliberate work landed.** The four authkestra pins carry `default-features = false, features = ["rustls-no-provider"]` (root `Cargo.toml`); `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/main.rs` and `backends/apps/sms-worker/src/main.rs` each call `rustls::crypto::ring::default_provider().install_default()` as the literal first line of `main`, before anything else runs, so it happens before any dependency — including ones this repo's own code never directly calls, like authkestra's internal flow/discovery/captcha HTTP clients — has a chance to construct a `reqwest::Client`. `ring`, not `aws-lc-rs`, matching this section's own "cratestack selects ring throughout." `backends/apps/sms-gateway/tests/tls_no_provider_live.rs` is the live TLS test that was missing: a second, independently-featured `reqwest 0.13.4` dependency (`reqwest-no-provider`, same version already in the graph via authkestra's own pin) performs a real HTTPS GET against `https://api.orange.com/` — the same host `ORANGE_CM_BASE_URL` defaults to in production — after installing the provider, and the test was verified to actually fail (the exact panic this section describes, reproduced on purpose by temporarily removing the install call, then restored) before being trusted to pass for the right reason. Corroborated independently, not just by this repo's own test: `cratestack-client-rust`'s own `CratestackClient::new` (found while investigating this mechanism, in `cratestack-client-rust-0.7.10/src/client/core.rs`) calls the identical `rustls::crypto::ring::default_provider().install_default()` before building its own client — the same fix, arrived at independently upstream.

`cargo tree -i aws-lc-rs` now reports no match against this repo's own `Cargo.lock`, not just a synthetic probe — confirmed by rerunning it after the pin change, not assumed from the paragraph above. `Cargo.lock`'s diff for this change removed eight packages outright: `aws-lc-rs`, `aws-lc-sys`, `cmake`, `pkg-config`, `dunce`, `fs_extra`, `jobserver`, and (unexpectedly, but harmlessly — checked, and nothing in this workspace's own code path constructs it) `authkestra-macros`, which turns out to be behind a default feature none of the four pins need with `default-features = false` set.

Related, and a good example of why the `crypto-aws-lc-rs` feature is *not* the lever: it was an empty feature whose `install_fips_crypto_provider()` returned `Ok(())` without installing anything, i.e. a false assurance in a compliance-facing API. Reported as [cratestack#334](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/issues/334), and [cratestack#341](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/pull/341) made enabling it a hard `compile_error!` (in 0.6.5+). Real FIPS support still needs the backend-selection work; the feature is now honest about not existing rather than silently lying.

Note also two `reqwest` majors coexist (0.12 via cratestack, 0.13 via authkestra). Pre-existing, harmless today, but it means TLS-feature reasoning has to be done per-major — and it's why the two sides picked different providers in the first place.

**`#87` is fixed — and it is the reference example for why this repo distrusts a green `cargo test`.** Found while verifying `#19` against a live database: `cratestack-sqlx` `=0.5.0`–`=0.5.2` discarded SQLSTATE/constraint on *every* generated write, so `map_database_error`'s `SM001`→409 mapping and `is_illegal_transition` silently did nothing against a real database, despite `#78` shipping specifically to guarantee them. Filed as [cratestack/cratestack#267](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/issues/267), fixed in `cratestack-sqlx` 0.6.0 (all twelve write paths now route through `cratestack_error_from_sqlx`); the pin is `=0.6.7`.

The reason it went unnoticed through `#78` is worth internalising: every test of that mapping constructed `CratestackError::DatabaseTyped` **by hand**, which is correct unit coverage of the mapping function and structurally incapable of seeing the conversion feeding it. `cargo build`/`cratestack check` can't see it either — the signature is `Result<T, CratestackError>` either way. `backends/crates/sms-api/tests/errors_live_postgres.rs` now covers it end to end through a real delegate call, and was confirmed to fail against `=0.5.2` and pass against `=0.6.3`+ before being trusted. **Never pin the cratestack family below 0.6.0.**

**Milestone 2 in progress.** `backends/crates/sms-worker` (library) + `backends/apps/sms-worker` (binary, package `sms-worker-bin` — see its module doc for why the package name differs from the produced executable) exist: `--roles` parsing and validation, the six roles from §7.1 with their `Cardinality`, and a `run(role)` per-role stub that logs once and then genuinely idles (`std::future::pending`) rather than busy-looping, matching `sms-api`'s "clearly-labelled, not a lying stub" convention for the six unimplemented procedures (`#27`).

Leader election landed on top of that (`#28`): `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/lease.rs` — an R1 exception, allowlisted by path in `ci/assert-no-raw-sqlx.sh`. Deliberately **doesn't** use §7.2's own illustrative `pool.acquire()` sample — a `PoolConnection` returns to the pool rather than closing on drop, so a lease dropped without an explicit `release()` (a panic, a `kill -9`) would leak the lock at the Postgres level forever, unreachable, on a session silently recycled to serve unrelated future queries. Verified live that this trap is real (a second, independent connection stayed unable to acquire a lock left behind by a dropped `PoolConnection`) and that `RoleLease` avoids it (a dedicated, never-pooled `PgConnection` — dropping it closes the socket immediately, and Postgres's own session-advisory-lock semantics release the lock the moment that session ends, no cooperation from `sqlx` required). `run_singleton` wires this into a retry loop (5s interval, per §7.2) behind a `CancellationToken`, releasing explicitly on SIGINT/SIGTERM as the fast path and falling back to the drop-triggered release on a hard kill. Manually verified end to end against a real two-node setup: standby correctly stands by while the other holds the lock, `kill -9` failover reclaims within one retry cycle (~5s, no cooperation from the killed process needed), graceful SIGTERM triggers explicit release before exit.

The CAS claim loop landed next (`#29`): `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/claim.rs`. A `Claimable` trait — `candidates()` plus `take_lease()` via `if_match(version)` — so the loop that actually matters (treat `PreconditionFailed` as a lost race and retry, log `Forbidden` loudly rather than folding it into the race branch, propagate anything else) is written once and reused per model; `Job` (`#35`) and `WebhookAttempt` (M3 `#40`) each add their own `impl Claimable` later rather than re-deriving this. `impl Claimable for Message` is §7.3's own worked example, with one correction: the candidate state filter is `accepted`/`queued`/**`routed`**, not just the first two — §7.3's own sample omits `routed`, but `take_lease` transitions into it, `routed -> queued` is a legal edge (§7.4), and `messages_lease_reclaim_idx` (§2.10, committed since milestone 0) is already built `WHERE ... state IN ('queued','routed')`. Without `routed` in the filter, a worker that crashes between claiming a message and finishing it leaves that row permanently stuck — caught by Greptile's review of `#93`, confirmed against the doc's own index before fixing.

`#30`–`#32` landed together in #94: `backends/crates/sms-provider` (the `SmsProvider` trait, `Capabilities`, a `ProviderError` taxonomy with a compiler-checked mapping to routing consequences), `backends/crates/sms-provider-orange-cm` (the Orange CM HTTP adapter, OAuth2 `client_credentials` token caching), and `sendMessage` itself — replacing the milestone-not-reached stub with §3.2's nine pre-persistence steps, live in `Procedures::send()`. A new generic `TtlCache<K,V>` (`backends/crates/sms-api/src/cache.rs`) backs both the client_id→App and operator-prefix lookups. Found live by the new `send_message_live_postgres.rs` (9 tests), not by inspection: four pre-existing schema policy gaps, all the same shape — `AppClient` create, `App`/`AppClient` read, `SenderIdRegistration` read, `OperatorPrefixRule` read were all missing `hasRole('system')`, which `sendMessage`'s internal system-context logic needs. Each fix was verified individually to produce a byte-identical DDL diff via `cratestack migrate diff` before moving to the next — this is the first of what turns out to be a recurring bug shape through the rest of M2.

`#33` — `Role::Dispatch`'s real body — landed in #96: `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/dispatch.rs`, the poll loop wired over #29's claim loop and #94's provider abstraction, walking `accepted -> queued -> routed -> submitted` per §7.4, with `ProviderError` classified onto the failure edges. Single-provider degradation is deliberate and documented, not a gap: `Permanent`/`Unavailable`/`TryNextRoute`/`OpenCircuitAndTryNextRoute` all collapse onto whichever §7.4 edge fits, since no second provider exists yet for real failover (M5, `#63`). Two real bugs found landing it: `claim.rs`'s `take_lease` wrote `-> routed` unconditionally regardless of the candidate's actual state, making the illegal edge `accepted -> routed` reachable; and — the more consequential one — **`#29`'s own live suite (`claim_live_postgres.rs`, 7 tests, previously written but never run against Postgres) ran for the first time in #96**, which is what surfaced that `Message`'s `list`/`detail` policies had never admitted `hasRole('system')` at all: the claim loop's own query had silently returned zero rows since `#29` merged, undetected because nothing had run that suite live until now. Both fixed; `claim_live_postgres.rs`, the new `dispatch_live_postgres.rs` (5 tests, `wiremock`-backed `OrangeCmProvider`), and `send_message_live_postgres.rs` all pass stably across interleaved reruns against the same persistent database.

`#34` — DLR ingestion — landed in #99: `POST /dlr/{providerKey}`, a raw axum route rather than CrateStack-routed, since a provider webhook carries no bearer token to validate against `GatewayAuth`. `backends/crates/sms-api/src/dlr.rs` matches a `DeliveryUpdate` against a `Message` by `providerMessageRef`/`providerMessageRefAlt`, writes the `DeliveryReceipt`, and drives the resulting transition; `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/dlr.rs` owns route wiring and provider-key dispatch, the same procedure-vs-router split `sendMessage` already uses. One correctness case the design doc's transition table implies but doesn't spell out, caught by the live suite (`dlr_ingestion_live_postgres.rs`, 5 tests): a retryable failure arriving while a message is `uncertain` must land in `failed`, not `undelivered` — `uncertain -> undelivered` isn't a legal edge in `message_state_transitions`. Explicitly out of scope, documented in `dlr.rs`'s own module doc: `undelivered -> queued` retry has no driver yet (`claim.rs`'s `candidates()` doesn't select `undelivered`), and DLR webhook signature verification — no scheme is documented for any provider yet, though `RawCallback` already preserves exact bytes for when one lands.

`#35` — the `jobs` role and generic job queue — landed in #100: `Claimable for Job` (`backends/crates/sms-worker/src/claim.rs`) adapts the same CAS discipline to `Job`'s own crash-reclaim semantics, `running -> pending`, a two-hop reclaim rather than `Message`'s same-state resume — proven live by asserting the handler isn't called on the reclaim tick, only on the claim after it. `Role::Jobs`'s real body (`backends/crates/sms-worker/src/jobs.rs`) claims, dispatches by `kind` through a `JobHandler` registry, and transitions on the outcome with backoff/`dead` handling; `Role::Scheduler`'s real body (`backends/crates/sms-worker/src/scheduler.rs`) enqueues due recurring jobs with singleton-owned, in-process cadence tracking — correct as long as exactly one instance holds the lease, and its dedupe path knowingly inherits `#87`'s already-documented `db_sqlstate()` gap (blast radius: a spurious error log, not data corruption, since the DB's unique index still blocks the insert either way). Exactly one real job kind is wired up for this MVP, `expire_stale` (`backends/crates/sms-worker/src/jobs/expire_stale.rs`, two rules: `submitted` past `expiresAt`, `uncertain` past its 6h grace) — the other eight §7.5 names (`poll_balance`, `probe_providers`, `reap_outbox`, `reconcile_clients`, `purge_retention`, `cleanup_secrets`, `anchor_audit`, `verify_backup`) are explicitly out of scope, each blocked on infrastructure this milestone doesn't build. Per #100's own count, `Job.list`/`detail` missing `hasRole('system')` was the **sixth** instance of the exact same policy-gap shape (`App`, `AppClient`, `SenderIdRegistration`, `OperatorPrefixRule`, `Provider` before it) — same fix, same byte-identical-DDL verification each time.

`#102` is fixed (each live-Postgres test binary now serializes against itself — see `ca653a1`).

`#36` (the M2 gate) has its automatable half done: `backends/apps/sms-worker/tests/kill9_reclaim_live.rs` spawns the real `sms-worker` binary against a live Postgres and a wiremock-delayed Orange stand-in, sends it a genuine `SIGKILL` mid-submit (not an in-process simulation — `Child::kill()`, so no Rust destructor ever runs), and proves the row survives as `routed` with no `providerMessageRef`, then that a second, independent process reclaims and resubmits it (same `attempts`, a new `providerMessageRef`). It also pins down, as a permanent regression assertion rather than a one-off manual finding: the resubmit is a genuinely new outbound call, so a crash in this exact window produces two real submissions — `providerMessageRef` has no DB-level uniqueness constraint and nothing today gives Orange a dedup key. That's a tracked, accepted gap, not something this test hides. `docs/runbooks/36-handset-gate.adoc` (from `#106`) covers what can't be automated — a real Orange handset receiving `delivered` within 15s, and a human-timed `kill -9` against a real (not mocked) Orange account — and stays the actual gate for closing `#36`; both remain to be run by a human with real credentials and a real phone.

**`ProviderError::Indeterminate` (#119) closed the gap where a timed-out submit was indistinguishable from an outage.** Before it, the five variants (`Permanent`/`Transient`/`Unavailable`/`Rejected`/`Unsupported`) had no way to say *"the request was sent and we never learned the outcome"*, so `sms-provider-orange-cm` mapped every transport failure — including a read timeout arriving after Orange may already have accepted the message — to `Unavailable`, whose own contract is "fail this submission over to the next route". That is a resubmit, and with no idempotency key anywhere it means a duplicate SMS. Meanwhile `uncertain`, the state that exists precisely to mean "we don't know", was reachable **only** from an inbound DLR.

Three parts, each with a trap:

- **The predicate is connect-vs-read, not "is it a timeout".** `is_connect()` is checked first and stays `Unavailable` — nothing was written, retry is safe. Only past the connect phase (`is_timeout() || is_body()`) is the outcome genuinely unknown. Getting this backwards either destroys legitimate failover or leaves the double-send in place. A 2xx whose body is malformed or missing `resourceURL` is also `Indeterminate` — Orange accepted it before we failed to read the reply.
- **`routed -> uncertain` had to be added to the state machine** (§2.10's table *and* §7.4's diagram, with `0002_bootstrap` regenerated, never hand-edited), because a timed-out submit leaves the row in `routed` with no provider ref — only `submitted -> uncertain` existed.
- **The message then leaves the claim candidate set permanently** (`candidates()` selects `accepted`/`queued`/`routed`), so it is never resubmitted. It stays resolvable because `providerMessageRefAlt` is stamped with `Message.id` on that path specifically — the value sent as Orange's `callbackData` *before* the network call, and therefore known regardless of outcome. The first attempt shipped without that stamp and a later DLR had nothing to correlate against; caught by the live test, not by review.

**The tradeoff is deliberate and is a product decision, not a free win: this trades a possibly *lost* message for never sending a *duplicate*.** For OTP traffic that is right — a duplicate OTP is worse than one the user re-requests. If notification traffic ever wants the opposite, the mapping needs to differ per message class.

**A fault-injecting fake of Orange, and a chaos suite over the message state machine, exist as of the `fake-orange-chaos` PR — a further automatable complement to `docs/runbooks/36-handset-gate.adoc`, and explicitly does not close `#36` either.** `backends/crates/sms-fake-orange` is a participant, not a response stub: it answers the token/submit HTTP calls per a `FaultPolicy` (`Scripted` — an exact ordered sequence, for deterministic CI-gate tests; `Seeded` — a seeded PRNG drawing a realistic-weighted mix, always reproducible by seed), and independently schedules DLR deliveries as real HTTP POSTs against a caller-supplied `/dlr/{providerKey}` endpoint — including a DLR that deliberately races the submit response, proving the documented "`providerMessageRef` may not be persisted yet" correlation gap is real: at that instant neither `providerMessageRef` nor `providerMessageRefAlt` is set (the latter is only stamped by `write_submitted`/`write_transition`, both of which run *after* the race), so the DLR is silently dropped and the message later reaches a real terminal state via `expire_stale`, not stuck and not wrongly `delivered`. `backends/crates/sms-worker/tests/chaos_live_postgres.rs` hand-rolls the same small `POST /dlr/{providerKey}` handler `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/dlr.rs` owns in production (same "binary crate has no `lib.rs`" constraint `oidc_flow_live.rs` already worked around) against a real `axum::serve` on an ephemeral port, so the fake's DLR scheduler makes a genuine HTTP round trip into this system. Ten scripted tests cover one fault mode each (no DLR ever, the race above, duplicate/out-of-order/unknown-ref/post-terminal DLRs, 429-then-recovery, outright rejection, a malformed/missing-resourceURL 201, a post-accept timeout, and a 401 token failure); five seeded sweeps (fixed seeds 1–5, ~7s each) drive a batch of messages through a weighted-random fault mix and assert invariants rather than exact outcomes — no message lost (terminal, or the separately-documented `undelivered` gap below), nothing left claimable, `attempts <= maxAttempts`, and — checked against the fake's own request ledger, not this system's database — a message that went `uncertain` via `Indeterminate` is never submitted again. Connection-level nastiness (RST mid-response, refused connections, byte-dribble) is explicitly out of scope, noted as future work: `wiremock`'s `Respond` trait can only ever emit a well-formed HTTP response, and the connect-refused half is already covered directly, against a real socket, by `sms-provider-orange-cm`'s own `a_connect_refusal_is_still_unavailable`.

Two real findings, not invented ones: **`DeliveryReceipt`'s `list`/`detail` policy was missing `hasRole('system')`** — the *seventh* instance of the exact same gap shape this file has now recorded six times before (`App`, `AppClient`, `SenderIdRegistration`, `OperatorPrefixRule`, `Provider`, `Job`) — found by the chaos suite's own `a_duplicate_delivered_dlr_is_idempotent` scripted test trying to read back `DeliveryReceipt` rows under a `system` context and getting zero. Fixed the same way as every prior instance (`schema.cstack` only — an `@@allow` change never touches DDL, confirmed both by rereading `#100`'s own commit, which states its analogous `Job` fix produced a byte-identical migration diff, and by diffing `cratestack migrate diff`'s output against the committed `0001_init` before and after this change). **Second, the chaos suite empirically confirms `dlr.rs`'s own documented `undelivered` gap is real, not just prose:** nothing currently drives `undelivered -> queued` (no retry path exists yet), and `expire_stale` only reaps `submitted`/`uncertain`, not `undelivered` — so a message that receives exactly one retryable-failure DLR and no follow-up sits in `undelivered` forever under this system's current scope — filed as [#122](https://github.com/vymalo/vsms/issues/122), since retryable failures silently vanishing is the worst-shaped bug an SMS gateway can have: not delivered, not failed, not expired, not reaped, and invisible to any alert. The seeded sweep's own invariant treats that as an accepted, documented outcome rather than "message lost," matching this file's own prior framing precisely rather than re-litigating it.

**`#134` closed the gap between what `docs/architecture.md:588` claimed and what `msisdnHash` actually was.** The doc had always said HMAC-SHA256 under a server-held pepper; `backends/crates/sms-api/src/procedures.rs`'s `sha256_hex` was plain, unkeyed `SHA-256` — reversible by brute force in seconds over Cameroon's mobile numbering space (~10^7 candidates, per `backends/crates/sms-msisdn`'s own prefix ranges), which means a "purge" that cleared `msisdn` but kept `msisdnHash` had not de-identified anything. The function's own doc comment already said the `sha256:` prefix existed "so a future second hash scheme... is distinguishable from this one" — it knew what was missing; the doc simply was never corrected to match, and this file never recorded the divergence. Found by a read-only audit ahead of a planned production release, filed and fixed in the same PR.

The fix: `backends/crates/sms-api/src/pepper.rs` — a `HashPepper` newtype (hand-written `Debug`, not derived, so it can never leak through a struct that does derive `Debug`; a 32-byte minimum length, rejecting a trivially short pepper) wrapping real secret material, and `hmac_sha256_hex`, the one function that turns a pepper + plaintext into `hmac-sha256-v1:<hex>`. `Procedures::new` now takes a `HashPepper` — its `Default` impl is gone entirely (this repo's own standing preference: a hard cutover, never a default that has to invent a pepper), so every construction site supplies one explicitly: the router (from `sms-gateway serve`'s new required `--hash-pepper`/`SMS_HASH_PEPPER` clap arg, validated and constructed *before* the pool connects, so a missing or too-short pepper fails at startup, never at the first `sendMessage`), `examples/send_test_message.rs` (its own copy of the same flag), and every live-Postgres test. `bodyHash` is peppered under the same scheme, for the same reason (a templated OTP body is exactly as low-entropy and enumerable as an MSISDN) — decided only after grepping every reader of `bodyHash` in this tree and the admin console's generated TS client and confirming it is write-only, never cross-compared against a value computed outside this system.

`sms-worker` needed no changes: it never computes `msisdnHash`/`bodyHash` at all (`grep -rn Sha256` across `crates/` and `app/` confirms the computation lives in exactly one place), so there was no second binary to keep in lockstep — only `sms-gateway` and the standalone `send_test_message` example construct a `Procedures`, and both now require the same pepper by construction, not by convention.

No dual-read/rehash path was built, and per this file's own "no live database anywhere yet" fact, none was needed — every `sha256:`-prefixed value in this tree was test fixture data, updated to `hmac-sha256-v1:` for consistency, not migrated. The rotation consequence for when real traffic exists is documented in `pepper.rs`'s own module doc rather than solved: rotating the pepper does not retroactively rehash a single stored row, a row that still holds plaintext `msisdn` *can* be rehashed by a job that doesn't exist yet, a row whose `msisdn` has already been purged never can, and until any rehashing happens, opt-out matching and dedupe against old rows silently stop working the moment the pepper rotates — a `msisdnHash` computed under the new pepper never equals one stored under the old one, and nothing detects the mismatch.

One test-authoring trap worth internalising for next time: `send_message_live_postgres.rs`'s own `an_opted_out_recipient_is_refused_before_persistence` test had a `sha_of` helper that hand-rolled a second copy of the hash algorithm to precompute a matching `OptOut.msisdnHash` — exactly the kind of duplication that silently drifts the moment the real algorithm changes, which is precisely what happened here. Fixed by exposing `pepper::hmac_sha256_hex` as a public function instead of a private method, so the test now calls the *same* function production does rather than reimplementing it — removing the drift risk rather than just patching the one copy that happened to be caught this time.

One environment note worth recording rather than working around silently: this machine's globally installed `cratestack` CLI is `0.7.4`, newer than the pinned library family (`=0.6.7`), and `cratestack migrate diff` under that newer CLI emits foreign-key `ALTER TABLE` statements the pinned `0.6.7` emitter never has (confirmed: the *same* diff, run against the *unmodified* `main` schema, already disagrees with the committed `0001_init`) — a real, pre-existing skew between the installed CLI and the pinned library, not something this PR's own schema edit caused. The committed migrations were correspondingly left untouched, on the strength of the policy-only edit reasoning above, not regenerated with the mismatched CLI.

**Milestone 3 in progress.** `#38` (subscribers) and `#39` (the `drain` role) landed together, as one PR — they're two halves of one subsystem and the epic (#37) itself says so. `backends/crates/sms-api/src/webhooks.rs` is #38's `Message.created`/`Message.updated` subscribers; `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/drain.rs` is #39's role.

The load-bearing question a PR like this has to resolve before writing any code: if subscribers already insert `WebhookAttempt` rows synchronously, what does `drain` actually drain? Read straight from `cratestack-sqlx`'s vendored source, not guessed: every `@@emit`-annotated mutation triggers an *automatic* post-commit drain of its own process's runtime (`create.rs`/`update.rs`: `let _ = self.runtime.drain_event_outbox().await;`), and `CratestackEventBus::emit` returns `Ok(())` for a topic with **zero registered handlers** — not an error, not a skip flagged anywhere (`cratestack-core/src/events/bus.rs`). So a process that writes to `Message` without having called `sms_api::webhooks::register_subscribers` on its own `Cratestack` instance first doesn't leave the row for `drain` to pick up later — its own automatic drain marks it delivered immediately, having done nothing, and the event is lost, silently, the moment the write commits, which is worse than "stalled." That makes registration mandatory in *every* writer process, not optional wiring for wherever `drain` happens to be scheduled: `backends/apps/sms-gateway`'s `serve` command and `backends/apps/sms-worker`'s `main` (unconditionally, regardless of `--roles`, since every role in one process shares one `Cratestack`/`CratestackEventBus` — `Clone` shares the `Arc`-backed registry, not a copy) both call it now. What `drain`'s own periodic `.events().drain()` call actually adds on top of that is the one thing no writer's own post-commit drain gives you: a write-independent retry trigger for a row whose handler failed on an earlier attempt. Proven live, not asserted: `backends/crates/sms-worker/tests/drain_live_postgres.rs` deliberately leaves a row stuck by registering an always-failing handler on one `Cratestack` instance, then shows a second, independent instance's `drain::tick` — running the real subscribers, with no further write on any emitting model in between — is what turns it into a real `WebhookAttempt`.

Two real schema-policy findings, not invented ones. First, **the eighth instance** of this file's own seven-times-repeated `hasRole('system')` gap: `WebhookEndpoint`'s `@@allow("read", ...)` didn't admit a system context, so the subscriber's own endpoint lookup would have silently matched zero endpoints against every event, forever, with nothing anywhere to notice. Fixed the same way as every prior instance — `schema.cstack` only, no DDL consequence, not regenerated — and `backends/crates/sms-api/tests/system_context_golden_list_live_postgres.rs` moved `WebhookEndpoint` into `SYSTEM_READABLE_MODELS` to match. Second, found live while writing this PR's own tests and **deliberately not fixed here**: `WebhookAttempt`'s own `list`/`detail` policy has no `hasRole('system')` branch at all — only `create`/`update` do. Nothing #38/#39 builds ever reads `WebhookAttempt` under a system context in production, so this PR's own live tests read it back under `owner()` instead of `sys()`; #40's `hooks` claim loop will need that clause the moment it exists to claim attempts for delivery — the same shape as the eight instances before it, flagged here rather than fixed pre-emptively for a role this PR doesn't own.

A third real finding, caught by Lightbridge's review of this PR and independently confirmed before fixing, not a policy gap this time but a structural-reachability bug of exactly the same "advertised but never actually fires" shape: `message_event_type` mapped `MessageState::accepted` to `Some("message.accepted")` — correct per §8.4's own catalogue — but the first cut of `register_subscribers` wired up only `on_message_updated`. `accepted` is `Message.state @default('accepted')`, the row's state the instant it's created, and `message_state_transitions` (§2.10) lists it only as a `from_state`, never a `to_state` — nothing transitions *into* `accepted`, ever. So `message.accepted` could only ever have arisen from a `Message.created` event, which nothing subscribed to: an endpoint configured for `message.accepted` would have silently never fired, forever, exactly the "would have shipped looking correct" failure class this repo keeps finding. Fixed by registering `on_message_created` alongside `on_message_updated` in the same function, both driving the same `enqueue_message_webhook_attempts` — so nothing can register one without the other — and proven with a live test that goes through a real `db.message().create(...)`, not just the mapping function in isolation (a unit test asserting `message_event_type(accepted) == Some("message.accepted")` would have passed both before and after this fix, which is precisely the blind spot that let it through). The dedupe index (`endpoint_id`, `aggregate_id`, `event_type`) was also verified live to hold across the `created`/`updated` boundary, not just reasoned about: calling `enqueue_message_webhook_attempts` a second time against the same still-`accepted` message, simulating a retry or a race, still produces exactly one row.

Event coverage is deliberately narrow, the same scoping precedent #35's single `expire_stale` job kind set: only `Message.created`/`Message.updated` → `message.accepted/submitted/delivered/failed/expired/uncertain/cancelled` per §8.4's own catalogue (`queued`/`routed`/`undelivered`/`rejected` produce no event — not in the catalogue, matching the doc's own list exactly, not a gap). `OptOut.created`, `Provider.updated` (→ `provider.degraded`/`provider.recovered`), and `SenderIdRegistration` (which doesn't even `@@emit` today) are explicitly out of scope: `OptOut` has no `appId`, and every match in this milestone keys on `WebhookEndpoint.appId`, so which endpoints a global, cross-app opt-out should notify is a real product decision this PR doesn't make.

The stored `WebhookAttempt.payload` is the `data` object only (§8.4's `messageId`/`appId`/`clientRef`/`to`/`state`/`operator`/`segments`/`costXaf`, with `to` masked per the matched endpoint's own `maskRecipient` at insert time via `sms_msisdn::Msisdn::masked`), not the full outer envelope (`id`/`type`/`occurredAt`) — the natural `id` for that envelope is `WebhookAttempt.id` itself, which doesn't exist yet at subscriber time (it's the row the subscriber is in the middle of creating). Building the envelope, signing it, and delivering it is #40/#41's job, not this one's.

`drain`'s oldest-undelivered-age alerting (§39's own acceptance line: "alert on oldest-undelivered age, not just on errors") is a fifth named R1 exception — `cratestack_event_outbox` isn't a schema model (it's created lazily by the framework itself), so no delegate can read it; `CONTRIBUTING.md`'s exceptions table and `ci/assert-no-raw-sqlx.sh` both name `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/drain.rs`. Found live while writing its own test, not assumed: `EXTRACT(EPOCH FROM ...)` returns Postgres `NUMERIC`, not `FLOAT8` — sqlx's `Option<f64>` decode rejected it outright ("mismatched types ... NUMERIC") until an explicit `::float8` cast was added.

`backends/crates/sms-worker/src/lib.rs`'s role dispatch (`Role::Drain => { drain::run(ctx, worker).await; return; }`) is a small, mechanical addition, kept minimal on purpose — a sibling PR adding `Role::Hooks`'s own real body (#40) will conflict trivially with it. The stub test that used to exercise `Role::Drain` (`a_stub_role_never_resolves_on_its_own`) now uses `Role::Hooks` instead, the only role still a pure `std::future::pending` stub.

## Regenerating migrations — this repo's actual workflow, found the hard way

**Check your `cratestack` CLI version before running any of this — every time, not from memory.** This has drifted before: at the time of #121 (see below) the globally installed CLI was `0.7.4` against a pinned library family of `=0.6.7`, and the newer CLI's emitter produced foreign-key `ALTER TABLE` statements the pinned emitter never had — confirmed by running the diff against the *unmodified* schema and seeing it already disagree with the committed `0001_init`. **Corrected 2026-08-11 (#59): that specific mismatch no longer exists** — `cratestack --version` now reports `0.7.10`, exactly matching the root `Cargo.toml` pin (`=0.7.10`), verified by hand before regenerating anything for that PR. This entry stays as a standing instruction, not a one-time fact, because the two numbers move independently (the CLI is a global install on whatever machine runs this; the pin is a file in this repo) and nothing automated checks them against each other — the next mismatch will look exactly like the last one: a diff that "by the book" silently produces migrations the compiled library never emits. **A policy-only `@@allow` change never touches DDL and must not be regenerated at all** (that is how #121 correctly avoided this). For a real schema change, match the CLI to the pin first, every time.

There is no committed `schema.snapshot.json` baseline, so `cratestack migrate diff` has nothing to diff *against* — every invocation emits the **complete current schema**, not an incremental delta. `0001_init` already followed this shape before anyone here made it a deliberate policy: it contains all 16 (now 19) `CREATE TABLE`s in one file. That's fine and intentional at this stage — there is no live database anywhere, so there is no data-preservation reason to want a true incremental migration.

So: when the schema changes, **regenerate `0001_init` wholesale** with the exact command in §2.10, don't try to hand-write an incremental one. Then regenerate `0002_bootstrap` from the doc. Both from scratch, both committed, every time:

```bash
cratestack migrate diff --schema schemas/vsms.cstack --out-dir backends/migrations --backend postgres --name init
# copy the output over backends/migrations/postgres/0001_init/{up,up.pre,down}.sql
# — up.pre.sql only exists when the generator scaffolds one for a blocking
# migration; delete the committed copy if a regeneration stops emitting one
cargo xtask bootstrap-sql backends/migrations/postgres/0002_bootstrap/up.sql
```

The tool also writes `backends/migrations/postgres/schema.snapshot.json` as a side effect. Delete it — this repo doesn't use snapshot-based incremental diffing, and a stale one committed by accident would be misleading.

**Verify against a real database before trusting the regeneration**, not just `cratestack check` — "parses" is not "compiles" is not "applies". `docker run --rm -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=postgres -p 5432:5432 postgres:16`, apply both migrations in order, then run `ci/test-state-machine.sql`. This is how the 0.5.0 enum-type change below was actually caught — `cratestack check` and `cargo build` both stayed green through it.

## The live suites run in CI now — and that changed everything

**Until #118, `.github/workflows/ci.yml` ran a bare `cargo test --workspace` with no `--ignored`, so all 14 live-Postgres suites were silently skipped in CI and had only ever been run by hand.** That is not a hypothetical gap: this file already records #29's `claim_live_postgres.rs` sitting unrun for a whole milestone while hiding a policy bug that made its query return zero rows. A suite CI never executes is the purest form of the "green `cargo test` proves nothing" problem `#87` is the reference example for.

`backends/crates/sms-test-support` (#118, stabilised by #120) replaced the old convention — a human running `docker run postgres:16` and `ci/apply-migrations.sh`, then exporting `DATABASE_URL`. It manages **one** container by talking to the `docker` CLI directly, keyed by a fixed name and a distinctive label, and gives **each test binary its own database** inside it, created via `CREATE DATABASE ... TEMPLATE` from a once-migrated template and dropped/recreated per run. Live suites stay `#[ignore]`d; `just test-live` runs them, `just test-live-clean` removes the container.

Five things about it are load-bearing, each learned by something breaking:

- **Never cache a container handle in a `static`.** The first attempt held `testcontainers::ContainerAsync` in a `OnceCell`, reasoning that `Drop` would clean up. **Rust never runs `Drop` for a `static`'s contents on any process-exit path**, so every run leaked its container — 56 orphaned Postgres instances filled a 32 GB laptop before anyone noticed. There is no Ryuk reaper installed to catch what `Drop` misses. Nothing here may depend on a destructor running at exit.
- **Cleanup is scoped by the harness's own fixed Compose project name (`vsms-test-harness`), never by image or a bare name pattern.** (Corrected: an earlier revision of this line named a `dev.vsms.test-harness=true` label from a pre-Compose `docker run` design; the harness moved onto `docker compose -p vsms-test-harness -f compose.yml` and Compose's own project-scoped labelling instead — see `backends/crates/sms-test-support/src/lib.rs`'s own module doc.) A sweep filtered by `ancestor=postgres:16-alpine` would cheerfully destroy a developer's unrelated running database. Never `docker prune` in any form.
- **One database per test *binary*, not one shared database.** All 14 sharing one database let `kill9_reclaim_live`'s real `sms-worker --roles dispatch` subprocess claim *other suites'* leftover messages — the claim loop deliberately selects any eligible row, as it must in production. Passed alone, timed out under the full sweep.
- **The migration check fingerprints migration *content*** (stamped via `COMMENT ON DATABASE`), not merely whether `public.messages` exists. The existence check silently served a **stale schema** from a pre-existing container after a bootstrap-only change, so tests reported `ok` against migrations that had never been applied.
- **The container name is fixed and global**, so two concurrent `cargo test` invocations on one machine fight over it and corrupt each other's runs. This is real, not theoretical — it cost one investigation a stress run. Don't run overlapping live-test processes; scoping that name is tracked follow-up work.

`ca653a1` (#102)'s per-binary `tokio::sync::Mutex` remains load-bearing on top of all this: tests *within* one binary still share that binary's database, and Postgres's own `pg_type` catalog race on first use is what it exists to serialize. **Two suites never picked that pattern up** — `rbac_layer2_live_postgres.rs` (added by #112 ten hours *after* `ca653a1` landed) and `sms-worker/tests/live_postgres.rs` (predating it, shielded only by being unrun) — and both flaked in CI within hours of #118 turning the lights on. Any new live suite must take the mutex.

**A live suite can also fail for a reason that has nothing to do with Postgres — and #192's own M3 gate did, immediately, on `main`.** `backends/apps/sms-worker/tests/hooks_node_receiver_live.rs` spawns `node src/gate-receiver.ts` as a real subprocess out of `examples/node/webhook-receiver`. The `live` job installs `psql` and nothing else, so there was no Node toolchain and no `node_modules` on that runner: every run since #192 merged died at spawn with the test's own `"check that `pnpm install` has been run"` panic, and `main` was red on exactly that until it was fixed. It passed locally because a human had run `pnpm install` in that directory by hand — the same "green on the one machine that has the undeclared prerequisite" shape as `#87`, one layer up from Rust.

Two things about that example directory are load-bearing and were the actual root cause:

- **`pnpm-workspace.yaml` covers `admin` and `frontends/packages/*` — `examples/*` is deliberately outside it**, because the example is meant to be copied out and run standalone by an integrator. So a root `pnpm install` never installs it, and running `pnpm install` *inside* it without `--ignore-workspace` resolves against the root workspace and writes **no lockfile at all** (it succeeds, which is what makes it misleading). It now carries its own committed `pnpm-lock.yaml` and CI installs with `--ignore-workspace --frozen-lockfile`, so a floating `express@^5.1.0` can't change under CI — the unpinned-dependency-in-a-pipeline shape this file's release-engineering notes warn about elsewhere.
- **The same gap silently un-gated the cross-language signature proof.** `pnpm turbo run test` reaches workspace packages only, so `examples/node/webhook-receiver/src/cross-language-vectors.test.ts` had never run in CI either — while this file claimed the HMAC-SHA256 scheme was proven on both sides against `openssl`-computed fixtures, naming `pnpm test` as the TypeScript half's gate. The Rust half genuinely was gated; the TypeScript half was hand-run once. A cross-language agreement proof that only one side checks in CI is precisely the half that drifts. The `js` job now runs it.

## The demo runs, and the deployment exists — both landed 2026-08-08

**`just demo` brings up the whole system and a message reaches `delivered`.** Proven end to end twice, once from a cold reset, including a send from the admin console's composer showing up on `/messages` — cross-checked three ways (the UI, the Postgres row, and `sms-fake-orange`'s own log for that exact message id). Three things had to land first, and each was a real hole rather than a packaging exercise:

- **`#134`** — `msisdnHash`/`bodyHash` were plain unkeyed `SHA-256`, reversible in seconds over Cameroon's ~10^7 numbering space, while `docs/architecture.md` had always claimed HMAC-under-a-pepper. Now `hmac-sha256-v1:<hex>` keyed by `SMS_HASH_PEPPER` (`backends/crates/sms-api/src/pepper.rs`). `Procedures::default()` is **deleted** so no construction site can silently get an unpeppered one, and the pepper is validated before the pool connects. Rotation does **not** rehash stored rows; see that module's doc.
- **`#137`** — nothing could produce an HTTP-usable credential. `provisionAppClient` needs `owner`/`admin`; `GatewayAuth` only ever mints `role: "app"`. `sms-gateway provision-client` closes it, writing `privateKeyPem` once with `O_EXCL` + `0600`. Its live test spawns a genuinely separate `serve` process and completes a real `private_key_jwt` exchange — the thing `send_test_message.rs` never proved, since the `AppClient` it writes has no `OauthClient.jwks`.
- **`#138`** — `backends/crates/sms-fake-orange` had no binary target, so nothing could demo past `routed`. `backends/apps/sms-fake-orange` runs it as a process on a fixed port. Note `FaultPolicy::Scripted` *silently degrades to a bare accept with no DLR* when its queue empties — fine for a test that scripts exactly as many entries as it expects, wrong for a long-lived server, which is why `FaultPolicy::Always` exists.

**`#139` built the deployment**: three non-root glibc Dockerfiles (**not** musl — `AGENTS.md`'s own note says `rustls-no-provider` panics at `reqwest::Client::new()` without a `CryptoProvider` installed in `main`, and that path is unverified), a `deploy/` compose stack with a Caddy edge, an advisory-lock-guarded one-shot migrate job that **applies but never regenerates** migrations, `GET /healthz` on the gateway, and a GHCR release workflow on `v*.*.*`. Unverified and worth not assuming: real ACME issuance (tested with `tls internal` only), the GHCR publish path (no tag pushed at the time), and concurrent `migrate` racing under load. **Stale as of 2026-08-10** — see "rustls, musl, and distroless" below: the unverified path got verified, `sms-gateway`/`sms-worker` are musl-on-distroless now, and the migrate job is a small Rust binary (`backends/apps/sms-migrate`), not the `postgres:16-alpine` + `psql` image this paragraph describes.

**Two corrections to this file, both found by running the system rather than reading it.** First: the M1 paragraph above used to say the gateway "fails loudly on the first `/token` request if no active key exists, not at process start". **That was wrong** — `serve` calls `load_signing_keys(...)?` before binding the listener, so it exits at startup. It matters for orchestration: anything that waits for the gateway to be healthy before rotating a key deadlocks permanently, which is why the deploy runbook uses `docker compose run --rm` rather than `exec`. Second: `rust-version` claimed `1.85` while the committed `Cargo.lock` needs `1.88`; no CI job ever checked the claim, so it drifted silently.

One more of the same shape, caught wiring a container `HEALTHCHECK`: `frontends/apps/admin/app/api/health` was behind Basic Auth, so under `DASHBOARD_AUTH=basic` — the only mode `NODE_ENV=production` accepts — an unauthenticated liveness probe got a permanent `401` and the container sat `unhealthy` while the process was fine.

**`#163` and `#168` both closed the same shape of gap — key the limiter where the identity is actually known — and landed together because the fixes interact.** `#163`: `backends/crates/sms-api/src/router.rs`'s `RateLimitLayer` on the authenticated API surface keyed on `client_id_fingerprint`, an unverified bearer-token `sub` — a caller willing to forge a fresh `sub` per request got a fresh, full bucket every time, bypassing the per-principal limit entirely (the idempotency layer had the same shape of bug and was fixed first, in #153/#161, by requiring a *verified* `sub`; that fix doesn't transfer here, since `RateLimitLayer` only ever consumes from a bucket, never writes a value another request reads back, so the cheaper unverified read stays correct — the aggregate-throughput consequence is the only thing that needed closing). Fixed with a *second*, coarser `RateLimitLayer` keyed on `ConnectInfo<SocketAddr>` (`source_fingerprint`) — the real TCP peer, unspoofable, populated because `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/main.rs` now serves through `into_make_service_with_connect_info`. Deliberately not `X-Forwarded-For`: found while designing this fix, not assumed, that `deploy/docker-compose.yml` has **two** internal callers of this router, not one — Caddy, and `admin` (`SMS_API_URL: http://sms-gateway:8080`, bypassing Caddy) — so a blanket trust config can't tell them apart, and Docker Compose assigns container IPs dynamically with no static-allowlist infrastructure to pin a single trusted hop against. `client_id_fingerprint` itself is untouched; the new layer only bounds the aggregate a flood of forged identities can reach.

`#168`: #156's Caddy-edge `/token` rate limiting keys on source IP only — `client_id` arrives solely in the form-urlencoded POST body, and every way the edge could read one field out of it was checked and rejected (documented with receipts in `deploy/Caddyfile`'s own comment) — so §4.2's "client_id *and* source IP" composite was never actually implemented, only the IP half. Fixed with a second, defense-in-depth limiter, `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/token_rate_limit.rs`, mounted on `/token` itself in `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/op.rs` (not `sms-auth`, not a first-party Caddy module — both real options, weighed and set aside for more work with the same outcome), keyed on the real `client_id` read from the request body it already buffers. This is a narrow, explicit reversal of §4's own "an infrastructure concern, not `sms-auth`'s" framing from #22 — justified because the edge #156 built to satisfy that framing turned out structurally unable to reach the composite key §4.2 itself already specified; the edge keeps doing flood protection, this layer adds only the dimension it cannot reach. Buffers and reconstructs the body via `axum::body::to_bytes` + `Request::from_parts` rather than consuming it — proven directly with a round-trip test, not assumed, since a `/token` handler receiving a drained body breaks every real exchange, not just an attacker's.

Both proven against the actual attack, not just a happy path: a flood of requests with a different forged `sub` each time is throttled collectively once the coarse bucket's burst is spent (`backends/crates/sms-api/src/router.rs`'s own live-server test asserts `404, 404, 404, 429, 429` against a 3-token bucket), while a lone honest client at default production budgets never sees a `429`; a burst of `/token` requests sharing one `client_id` is throttled while a different `client_id` sent alongside is unaffected (`backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/token_rate_limit.rs`'s own tests). `docs/architecture.md` §4.2/§4.5/§4.6 and `deploy/Caddyfile`'s own `#168` comment are updated to match — this file has now recorded three separate instances of doc-vs-code drift in one day (the M1 "fails on first /token request" claim, `rust-version`, and the `/token`-rate-limiting-mechanism table row that named a crate `sms-auth` never linked), which is why this PR treated updating both as part of the fix, not a follow-up.

## cratestack bumped to `=0.7.10` (from `=0.7.8`)

`v0.7.8...v0.7.10` (compare: [cratestack/cratestack](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/compare/v0.7.8...v0.7.10)) landed several changes that reach this repo directly:

- **MSRV is now declared upstream as `1.95.0`** ([cratestack#480](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/pull/480)). This repo's own `rust-version` is raised to match — the whole `cratestack` family is pinned exactly, so cratestack's MSRV is a floor on ours by construction, not a courtesy bump. Verified by hand (`cargo +1.95.0 check --workspace --all-targets`, clean); `.github/workflows/ci.yml` still uses `dtolnay/rust-toolchain@stable` (always latest, not pinned), so nothing in CI enforces this floor yet the way cratestack's own upstream `msrv` job does — the declaration is correct, not yet gated. The Dockerfiles already pinned `rust:1.95-bookworm` (from #139), so they needed no change; only `Cargo.toml`'s declared floor was stale.
- **DDL emission is unchanged.** `cratestack migrate diff` was run against `schemas/vsms.cstack` with an isolated `cratestack-cli =0.7.10` install (never the machine's global CLI) and diffed byte-for-byte against the committed `0001_init/{up,down}.sql` — identical. Unlike the `=0.7.8` bump (which added twelve emitted foreign keys), 0.7.10 changes nothing about what this schema compiles to. Migrations were left untouched, per this file's own standing rule that a change with no DDL consequence isn't regenerated.
- **The generated TypeScript client's `Decimal` fields are now real `decimal.js` objects, not strings** ([cratestack#498](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/issues/498)/[#499](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/pull/499)) — upstream's own CHANGELOG marks it breaking. Checked and found to have **no live consumer inside vsms**: `frontends/packages/sms-client` (T3) is generated but not yet imported by `admin`/`frontends/packages/gateway` at runtime — `frontends/packages/gateway/src/client.ts`'s own module doc explains why (a deliberate, temporary hand-rolled seam, keeping the three `Decimal` money fields as plain strings). `frontends/packages/sms-client`'s regenerated `package.json` now carries a `decimal.js` dependency; `pnpm-lock.yaml` was updated to match, and `frontends/packages/sms-client/GENERATING.md` now documents the break for whenever T3's actual migration lands. `admin`'s own `typecheck`/`build` are unaffected either way — confirmed, not assumed.
- **Two workarounds this repo carried in `sdks/rust/vsms-sdk-rust` are gone**, both closed upstream in this release:
  - [cratestack#489](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/issues/489)/[#491](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/pull/491) — server-side response content-type negotiation is now codec-aware, so a `JsonCodec`-only router like `sms-gateway`'s no longer 406s on the generated client's default multi-codec `Accept` header. `src/client.rs`'s `JSON_ONLY_ACCEPT` const (and the equivalent inline override in `send_message`) is deleted; both call sites verified live against a real gateway (`tests/live_gateway.rs`'s three `--ignored` tests, run against `just demo`).
  - [cratestack#490](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/issues/490)/[#492](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/pull/492) — a fourth, client-only facade, `cratestack-client`, now exists specifically for pure HTTP-client SDKs, with `cratestack-axum` (and therefore `axum`/`tower`/`hyper`) structurally absent from its dependency graph. `vsms-sdk-rust` moved onto it from `cratestack-pg`. Confirmed via `cargo tree`: `axum`/`cratestack-axum` are completely gone from this crate's own dependency tree (277 → 260 resolved packages), not merely feature-gated off.
- **`@no_rate_limit` now actually holds for REST, not just RPC** ([cratestack#481](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/pull/481)) — checked and found inert here: `schemas/vsms.cstack` never uses `@no_rate_limit` anywhere, so `backends/crates/sms-api/src/router.rs`'s `RateLimitLayer` (mounted as blanket Tower middleware over the whole router, not scoped per-op) rate-limits exactly the same routes exactly the same way before and after.
- **A real `decimal-bigdecimal` backend now exists** ([cratestack#496](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/pull/496)), but this repo stays on the default `decimal-rust-decimal` backend — `Provider.costPerSegmentXaf` and friends are still plain `rust_decimal::Decimal` on the Rust side, unaffected.
- **Three changes are deliberately not adopted here yet** — each is a real decision or a real audit, not a mechanical follow-on, so each got its own tracked issue instead of a drive-by schema edit: `auth().isSystem()` ([cratestack#500](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/pull/500), a first-class system-principal predicate that could replace the `hasRole('system')` convention behind this file's own seven-times-repeated gap — [#176](https://github.com/vymalo/vsms/issues/176)) and per-call-site `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` upserts ([cratestack#501](https://github.com/cratestack/cratestack/pull/501), which may soften the "upsert doesn't exist when the `@id` has a default" constraint below for `ClientAssertion`/seed-provider/scheduler dedupe — [#177](https://github.com/vymalo/vsms/issues/177)).

## rustls, musl, and distroless — landed 2026-08-10

The gap this file's own "aws-lc-rs enters this tree from authkestra alone" section flagged (`rustls-no-provider` chosen but not adopted, "do that work deliberately, with a live TLS test, not as a drive-by") is closed — see that section's own follow-up paragraphs for the mechanism and the corroborating upstream evidence found while landing it. Three things changed together, deliberately, not as separate PRs — the Dockerfile rewrite is only safe once the TLS ordering is proven, and the health-check rewrite is only necessary because of the base-image swap the TLS fix unblocks:

- **`backends/apps/sms-gateway/Dockerfile` and `backends/apps/sms-worker/Dockerfile` are now a two-stage build: `rust:1.95-alpine3.22` (musl-native — Alpine's own libc *is* musl, so a plain `cargo build --release` on that image produces a static musl binary with no cross-compilation, no `rustup target add`, no cross-linker) into `gcr.io/distroless/static-debian12:nonroot`.** `cmake`/`pkg-config` are gone from both builder stages — those existed solely for `aws-lc-sys`, which no longer compiles into this graph at all. Confirmed by inspecting the distroless image's own layers directly (`docker save` + `tar -tzv`, not assumed from documentation): it ships a current `/etc/ssl/certs/ca-certificates.crt`, `/etc/passwd`/`/etc/group`/`/etc/nsswitch.conf` (so `:nonroot`'s uid 65532 needs no `useradd`/`USER` line — there's no `useradd` binary in the image to run one with), and a `1777` `/tmp` (`sms-worker`'s heartbeat file depends on this and was verified to still work). Image size: `sms-gateway` ~44.8MB, `sms-worker` ~36.8MB (compressed layers) — down from a `debian:bookworm-slim`-based image several times that size.
- **Neither runtime image has a shell, `curl`, or coreutils any more, so every shell-based health check needed a replacement.** Both binaries gained a `healthcheck` exec-form entry point instead: `sms-gateway healthcheck [--addr] [--path]` is a real clap subcommand (plain HTTP/1.1 GET over a raw `std::net::TcpStream`, no `reqwest` needed for a loopback plaintext request) reproducing the old `curl -fsS http://127.0.0.1:8080/healthz`; `sms-worker healthcheck` is intercepted ahead of `Cli::parse()` (see that interception's own comment for why it isn't a real clap subcommand — `Cli` is flat, and every documented invocation of this binary is `sms-worker --roles ...` with no subcommand at all) and reproduces the old `sh -c 'test -f ... && [ ... -lt 90 ]'` mtime-staleness check in `std`-only Rust. Every place that used to shell out to `curl`/`sh` now execs the binary directly, JSON-array form: the Dockerfile `HEALTHCHECK`s, `deploy/docker-compose.yml`'s `sms-gateway` readiness override, and `deploy/charts/vsms/values.yaml`'s `sms-worker` liveness/readiness exec probes. `sms-gateway`'s own Kubernetes `httpGet` probes needed no change — `kubelet` does that GET itself, no in-container shell involved either way.
- **`backends/apps/sms-migrate` is a new workspace member, replacing `deploy/migrate.Dockerfile`'s `postgres:16-alpine` + `psql` shell script entirely (hard cutover — both deleted in the same change).** Prompted by the observation that `cratestack-sqlx` already vendors `sqlx-core`/`sqlx-postgres` under `cratestack::sqlx` (confirmed: `pub use sqlx_core::raw_sql::{RawSql, raw_sql};` is already re-exported, unused until now), so a small Rust binary can embed the committed migration `.sql` via `include_str!` at compile time and apply it with `cratestack::sqlx::raw_sql(...).execute(...)`, rather than `COPY`ing `.sql` files into a `postgres:16-alpine` image and shelling out to `psql \i` at runtime. This is **not** an adoption of `sqlx`'s own `migrate!` macro/`_sqlx_migrations` framework — that needs a `<version>_<description>.sql` flat-file layout this repo's `cratestack migrate diff`-generated directory-per-migration layout doesn't match, and mixing two bookkeeping tables would be worse than one. Instead `backends/apps/sms-migrate/src/main.rs` ports `deploy/migrate.sql`'s own `schema_migrations`-tracked, advisory-lock-guarded logic statement-for-statement into Rust — same lock key (`hashtext('vsms_migrate')`), same skip-if-already-applied behaviour, verified live (fresh database applies every migration and prints it; a second run against the same database skips all of them; `ci/test-state-machine.sql`'s full assertion suite passes against the result) before being trusted. One behavioural improvement, not just a port: `raw_sql` wraps a multi-statement script in an implicit transaction, which `psql \i` never did unless the script opened its own `BEGIN` — checked that none of the committed migrations use anything transaction-incompatible (`CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY`, specifically) before relying on this. Uses a dedicated, unpooled `PgConnection`, not a `Pool` — same reasoning `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/lease.rs` already documents for advisory locks (a pooled connection can silently outlive the code that took a session-scoped lock on it). This is R1's existing "migrations" exception, not a new one — `ci/assert-no-raw-sqlx.sh` and `CONTRIBUTING.md`'s own exceptions table were both updated to name the new file rather than adding a fifth entry.
- **Follow-up, same day: `backends/apps/sms-migrate` discovers its migrations from disk rather than hand-listing three names.** `sqlx`'s own directory-scanning `Migrator`/`MigrationSource` (`sqlx-core`, already reachable through `cratestack::sqlx::migrate`, no new dependency) was checked and rejected for this — its resolver (`sqlx-core-0.8.6/src/migrate/source.rs`, read directly, not assumed) only reads flat files immediately inside one directory, named `<VERSION>_<DESCRIPTION>.sql`, and silently skips anything that isn't a file, so it structurally cannot see this repo's `<name>/up.sql`-per-directory layout. `backends/apps/sms-migrate/build.rs` reads that layout itself instead — every subdirectory of `backends/migrations/postgres` with an `up.sql`, sorted lexically — and generates the `include_str!` array `main.rs` used to hand-maintain, so a fourth migration needs no code change, only a new directory. The same day, `ci/idempotency-table.sql` (previously a fourth, hand-maintained, separately-applied entry in three different places) relocated to `backends/migrations/postgres/0003_idempotency_table/up.sql` — the exact same directory shape as `0001_init`/`0002_bootstrap` — which let `ci/apply-migrations.sh`'s own glob loop and `backends/crates/sms-test-support`'s own `migration_dirs()` (both of which already walked this directory) drop their special-cased extra step for it entirely, not just `backends/apps/sms-migrate`. The `schema_migrations` bookkeeping name for that table changed from `cratestack_idempotency_table` to `0003_idempotency_table` as a result — safe with no live database anywhere yet (this file's own standing fact), and harmless even against one that already ran the old name, since the DDL itself is `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` either way.
- The `backends/apps/sms-gateway` `Cargo.toml` also gained `reqwest-no-provider` (a second, differently-featured resolution of the identical `reqwest 0.13.4` already in the graph) as a dev-dependency purely for `tests/tls_no_provider_live.rs` — see this file's own TLS section above.
- **Correction, same section, 2026-08-10, found by a red `main`:** "the TLS ordering is proven" above was true for the two production binaries and no further than that. `install_default_crypto_provider` is the first line of each binary's own `main`, and a test binary's entry point is the libtest harness, not that `main` — so it never runs. `backends/crates/sms-auth/tests/oidc_flow_live.rs` and `backends/crates/sms-auth/tests/rbac_layer2_live_postgres.rs` both construct `GatewayAuth` in-process (real RS256/JWKS validation against a real issuer, via `authkestra_resource::jwt`'s `JwksCache`, which builds its own `reqwest` 0.13.4 client internally) and both panicked with the exact "No rustls crypto provider is configured" message the binaries were fixed against, PR #180 having dropped `aws-lc-rs` without adding the equivalent install anywhere a test binary would hit it. `tests/tls_no_provider_live.rs` itself was never blind to this — it installs the provider in its own test body — but nothing generalised that to every other live suite. Fixed by `backends/crates/sms-test-support`'s own `install_default_crypto_provider`, called unconditionally at the top of `database_url()` — every live suite already calls `database_url()` to get a migrated Postgres, so this is the one change that reaches all 19 of this workspace's live test binaries (present and future) rather than one more line to remember per test file. Checked, not assumed, that no other live suite needed it: `cargo test --workspace --no-fail-fast -- --ignored` (workspace-wide `cargo test` is fail-fast by default, which is exactly how this stayed hidden behind whichever suite CI happened to reach first) showed precisely these two binaries panicking and nothing else — the subprocess-spawning suites (`m1_acceptance_gate_live_postgres.rs`, `provision_client_cli_live_postgres.rs`, `kill9_reclaim_live.rs`) exercise `GatewayAuth` only inside a real child process whose own `main` already installs the provider, and `provision_app_client_live_postgres.rs`'s in-process OP router never constructs a `GatewayAuth` at all.

## `cargo deny` wired into CI, and the `rsa`/Marvin-Attack reachability call — landed 2026-08-10

`.github/workflows/ci.yml` never ran `cargo deny check` (#174) — the `deny.toml` and `just deny` existed, but nothing in CI called either, so `cargo deny check advisories` had been failing on `main` on RUSTSEC-2023-0071 (Marvin Attack: timing sidechannel key recovery in `rsa`) for however long it was present, silently. Same shape as the pre-#118 problem of live suites nobody ran. A new `deny` job now runs `EmbarkStudios/cargo-deny-action@v2` (all four categories: advisories, bans, licenses, sources) on every push and PR.

**There is no fixed version of `rsa` for RUSTSEC-2023-0071 — it cannot be resolved by bumping.** The advisory reads as a blanket "avoid using the `rsa` crate in settings where attackers can observe timing," which is not the same question as "is *this deployment's* actual use of it exploitable." `rsa` enters the graph twice, both under `authkestra-engine 0.3.3` (the OP that signs every access token and verifies `private_key_jwt` client assertions — not a leaf dependency): directly, and transitively through `jsonwebtoken 11.0.0`'s `rust_crypto` RS256 backend. Read both call sites rather than trusting the advisory headline (`cargo tree` cannot answer this — it only shows edges, not what the code does with them):
