- `jsonwebtoken-11.0.0/src/crypto/rust_crypto/rsa.rs` calls only `Pkcs1v15Sign`/`Pss` `Signer::sign_with_rng` (a private-key **sign**) and `Verifier::verify` (a **public-key-only** verify). No `Decryptor`, no `decrypt`/`decrypt_blinded` call anywhere in the file.
- `authkestra-engine-0.3.3/src/token/mod.rs`'s `TokenManager::new_asymmetric` uses `rsa::` only to parse a PEM-encoded RSA private key and read `n`/`e` off it to publish the public JWK. Also no decrypt call.

The Marvin Attack (RUSTSEC-2023-0071's own CVSS vector: `C:H/I:N/A:N`, confidentiality-only) is specifically a **decryption**-oracle timing attack: an attacker submits chosen ciphertext and a network-observable timing difference reveals whether PKCS#1 v1.5 padding validated, which is what lets the private key be recovered. RSA **signing** exponentiates the private key over data this service itself chooses (never attacker-supplied ciphertext), and verification touches only the public key — neither operation hands a network attacker the decryption oracle the attack needs. Grepping both dependency sources for any decrypt-shaped call (`rsa::.*[Dd]ecrypt`, `Oaep.*decrypt`, `Pkcs1v15Encrypt`) returns nothing. Conclusion: **accepted, not silenced** — `deny.toml`'s `[advisories] ignore` entry for RUSTSEC-2023-0071 carries this same analysis inline, is scoped to that one advisory id (so any *other* new advisory still fails the job), and is time-boxed to be re-checked by 2026-11-10 or sooner if `authkestra` bumps past `0.3.3` or `rsa` ships a patched release — whichever comes first.

## The mermaid CI job stopped needing a browser — landed 2026-08-10

The `mermaid diagrams parse` job (#179) used to `npm install -g @mermaid-js/mermaid-cli` and render every diagram to SVG through `mmdc`, which launches Chrome via Puppeteer — downloaded fresh from an upstream CDN on every run, at a version this repo never pinned. It failed on #178, a PR that touched no diagrams at all, purely because that download failed; it had already been patched once before for a Puppeteer sandbox failure (the `--no-sandbox` flag it used to pass). Two failures of the same browser-download shape is the unpinned-moving-dependency-in-a-pipeline pattern this file's release-engineering notes warn about elsewhere — worth a durable fix, not a third patch.

The job's actual acceptance bar is "diagrams parse," not "diagrams render to SVG" — rendering is what needed a browser, parsing never did. `mermaid.parse()` runs each diagram's own grammar (jison, for the `flowchart`/`sequenceDiagram`/`stateDiagram-v2` types this doc actually uses — `@mermaid-js/parser`, mermaid's newer langium-based standalone parser package, was checked and rejected: as of `1.2.0` it only covers newer diagram types — `info`, `packet`, `pie`, `architecture`, `gitGraph`, `radar`, etc. — not these three) without ever touching layout, canvas measurement, or a real display. The only browser-shaped thing it touches is `document`, to construct a handful of DOM/SVG elements — confirmed empirically, not assumed: `jsdom` stands in for that with no Puppeteer, no Chrome, and no CDN download anywhere in the tree, and `mermaid.parse()` still correctly threw on a deliberately corrupted diagram in local testing. `ci/mermaid-parse/` is a standalone npm project (`package.json` + committed `package-lock.json`, exact `mermaid`/`jsdom` pins), deliberately outside the `pnpm-workspace.yaml` scope — it's a CI tool, not application code, and installed with `npm ci` rather than pnpm to keep it independent of the JS monorepo's own dependency graph. `ci/mermaid-parse/parse.mjs` extracts and parses every `` ```mermaid `` block from `docs/architecture.md` directly; the CI job's Python extraction step is gone, folded into that one script.

## Milestone 3 started: webhook signing and secret rotation (#41) — landed 2026-08-10

M3 (events, outbox drain, webhook delivery — epic #37) opens with `#41`, landed independently of `#38`–`#40`/`#42` (subscribers, `drain`, `hooks`, `reap_outbox` — all still open). Two things, deliberately factored apart:

- **`backends/crates/sms-webhook`** — a new, pure library crate (no `include_server_schema!`, no schema types in its public API) implementing §4.4's canonical webhook signature: `HMAC-SHA256("v1\n{timestamp}\n{eventId}\n{sha256_hex(body)}")`, hex, `v1=<hex>` header form, and rotation (`sign_header`/`verify` try every candidate secret against every presented `v1=` value, first match wins — the `hooks` role, #40, is the intended caller of `sign_header`; nothing about it depends on that role existing yet). `verify` never compares raw bytes or hex strings — every candidate goes through `hmac::Mac::verify_slice`, which does the constant-time comparison internally (`subtle::ConstantTimeEq`). `generate_secret()` (`whsec_<64 hex chars>`, 32 bytes of `OsRng`) is the crate's one non-pure function, used by `rotateWebhookSecret` below. Verified against a real, independent computation, not just self-consistency: `sign_v1`'s own unit test asserts a fixed vector against `openssl dgst -sha256 -hmac`'s output, computed by hand before the Rust code ran.
- **`rotateWebhookSecret`** (`backends/crates/sms-api/src/procedures.rs`) replaces its `not_yet("milestone 3 (webhooks)")` stub: moves `WebhookEndpoint.secret` → `prevSecret`, mints a fresh `sms_webhook::generate_secret()`, stamps `secretRotatedAt`, inside the procedure's own `@isolation("serializable")` transaction (no `@version` on this model at the time, so serializability — not `if_match` — is what stopped two concurrent rotations of the same endpoint from clobbering each other's `prevSecret`; **#59 gave `WebhookEndpoint` `@version` and this write now carries `if_match` too, additively — see that section below**). Reads/writes as `sys`, the same convention every other procedure in this file uses, which meant `WebhookEndpoint`'s `read`/`update` `@@allow` needed `hasRole('system')` added — the pattern this file's own "Invariants" section below now records an eighth time, but caught by #155's own golden test *before* merge rather than found live afterward; see that section for the distinction.

**`WebhookEndpoint.secret` readability, checked as the issue asked:** the model's `read` policy was (and, with `hasRole('system')` now added for the procedure above, still is) `auth().kind == "user"` — *any* authenticated human role, not scoped to owner/frontends/apps/admin/developer the way `create`/`update` are. `@pii`/`@sensitive` were never on this field and wouldn't have helped regardless (AGENTS.md's own §2.0: redacts audit snapshots only, no serde attribute, still returned by the API). This is a real, pre-existing finding, not introduced by #41 — flagged in the PR for reviewer judgment rather than silently narrowed, since §4.4's own design doc paragraph (`docs/architecture.md:781`) explicitly chose "readable by humans, not scoped to `appId`" over an app-scoped policy, and this PR didn't want to silently reinterpret that human/role boundary as part of a signing-and-rotation change.

**Cross-language proof, not just a doc-comment claim.** `backends/crates/sms-webhook/tests/fixtures/cross_language_vectors.json` — signatures computed by a third tool (`openssl dgst -sha256 -hmac`, receipts in the file's own `$comment`), not by this crate's code or by the Node example's — is asserted against by both `backends/crates/sms-webhook/tests/cross_language_fixtures.rs` (plain `cargo test`, no `--ignored`) and `examples/node/webhook-receiver/src/cross-language-vectors.test.ts` (`pnpm test`, Node's built-in test runner). `examples/node/webhook-receiver` predates #41 (`#150`) and was written against §4.4's prose with one flagged, honest guess — the MAC algorithm; its own README called this out explicitly as "the one genuine guess." That guess (HMAC-SHA256) is now confirmed, not just resolved by fiat: the same fixture the Rust crate is checked against is what the Node receiver's `verifySignature` is checked against too, run for real (`pnpm test`, both suites pass), not merely inspected for consistency.

Explicitly out of scope, matching #41's own issue text and this repo's M2-era convention of naming a scope cut rather than leaving it implicit: the `cleanup_secrets` job (§7.5) that clears `prevSecret` after 24 hours — one of the eight not-yet-built job kinds `#35`'s own status paragraph already lists — and everything `#38`–`#40`/`#42` own (outbox subscribers, the `drain` role, the `hooks` role that would actually attach these headers to an outbound HTTP request, and poison-row handling). `rotateWebhookSecret` and `backends/crates/sms-webhook` are the signing/rotation half of the epic's own gate ("signature verifies in a sample Node receiver"); the other two clauses (no event loss on a mid-drain kill, exactly-once attempts across two workers) are `#44`'s, not this PR's.

## `#42` — `reap_outbox` and poison-row handling — landed 2026-08-11

§7.5's own table already answers the question a story like this has to resolve before writing any code — reap or quarantine a poison row: "Delete delivered `cratestack_event_outbox` rows >24h; alarm on high-`attempts` rows." That is a hard split, not a suggestion. `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/jobs/reap_outbox.rs` implements exactly that, nothing more:

- **Delivered rows** (`delivered_at IS NOT NULL`) past a 24h retention are deleted. They already did their job — nothing in this codebase ever reads a delivered outbox row again — so keeping them is pure bloat.
- **Poison rows** (`delivered_at IS NULL`, `attempts` past #42's own threshold of `5`) are never deleted. A row stuck retrying forever is live evidence of a subscriber bug (§8.2: "short-circuits on the first failing handler," retried from the top on every drain); deleting it would erase the only record the bug happened *and* silently drop the event it was trying to redeliver — a customer-visible loss with no trace, strictly worse than an oversized table. Instead this job logs a `warn!` per row, every run, carrying `model`/`operation`/`last_error` so an operator can actually diagnose it. "Quarantine" (moving the row to a second table) was considered and rejected: there's no schema model backing this table in the first place, so a quarantine table would just be a second hand-rolled, policy-less table for no benefit over leaving the row in place and alerting loudly.

**Checked directly against the vendored source before writing any of this, not inherited from this file's own prose** (this file's own §8.2 section already says "no retry cap, no backoff, no dead-letter," but repeating a claim isn't the same as verifying it): `cratestack-sqlx-0.7.10/src/descriptor.rs`'s `drain_event_outbox` only ever `UPDATE`s `attempts = attempts + 1, last_error = $2` on a failed delivery and leaves `delivered_at` `NULL` — no comparison against any cap anywhere in that function, and no code path anywhere in `cratestack-sqlx` deletes a row of this table at all. `reap_outbox` is the entire mechanism; nothing upstream partially covers it.

**R1 exception, the sixth one** (`backends/crates/sms-worker/src/jobs/reap_outbox.rs`, added to `ci/assert-no-raw-sqlx.sh` and `CONTRIBUTING.md`'s own table) — same reasoning as `drain.rs`'s fifth: `cratestack_event_outbox` has no schema model and therefore no delegate. Unlike `drain.rs`, this job can't lean on `db.events().drain()` having already run `ensure_event_outbox_table` immediately beforehand (it never calls `.drain()` itself, and nothing guarantees it runs after some other write already has), so both queries treat Postgres's `42P01 undefined_table` as "nothing to reap yet" rather than duplicating the framework's own table DDL a second time in application code.

Wired into the existing #35 infrastructure as a sibling of `expire_stale`, not a parallel mechanism: registered in `jobs::default_registry()`, scheduled hourly (§7.5's own cadence) in `scheduler::schedule()`.

**Live-tested against a real Postgres, and the tests were verified to actually fail, not just pass by construction** — the same discipline `#155`'s golden test and `ExpireStale`'s own tests already established in this file. `backends/crates/sms-worker/tests/reap_outbox_live_postgres.rs` has no delegate to assert outbox state through either, so it proves "still there" and "gone" indirectly: a poison row's survival is proven by re-triggering `db.events().drain()` after `ReapOutbox::run_at` and observing the same always-failing handler fire again for the same message (a deleted row cannot be redelivered to anything); a delivered row's fate is read directly off `reap_delivered`'s own return value, `pub` for the same reason `drain::oldest_undelivered_age` is. Both guards were confirmed to catch a real regression by temporarily loosening each query's own `WHERE` clause (dropping the cutoff comparison, and admitting `delivered_at IS NULL` rows) and watching the corresponding test fail with the expected assertion, then restoring the correct query and watching it pass again — not assumed to work from reading the SQL.

## Milestone 3: the `hooks` role landed (#40) — outbound HTTP delivery, retries, and circuit breaking

`backends/crates/sms-worker/src/hooks.rs` is `Role::Hooks`'s real body — the fifth role with an actual loop, and the piece that finally makes a `WebhookAttempt` row turn into an outbound HTTP request. `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/claim.rs` gained `impl Claimable for WebhookAttempt`, reusing #29's CAS claim loop rather than a bespoke one, per that trait's own stated intent.

**The missing transition table, resolved in favour of R2 discipline, not an exception.** `AttemptState` shipped with #38/#39 but no `attempt_state_transitions` table or trigger existed — nothing had written `WebhookAttempt.state` yet, so R2 had nothing to decide against, and #38/#39's own PR left the question open for whoever built the first real driver. This PR adds `attempt_state_transitions` + `attempts_guard_transition` (§2.10, `0002_bootstrap` regenerated from the doc) — five edges: `pending -> delivering`, `failed -> delivering`, and `delivering -> {succeeded, failed, dead}`. `delivering -> dead` covers both reasons §8.5 stops retrying outright (`maxAttempts` exhausted, or an immediate 410 Gone); there is no `failed -> dead` edge, because the exhausted-attempts decision happens once, at the `delivering -> {failed | dead}` write itself. A `delivering` row whose lease expired (crash-reclaim) is a same-state write — same shape `Message`'s own `routed` reclaim already uses — so it needs no row in the table at all. Verified against a real Postgres, not just `cratestack check`: `ci/test-state-machine.sql` gained assertions for the auto-stamped `delivered_at`, the terminal `succeeded`/`dead` states, and the `failed -> delivering` retry edge; `ci/assert-state-machine-parity.py` now checks a third machine (`attempt`, alongside `message`/`job`) against a new `stateDiagram-v2` block in §8.5.

**`maxAttempts` is a `WebhookEndpoint` column, read per candidate, not a constant** — the backoff schedule itself (§8.5: 1s, 5s, 25s, 2m, 10m, 1h, 6h, 24h) is fixed and shared, same convention `jobs::backoff_for` already set, but how many entries into it a given endpoint actually gets before `dead` is that endpoint's own row.

**What resets `WebhookEndpoint.consecutiveFailures` — a design question the issue posed explicitly, answered and documented in §8.5's own "Implementation, #40" note:** a successful delivery resets it to zero (and clears `circuitOpenUntil`, defensively); crossing the 20-failure threshold that *opens* the circuit also resets it to zero, so the cool-down period is followed by a fresh 20-failure allowance rather than the very next post-cooldown failure reopening the circuit immediately. Candidate selection excludes both an inactive endpoint and one whose circuit is open — application-side, in `claim.rs`'s own `filter_by_endpoint_health`, not a join in the claim query itself, since `WebhookEndpoint` has no `@version` for a join to claim against even if the delegates offered one. By construction this also means `hooks` never records a failure against an endpoint whose circuit is already open — it never attempts one.

**What gets POSTed, confirmed before deciding what to sign, per the issue's own warning against that exact bug class:** `WebhookAttempt.payload` (written by #38's subscribers) is the §8.4 `data` object only, not the outer envelope — confirmed by rereading `webhooks.rs`'s own module doc before writing a line of `hooks.rs`. `build_envelope` constructs `{id, type, occurredAt, data}` at delivery time: `id` from `WebhookAttempt.id` (the only value that has existed since the row was created), `type` from `eventType`, `data` from `payload` parsed back into a JSON value and embedded as a nested object — never re-wrapped as a string. `occurredAt` is a documented approximation, not the original event's timestamp: `WebhookAttempt` carries no creation timestamp (no `@use(Timestamps)` on that model), and the framework's own `ModelEvent::occurred_at` is read and discarded by #38's subscriber before this row exists to store it on. `hooks` stamps it with the time of delivery instead — accurate for a first attempt, wrong by up to the full backoff schedule's span (24h) for an attempt that only succeeds on its last try. Closing this needs either `WebhookAttempt` to gain a stored event timestamp or #38's subscriber to thread `ModelEvent::occurred_at` through to it — both real changes to a model #38 owns, flagged here rather than silently accepted or fixed as a drive-by. **What gets signed is exactly what gets sent:** `build_envelope` returns one `String`; its bytes are both what `sms_webhook::sign_header` HMACs and what `reqwest` puts on the wire, never two independent serializations of the same logical value — `serde_json::Value`'s map type does not guarantee stable key order or whitespace across two separate `to_string()` calls, so a second serialization would risk exactly the silent sender/receiver mismatch this issue named as the classic bug to avoid.

**`maskRecipient` — enforced upstream (#38), verified not to be defeated here.** The masked (or unmasked) `to` value is already baked into `payload` at insert time via `sms_msisdn::Msisdn::masked`; `hooks.rs` never reads a `Message` row and never touches `payload`'s individual fields — it parses the whole object and embeds it verbatim. `hooks_live_postgres.rs`'s `a_masked_recipient_reaches_the_endpoint_unchanged_never_reconstructed` proves this against a real HTTP capture (wiremock's own `received_requests()`), not just a unit test of `build_envelope`: the masked value from a hand-seeded payload survives byte-for-byte into the actual POST body, and the fixture's own unmasked E.164 form is asserted absent from that body entirely.

**The ninth instance of this file's own `hasRole('system')` gap — flagged in advance by #38/#39's own PR, not found live.** `WebhookAttempt`'s `list`/`detail` policy had `create`/`update` admitting `hasRole('system')` from the start but no such clause on `list`/`detail` — #38/#39's own module doc named this explicitly as the gap `#40`'s claim loop would need closed the moment it existed. Fixed here (`schema.cstack` only, no DDL consequence), `backends/crates/sms-api/tests/system_context_golden_list_live_postgres.rs` moved `WebhookAttempt` into `SYSTEM_READABLE_MODELS`, and the guard was proven to actually fail first — `hasRole('system')` temporarily removed from the `list`/`detail` clause reproduced exactly `assert_system_can_read_back!`'s failure message, naming `WebhookAttempt` and its (broken) clause, before being restored.

**Endpoint-side writes (`consecutiveFailures`, `circuitOpenUntil`, `active`) carried no CAS at the time** — `WebhookEndpoint` had no `@version`, the same fact `rotateWebhookSecret`'s own doc named (that procedure closed a *different* race with `@isolation("serializable")`, which didn't cover concurrent `hooks` workers failing against the same endpoint). Accepted as a best-effort read-modify-write: under concurrent failures the counter could under-count by a small amount, which only mattered for a heuristic that exists to stop hammering a dead endpoint, not a value anything bills against. **#59 added `@version` and `if_match` to these three writes** (`backends/crates/sms-worker/src/hooks.rs`'s `record_endpoint_failure`/`reset_endpoint_failures`/`deactivate_endpoint`) without changing this paragraph's own accepted-under-count reasoning: each call site's existing `if let Err(error) = ... { warn!(...) }` already treated any write failure as best-effort and moved on, so a lost CAS race falls into the identical arm a lost silent-overwrite used to — the under-count is now a *detected*, not a *silent*, best-effort miss. See that section for the full reasoning.

`backends/crates/sms-worker/src/lib.rs`'s role dispatch gained `Role::Hooks => { hooks::run(ctx, worker).await; return; }` — the small, mechanical addition #38/#39's own PR predicted would conflict trivially with a sibling PR adding `Role::Hooks`'s real body, which this is. The stub test that used to exercise `Role::Hooks` (`a_stub_role_never_resolves_on_its_own`) now uses `Role::Smpp` instead — the only role left with no real body (M7).

`backends/crates/sms-worker/tests/hooks_live_postgres.rs` (8 tests, `wiremock`-backed): a clean delivery with a verified `sms_webhook::verify` signature and envelope shape against the exact bytes captured off the wire; a transient failure's backoff and endpoint bookkeeping; `maxAttempts` exhaustion going straight to `dead`; 410 Gone killing the attempt and deactivating the endpoint in the same tick; the circuit breaker opening and excluding the endpoint from the very next claim; the masked-recipient pass-through above; a malformed-payload attempt proven to leave the endpoint's circuit-breaker state untouched (see below); and a simulated crash-reclaim (a stale `delivering` lease) resuming without double-counting `attempts`. All pass stably across reruns against the same persistent database, same convention every other live suite here follows.

**Found by Lightbridge's review of this PR, confirmed real, and fixed before merge: a malformed payload must never trip an endpoint's circuit breaker.** The first cut routed a `WebhookAttempt.payload` that failed to parse as JSON — our own bug, written by #38's subscriber, not the endpoint's fault — into the same `Outcome::Retryable` arm a genuine HTTP failure uses, whose handling unconditionally calls `record_endpoint_failure`. A single corrupt row could therefore increment `consecutiveFailures` and open the circuit on an endpoint that was never sent a request and never did anything wrong — and retrying was pointless besides, since a stored payload never becomes parseable, so it only delayed an identical `dead` outcome while tripping the breaker on every attempt along the way. Fixed with a fourth `Outcome::MalformedPayload` variant, handled by its own arm: straight to `dead` with a loud `error!` (matching `#42`'s own `reap_outbox` precedent above — "make a broken row loud rather than quietly retry or hide it" — landed in the same milestone), and no call to `record_endpoint_failure` at all. `hooks_live_postgres.rs`'s `a_malformed_payload_goes_dead_without_ever_touching_the_endpoints_circuit_state` asserts the thing that actually matters — `consecutiveFailures` stays `0`, `circuitOpenUntil` stays unset, `active` stays `true`, and zero HTTP requests were ever made — not merely that the attempt reached a terminal state, which would have passed both before and after the fix. Verified to actually catch the regression: temporarily routing back through `Outcome::Retryable` reproduced a real test failure before the fix was restored.

## `#187`/`#193` — `WebhookEndpoint`'s two RBAC gaps closed together, landed 2026-08-11

Both found while implementing #41/#43, both filed rather than fixed inline (a schema policy question and a Layer 2 asymmetry, neither in scope for the PR that noticed them), both latent for the identical reason and closed together because they're the same shape: a caller narrower than "any human"/"nothing" respectively, and both invisible until M4 ships human logins (`GatewayAuth` still only ever mints `role: "app"`/`"system"` — #97/#98's scope cut, unchanged by this PR).

**`#187`: `WebhookEndpoint.read` narrowed from `auth().kind == "user"` to `hasRole('owner') || hasRole('admin') || hasRole('developer') || hasRole('system')`.** Before this, `auditor`/`operator`/`support` — none of which hold any webhook permission per §5.2 — could read a live signing secret that only `owner`/`admin`/`developer` could ever write. `@sensitive` never mitigated this (redacts audit snapshots only, no serde attribute — the field is still returned by the API, per this file's own §2.0 entry). **This is a reversible product call, not a forced one** — the issue's own named alternative was deciding secrets shouldn't live on a model humans read at all, on the grounds that an `auditor`'s oversight mandate is "see everything." Taken anyway, on the reasoning that oversight is served by seeing *that* a secret exists, was rotated, and when (every other field, untouched), not by seeing the secret's own value: a signing secret is a credential, not a record. `docs/architecture.md` §2.7's own `WebhookEndpoint` code sample and its "readable by humans only" paragraph are both updated to match — the code sample had also drifted from #41's actual `hasRole('system')` clause on `update` (present in `schema.cstack` since #41, never synced into the doc's illustrative block), fixed in the same edit since this PR was already touching that exact block.

Policy-only, no DDL: confirmed byte-identical, not assumed — `cratestack migrate diff` (isolated `=0.7.10` CLI, matching the pin) against the changed schema still diffs to nothing against the committed `0001_init/{up,down}.sql`, same verification `#100`/`#121` each record for their own analogous `@@allow` fixes. Migrations were correspondingly **not** regenerated.

**`#193`: `rotateWebhookSecret` gained `require_permission(ctx, "webhook:manage")`, matching `replayWebhookAttempt`'s own Layer 2 gate (#43, #191).** Before this fix, rotation — the more sensitive of the two operations, since it changes the credential every future delivery is signed with and starts the `prevSecret` overlap clock — enforced only Layer 1, while the less sensitive replay enforced both. The issue's own honest alternative (decide Layer 2 is redundant given Layer 1's already-role-scoped `@@allow`, and strike `webhook:manage` from §5.2's vocabulary instead) was rejected: a permission that appears in the role table and is never checked anywhere is worse than none, since it implies a control that doesn't exist, and `replayWebhookAttempt` already depends on it being real.

**The trap this PR's own test-writing hit, worth recording for the next Layer-2 addition to an existing procedure: adding a `require_permission` call to a procedure breaks every existing test that calls it with a context built by hand.** `Principal::into_context()` (`backends/crates/sms-api/src/auth.rs`) never populates a `perms`/`scope` extension — only a real `GatewayAuth::authenticate` does, from JWT claims. `rotate_webhook_secret_live_postgres.rs`'s three pre-existing tests all called the procedure with a plain `owner()` context and started failing `Forbidden` the moment the gate was added, including the one asserting `NotFound` for a bogus id — `require_permission` runs *before* the lookup, so a missing-permission denial and a missing-row `NotFound` are trivially confused if the test context doesn't carry the claim the new gate checks for. Fixed by adding an `owner_with_webhook_manage()` helper (a plain `owner()` context plus a hand-inserted `perms: ["webhook:manage"]` extension) and switching every direct-call test onto it — the same pattern `replay_webhook_attempt_live_postgres.rs`'s own `developer_with_webhook_manage()` already established for the sibling procedure, just not yet needed here until this PR added the gate that makes it necessary.

**Denial proven live, the same way `replayWebhookAttempt`'s own gate was proven — the *allow* case still has no live token to prove it with (#97/#98).** `rotate_denies_a_caller_with_no_webhook_manage_permission` points the call at an endpoint id that doesn't exist and asserts `Forbidden`, not `NotFound` — the latter would mean the check was skipped and the lookup ran anyway. Verified to actually catch a real regression, not just pass by construction: temporarily commenting out the `require_permission` call reproduced exactly that failure (`expected Forbidden, got NotFound("no WebhookEndpoint with id ...")`) before the call was restored.

## Milestone 5 started: the MTN adapter via a licensed aggregator (#61)

`backends/crates/sms-provider-mtn` is a second `SmsProvider` implementation, mirroring `backends/crates/sms-provider-orange-cm`'s structure and both traps it already learned. Per the epic (#60) and the issue's own text, this targets MTN capacity **bought through a licensed aggregator, not a direct MTN interconnect** — the same posture §6.4 already recommends and the path that avoids decision #4 (own ART title?) entirely.

**No real aggregator contract exists, and none of this has been run against a live endpoint** — the crate's own module doc carries the full honesty ledger, but the short version: the connect-vs-read transport-error classification (`classify_transport_error`, identical reasoning to Orange's) is exactly as trustworthy as it is in `sms-provider-orange-cm`, because it follows from what `reqwest` itself guarantees, not from any vendor's docs. The request/response JSON shape (`POST {base_url}/v1/messages`, Bearer API-key auth, a `201` + `messageId`, a DLR `POST` echoing that same `messageId`) is an **invented placeholder**, chosen to match the common pattern across the aggregators §6.2 already names as candidates (Nexah, Africa's Talking, Infobip, Twilio), not transcribed from a real Swagger the way Orange's shape was transcribed from §6.2. Replace the request/response structs — not the `SmsProvider` impl or the error classification around them — the moment a real contract exists.

**`Capabilities` is genuinely different here, and it's a structural difference, not just different constants.** Orange's `capabilities()` is a bare function with no inputs — every field is a fixed fact about a self-service product. MTN-via-aggregator has no such fixed facts: `tps_ceiling`, `cost_per_segment_xaf`, and `supports_alphanumeric_sender` are commercial terms of a specific contract, so `MtnAggregatorConfig` carries them as caller-supplied fields and `capabilities()` reads them back rather than returning a compiled-in constant. This is the actual answer to #61's "the routing layer must read them rather than assume parity" — a router that special-cased `if key == "orange_cm"` couldn't even express MTN's numbers, since they aren't constants to special-case against. One field goes the other direction: this crate's assumed DLR shape echoes back the same `messageId` `submit()` already returns, so unlike Orange (which needs `receiptRequest.callbackData` + `SubmitAck::provider_ref_alt` to work around a DLR that can't otherwise correlate, #95), `provider_ref_alt` is always `None` here — a property of the *assumed* shape, not a proven simplification; revisit it alongside `dlr::parse` the moment a real payload exists.

**The connect-vs-read `Indeterminate` guard was proven to fail, not just asserted to pass** — house standard, applied here as it was for `#119`'s original Orange implementation: `classify_transport_error` was temporarily edited to swap the two branches (a connect failure reporting `Indeterminate`, a post-connect timeout/body error reporting `Unavailable`), and both `a_connect_refusal_is_still_unavailable` and `a_post_connect_timeout_is_indeterminate` failed with the exact `assertion failed: matches!(...)` message naming the wrong variant, before the fix was restored and both passed again.

**No new external dependency.** Every crate `sms-provider-mtn` depends on (`sms-provider`, `async-trait`, `chrono`, `reqwest`, `rust_decimal`, `serde`, `serde_json`, `thiserror`, `tracing`; `sms-encoding`/`tokio`/`wiremock` as dev-dependencies) was already in the workspace graph via `sms-provider-orange-cm`. `cargo tree -i aws-lc-rs` still reports no match, `cargo deny check` stays clean, and `./ci/assert-no-raw-sqlx.sh` passes untouched — this crate makes no database call of any kind, matching `sms-provider-orange-cm`'s own R1-exempt status (never touches R1 in the first place: it's a pure HTTP adapter).

**One real duplication worth naming rather than fixing here:** `classify_transport_error`'s connect-vs-read logic is now byte-for-byte the same reasoning in two crates (`sms-provider-orange-cm` and `sms-provider-mtn`), because it's provider-agnostic `reqwest` behaviour, not provider-specific. Not factored into a shared helper in `sms-provider` itself in this PR — two instances isn't yet a rule, and #61's scope is "build the adapter," not "refactor the trait crate." Worth doing the moment a third HTTP adapter (the config-driven `AggregatorHttpProvider` §6.2 also names, or SMPP's own connect/write distinction) needs the same logic a third time.

## Milestone 5: the routing rules engine landed (#62)

`backends/crates/sms-routing` replaces `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/claim.rs`'s M2 `cheapest_active_provider` placeholder with real `Route`-rule routing over §6.3: priority, weight, and `matchOperator`/`matchClass`/`matchAppId`/`matchPrefix` predicates. `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/routing.rs` is the I/O glue between the two.

**The engine is a new, deliberately pure crate, not a module inside `sms-worker`.** #54's admin route simulator has to answer "given this recipient/class/app, which route wins and why" *without sending anything* — the same requirement that shaped `sms-provider`'s own purity (no cratestack, no I/O). `sms_routing::select_route` takes a caller-fetched `&[RouteRow]`, a caller-fetched `&HashMap<String, ProviderRow>`, a `RoutingCandidate`, a caller-supplied `exclude: &ExcludedRouteIds`, and a caller-drawn `f64`, and returns a `Decision` — nothing in the crate touches a database, a clock, or an RNG. `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/routing.rs::decide` is the one place that fetches rows and draws the randomness (`rand::random()`), the same "convert the schema's enum onto the pure crate's mirrored one" pattern `dispatch.rs`'s own `decode_encoding` already established for `Encoding`.

**Weight implies randomness; "deterministic and explainable" (#62's own framing) implies it can't generate its own.** Resolved by injecting the draw rather than generating it: production draws once per decision at the I/O boundary; a replay (#54's simulator, every unit test in `backends/crates/sms-routing/src/engine.rs`) calls `select_route` again with the exact same `draw` and gets the exact same `Decision`, because the function is a pure fold over its arguments. Within the winning priority band, the draw maps to a cumulative-weight range per member (`[low, high)` over `[0.0, 1.0)`); an all-zero-weight band falls back to a uniform split rather than dividing by zero (`weight == 0` is a legal `@range` value, not an error).

**Priority is the only cross-band ordering — no independent "more specific route wins" tiebreak.** A route with three `match_*` predicates set doesn't automatically outrank a wildcard route at the same priority; an operator who wants that has to set a higher `priority` explicitly. Either reading was defensible; this one was chosen because §6.3's own text ("sort by priority then weighted-random within a priority band") already says so — it wasn't a free design choice this PR made, just the first place anyone had to act on it precisely.

**`NULL` on any `match_*` column is a wildcard ("matches anything"), not "matches only a `NULL` value".** Stated explicitly because the schema gives no other signal — `matchOperator OperatorCode?` reads exactly as ambiguously as "match nothing with an operator set" would, and getting this backwards would make every route in a fresh deployment silently match zero traffic.

**`Route.matchPrefix` reuses `sms_msisdn::Msisdn::national()`, not a hand-rolled E.164 substring check** — the same national-digit convention `OperatorPrefixRule.prefix` and `sms_msisdn::OperatorPrefixTable` already use, so a route authored against `"677"` means the same thing an `OperatorPrefixRule` row authored against `"677"` does.

**No `Route` rows configured at all now refuses to dispatch, loudly — a real behaviour change from the M2 placeholder, weighed deliberately.** `cheapest_active_provider` always had exactly one thing to pick from (any active `Provider`); once routing is a real rule set, a silent "no routes configured yet, fall back to picking anything" path would mean routing quietly stops being explainable the moment *any* `Route` row exists but doesn't match — precisely the failure mode #62 exists to close. So an `accepted` message with zero eligible routes goes to `rejected`, `stateReason` carrying `routing::explain_no_route`'s own per-route summary. The cost of that choice is real, not hypothetical: `just demo` seeded no `Route` row before this PR, so `backends/crates/sms-api/examples/send_test_message.rs` (what the demo's own fixture step runs) now also seeds a catch-all `Route` alongside its `Provider`, idempotently, the same self-healing discipline its neighbouring `ensure_provider` already uses.

**`WorkerContext.provider` (a single `Arc<dyn SmsProvider>`) became `WorkerContext.providers` (a registry keyed by `SmsProvider::key`/`Provider.key`).** Routing can pick any configured route's provider now, so `dispatch` has to resolve *which* adapter a given routed message actually needs at submit time (`dispatch::resolve_provider`: `Message.providerId` → `Provider` row → `Provider.key` → registry lookup) rather than submit everything through one hardcoded field. `backends/apps/sms-worker/src/main.rs` builds this registry with exactly one entry today (`"orange_cm"`) — unaffected by #61's `sms-provider-mtn` landing in parallel, since that PR added only the adapter crate, not its own wiring into `main.rs`; a second real entry is a second `.insert(...)`, not a redesign. Two failure modes `resolve_provider` treats as retryable (`ProviderError::Unavailable`-shaped backoff, not an outright fail): the `Provider` row named by `providerId` was deleted since routing ran, and — a real operational case, not just defensive coding — a `Route` naming a provider this specific `dispatch` process has no credentials configured for (today `dispatch` is a §7.1 singleton, so this keeps retrying until an operator fixes the mismatch, which is the correct, safe behaviour, not a crash). The per-tick claim budget is now the *sum* of every registered provider's `tps_ceiling` (`dispatch::total_tps_ceiling`) rather than one provider's — identical to the old behaviour with exactly one provider registered, and deliberately coarse about per-provider throughput isolation (§6.3's own "remaining TPS/daily budget" filtering), left for #63.

**The tenth instance of this file's own repeated `hasRole('system')` gap — flagged in advance, not found live.** `Route`'s `read` `@@allow` didn't admit `hasRole('system')`, the same shape as the nine instances before it, and `routing::decide` is the first internal reader this model has ever had. Caught by #155's own golden test before merge: `every_model_in_the_schema_is_classified` would have failed the moment `Route` moved from `NOT_REQUIRED_TO_BE_SYSTEM_READABLE` to `SYSTEM_READABLE_MODELS` without a clause to back it up, and `every_system_readable_model_actually_admits_a_system_read` failed for real, on purpose, when the clause was pulled to prove the guard actually guards something — reproduced the exact `"Route... could not be read back"` failure message before being restored. Policy-only, no DDL consequence — `schema.cstack` edited, migrations left untouched, per this file's own standing rule.

**Test-fixture blast radius, found by actually running the affected suites, not assumed contained.** Four live suites create a `Message` and drive it through `claim.rs`'s `accepted` branch, which now genuinely needs a matching, enabled `Route`, not just an active `Provider`: `claim_live_postgres.rs`, `dispatch_live_postgres.rs`, `chaos_live_postgres.rs` (`backends/crates/sms-worker/tests/`), and `kill9_reclaim_live.rs` (`backends/apps/sms-worker/tests/`, the M2/#36 gate). Each now seeds a matching wildcard `Route` alongside its `Provider`. `dispatch_live_postgres.rs` needed the strongest fix, since its own assertions depend on submitting through one specific `wiremock` server, not just "some" provider: `seed_routed_provider` disables every leftover enabled route first (`disable_every_route`) so a stale route from an earlier run in the same never-reset-between-runs database can't win the weighted draw and point at a provider this test's own `WorkerContext.providers` registry was never told about — the same shape of test-isolation bug this file's own `clear_claimable_backlog`/`deactivate_every_active_provider` already document, applied to `Route`. `chaos_live_postgres.rs` and `kill9_reclaim_live.rs` didn't need that stronger guarantee: both already deactivate every other `Provider` (or, for `kill9_reclaim_live.rs`, reuse one fixed, idempotently-created `Provider` keyed exactly `"orange_cm"` — the real `OrangeCmProvider::key()`, required now that the spawned real worker process resolves providers by key, not by picking *any* active row) before seeding their own, so a leftover route's own provider is already excluded as unavailable regardless of which route wins a tie.

Full workspace live suite (`cargo test --workspace --no-fail-fast -- --ignored`) run clean against this change, including the M2 gate (`kill9_reclaim_live.rs`) and the M2/M3 chaos suite's five seeded sweeps — the one failure encountered (`hooks_node_receiver_live.rs`, missing `pnpm install` in `examples/node/webhook-receiver`) was a pre-existing local environment gap, unrelated to this PR, fixed separately.

Out of scope, named explicitly rather than silently deferred: failover and circuit breakers (`failoverRouteId`, #63) — the engine's `exclude: &ExcludedRouteIds` parameter and its `Winner.failover_route_id` field exist specifically so #63 can ask "give me the next route after this one failed" by calling `select_route` again with the failed id added to `exclude`, without needing to know anything about how routing itself works. Capability fit (UCS-2 support, alphanumeric sender) and remaining TPS/daily budget (§6.3's own further post-ranking filters) aren't modelled either — no second provider with materially different capabilities existed to make either concrete when this landed, `#61` having only just added `sms-provider-mtn`'s crate, not its wiring. The admin simulator UI itself is #54.

**The fifth instance of this file's own "documentation asserts something the code does not do" pattern — caught in review, before merge, not found live.** The paragraph above fixed the fixtures every test file needed and `just demo`'s own seed script, but missed the *production* deploy path entirely: `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/main.rs`'s `seed-provider` subcommand (#148) — the command both `docs/runbooks/deployment.adoc` and `docs/runbooks/getting-started.adoc` tell an operator to run — only ever created the `Provider` row, never a `Route`. A deployment that followed either runbook via that command after this PR merged would have started `sms-gateway` healthy, started `sms-worker` healthy, and silently rejected every single message forever — the exact failure class this file's own four prior doc-drift incidents (the M1 `/token` claim, `rust-version`, the `/token`-rate-limiting table row, OTP body storage) already warned about, found here a fifth time because the reviewer checked the actual deploy path a PR touching routing's own default behaviour implies, not just the PR's own diff. Fixed by renaming the subcommand `seed-provider` → `seed-dispatch` (a hard rename, not an alias — every reference updated in the same change: `deploy/docker-compose.yml`, `deploy/charts/vsms/values.yaml`'s `seedProvider` hook renamed to `seedDispatch`, both runbooks) and extending it to seed a hardcoded catch-all `Route` alongside the `Provider`, idempotently on both halves — `Provider` by `create` + catch-`23505` as before, `Route` by find-by-`providerId`-then-create (no unique column to catch a conflict on; a documented, accepted TOCTOU window narrower in practice than it sounds, since this command only ever runs from a Helm hook Job or a manual one-at-a-time `docker compose run --rm`, neither of which runs two instances concurrently). One real implementation bug caught mid-review, not left in: the first draft returned early on "`Provider` already active" *before* the `Route` half ran at all — the exact steady-state case a `pre-upgrade` hook hits on every single upgrade — which would have meant the `Route` half only ever ran on a truly fresh install, never a re-run. Verified against a real fresh scratch Postgres, not by inspection: `createdb` + `ci/apply-migrations.sh` + `cargo run -p sms-gateway -- seed-dispatch` (confirmed idempotent by running it twice), then a real `accepted` `Message` row created with no other fixtures at all, run through the real `claim::claim_batch::<Message>` routing pass — reached `queued`, stamped with exactly the `providerId`/`routeId` `seed-dispatch` had just created, using nothing but that one command's own output.

## Milestone 5: failover and the provider circuit breaker landed (#63)

`backends/crates/sms-worker/src/dispatch.rs` closes the gap #62's own status paragraph named explicitly: "failover and circuit breakers (`failoverRouteId`, #63)". The mapping is driven entirely by `sms_provider::ProviderError::routing()` (`backends/crates/sms-provider/src/error.rs`) — a compiler-checked table, not a second hand-derived one — so this PR adds no new classification logic of its own, only the reaction to an existing one:

| `RoutingConsequence` | error | what `handle_submit_error` does |
|---|---|---|
| `RetryThisProvider { after }` | `Transient` | same-provider backoff, `routed -> queued`; no failover |
| `TryNextRoute` | `Permanent` | `attempt_failover`; exhausted -> `routed -> failed`, "no alternate route available" |
| `OpenCircuitAndTryNextRoute` | `Unavailable` | `record_provider_failure`, then `attempt_failover`; exhausted -> same-provider backoff (the old M2 single-provider degradation, still correct once no alternate exists) |
| `FailMessage` | `Rejected`/`Unsupported` | `routed -> failed`; no failover — retrying anywhere fails identically |
| `HoldIndeterminate` | `Indeterminate` | `routed -> uncertain`; unchanged from #119 — never even considered for failover |

**"Failover must not double-send: the claim loop's lease is what prevents it"** (the issue's own words) is the property `attempt_failover` is built around, and it is why this PR needed no new claim-loop mechanism at all. A failover reroute never calls `SmsProvider::submit` a second time inline — it writes `routed -> queued` with a *new* `providerId`/`routeId` stamped on the same `if_match(version)`-CAS'd row (`dispatch::write_failover`, mirroring `claim::apply_routing_decision`'s own `accepted` winner branch: `leaseUntil` set to `now`, immediately claimable, not a real lease) and returns. The actual resubmit happens on a later claim, through the ordinary `queued -> routed -> submit` path every other reclaim in this crate already relies on. This is only safe because `TryNextRoute`/`OpenCircuitAndTryNextRoute` (`Permanent`/`Unavailable`) both mean *nothing was ever accepted by the provider* — `Indeterminate`, the one variant that can't make that claim, never reaches `attempt_failover` at all, proven live (not just by the existing `ProviderError::routing()` unit test) by `dispatch_live_postgres.rs`'s pre-existing `an_indeterminate_submit_lands_in_uncertain_and_is_never_resubmitted`, unchanged by this PR and still passing.

**`Route.failoverRouteId` is not read by the failover mechanism.** Per #62's own module doc on `sms_routing::select_route`'s `exclude` parameter — "call `select_route` again with the failed route's id added to `exclude`" — that is exactly what `attempt_failover` does, over the *whole* route set, not a hand-rolled walk of one field naming a single specific next hop. `failoverRouteId` stays exactly what #62 already said it was: carried through `Winner` for an operator's own explanation trail, never consulted by the pure engine or by this PR's own caller.

**`Message.excludedRouteIds` (a new nullable column, `schema.cstack`) is each message's own accumulated exclude set, sentinel-packed via `sms_core::pack`/`unpack`** — necessary, not just convenient. A `Permanent` failure never opens a provider's circuit breaker (`backends/crates/sms-provider/src/error.rs`'s own `permanent_never_opens_the_circuit_breaker` test) because it is specific to this one message (an unapproved sender ID, say), not a provider-wide outage — so nothing else marks that route ineligible for a second attempt by this same message. Without remembering it per-message, a second failover hop could pick the exact same already-failing route right back and fail identically forever. Capped at two entries (§6.3: "Failover capped at two hops — beyond that you're not routing, you're spraying") — `attempt_failover` checks the set's length *after* adding the currently-failing route and refuses to even query `select_route` once it would exceed two, the same "the caller isn't willing to act on the answer anyway" reasoning `sms_routing`'s own doc uses elsewhere.

**The provider circuit breaker (`Provider.consecutiveFailures`/`circuitOpenUntil`, two new columns) deliberately mirrors `WebhookEndpoint`'s own shape (#40/#41/#59) rather than inventing different semantics for a second breaker in this codebase** — same two fields, same reset-on-success-and-on-trip discipline (`dispatch::record_provider_failure`/`reset_provider_failures`, matching `hooks::record_endpoint_failure`/`reset_endpoint_failures` almost line for line), same best-effort `if_match`-CAS'd writes (a lost race under concurrent `dispatch` workers undercounts by a small amount — accepted, matching the identical reasoning `hooks.rs` already documents, since this is a heuristic to stop hammering a dead provider, not something anyone bills against). The constants differ because the spec differs: §6.3 says five consecutive failures and 60s for providers, not webhook delivery's 20-failure/15-minute numbers — same shape, different subsystem.

`backends/crates/sms-worker/src/routing.rs::convert_provider` is the one reader, and it is the literal mechanism behind the ticket's own second acceptance clause ("must not fail a message a healthy alternative could carry"): an open circuit is treated exactly like `state != active` (`available = false`, with a reason) in the same `HashMap<String, ProviderRow>` every routing decision already builds, so *every future* `accepted` message — not just the one whose failure tripped the breaker — naturally skips that provider. Proven live, at the strongest level this admits: `dispatch_live_postgres.rs`'s `an_open_circuit_routes_new_messages_to_the_alternative_instead_of_rejecting` asserts the broken provider's own submit-call counter stays flat while a sixth, brand-new message reaches `submitted` entirely through the healthy alternative — not merely that it *eventually* succeeds after being tried against the broken one first.

**Deliberately not the literal "half-open with a single probe" §6.3's own prose describes.** Both breakers in this codebase (`WebhookEndpoint`'s and now `Provider`'s) fully reopen the moment `circuitOpenUntil` passes, with no rate-limited single-probe admission — a true single-probe half-open state needs a coordination mechanism (exactly one in-flight probe across however many `dispatch` processes exist) neither breaker has, and building one just for `Provider` would be a second, differently-shaped breaker for one property, which this section's own precedent (follow `hooks.rs`'s shape rather than inventing a better one) argues against. Revisit if a real incident shows the simpler reopen is too aggressive.

**Found live, not by review, and it is why the first attempt at the circuit-breaker test failed with the breaker never actually opening: `Provider`'s own `update` `@@allow` didn't admit `hasRole('system')`.** `dispatch.rs`'s new circuit-breaker writes run under this crate's internal `sys()` context, and without that clause every one of `record_provider_failure`/`reset_provider_failures`'s writes returned `Forbidden("update policy denied this operation")` — silently absorbed by the exact same best-effort "log and drop" handling `hooks::record_endpoint_failure` already established for a lost CAS race, so nothing anywhere *looked* broken except the one live assertion that actually checked the effect (`circuitOpenUntil` genuinely set on the `Provider` row) rather than merely that the surrounding calls didn't panic. This is a different shape from this file's own nine-times-repeated `list`/`detail` gap (that one fails silently to an empty array; this one fails loudly with `Forbidden`, just absorbed by a call site that was already designed to treat any write failure as best-effort) — worth naming as its own instance rather than folding into that count. Fixed the same way every prior instance was: `schema.cstack` only, confirmed byte-identical DDL via `cratestack migrate diff` before and after the policy-only change. Safe to grant: `GatewayAuth::authenticate` (`backends/crates/sms-api/src/auth.rs`) constructs `role: "system"` exactly once, inside `Procedures::sys()`, never from a real bearer token (see that module's own "Never `system`" comment), so this adds no new HTTP-reachable capability to `PATCH /providers/{id}` (`router::PROVIDER_WRITE_ROUTES`) — `policy_golden_list_live_postgres.rs`'s own `provider_update_policy_matches_the_schema_exactly` now asserts the system-context case succeeds instead of asserting it was forbidden, with an inline note explaining why the reversal is safe.

**Guard-failure proofs, house standard.** (a) The pre-existing, single-provider `an_indeterminate_submit_lands_in_uncertain_and_is_never_resubmitted` turned out unable to prove "never fails over" on its own — its fixture has only one route, so a broken implementation that fails `Indeterminate` over anyway finds nothing eligible to reroute to and lands in the identical `uncertain` outcome by accident, passing for the wrong reason. Caught by actually running the break, not assumed: temporarily adding `RoutingConsequence::HoldIndeterminate` to `handle_submit_error`'s `should_attempt_failover` match left that test green. Fixed by adding a second test with a real healthy alternative, `an_indeterminate_submit_never_fails_over_even_with_a_healthy_alternative_available` (the two-provider fixture, `AlwaysErr`/`AlwaysOk`) — the same break against this test failed with `left: queued / right: uncertain` at the post-submit assertion (the message had rerouted to B and reached `queued` instead of staying `uncertain`), reverted, and reconfirmed all 11 of this file's tests green. (b) An open circuit routing to the alternative rather than rejecting: temporarily dropping the `!circuit_open` clause from `routing.rs::convert_provider`'s `available` computation reproduced `an_open_circuit_routes_new_messages_to_the_alternative_instead_of_rejecting` failing with `left: Some("<provider-a-id>") / right: Some("<provider-b-id>")` — the sixth, brand-new message's `providerId` came back pointed at the broken provider instead of the healthy one — reverted, reconfirmed green.

**Two active `Provider`s and two `Route`s, not one, is a new live-fixture shape this PR introduces (`dispatch_live_postgres.rs::seed_two_routed_providers`)** — every earlier live suite touching `claim.rs`'s `accepted` branch (`claim_live_postgres.rs`, the rest of `dispatch_live_postgres.rs`, `chaos_live_postgres.rs`, `kill9_reclaim_live.rs`) seeds exactly one of each, per #62's own "test-fixture blast radius" paragraph above. The three new tests (a `Permanent` failover, an open-circuit reroute, and the `Indeterminate`-never-fails-over guard above) also needed two small hand-rolled fake `SmsProvider` implementations (`AlwaysErr`/`AlwaysOk`, call-counted via `AtomicUsize`) rather than reusing `wiremock`-backed `OrangeCmProvider`: that adapter structurally never produces `ProviderError::Permanent` — every 4xx it sees other than 429 becomes `Rejected` (`sms-provider-orange-cm`'s own `classify_submit_error` doc) — so the `TryNextRoute` arm can't be exercised through the real adapter at all, and an exact submit-call count (not just a final `Message` state) is what actually proves "never resubmitted"/"never even attempted," the same discipline `an_indeterminate_submit_lands_in_uncertain_and_is_never_resubmitted`'s own `wiremock` `.expect(1)` already established.

## Milestone 5: grey-route detection landed (#64)

§6.4's own two-sentence spec — "monthly handset validation per route, plus an alert on delivery-rate divergence between routes that should behave identically" — splits cleanly into a buildable half and one that fundamentally cannot be automated, and the split is the whole design decision here, restated because it is easy to blur: a grey route rewrites the sender ID on the wire, after this system has already handed the message to a provider, and `DeliveryReceipt` still reports `delivered` regardless. No server-side signal can see the substitution. `OPEN_QUESTIONS.md` §2.4 already recorded this before any code landed; this PR narrows, but does not close, that gap — see that entry's own updated text for exactly what changed.

**The divergence half is fully buildable, and "should behave identically" is taken verbatim from the issue's own text: two routes to the *same operator*, for the *same message class*.** `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/jobs/grey_route_watch.rs` groups terminal `Message` outcomes by `(Message.operator, Message.class)` — not `Route.matchOperator`/`matchClass`, which describe what a route is willing to carry, not what it actually carried for a given message; `Message.operator`/`class` are stamped once at classification time and (for `routeId`) reflect the final route after any #63 failover reroute. `uncertain` (#119's own "the outcome was genuinely never learned") is excluded from both sides of the ratio, not folded into either — counting it as failure would flag every route behind a slow-to-respond provider purely from timeout volume.

**Sample size matters more than the delta — three independent, deliberately redundant gates, all documented inline in `grey_route_watch.rs`'s own module doc:** `MIN_SAMPLE` (30 terminal messages per side, a visible floor an operator can point to, not an emergent property of a formula), a two-proportion z-test (`Z_THRESHOLD = 3.0`, ~p<0.0027 two-tailed — the standard-error term is what naturally makes a 50%-vs-100%-over-4-messages delta score low and a 70%-vs-98%-over-1000-messages delta score high), and `MIN_DELTA` (15 points, a practical-significance floor stopping a statistically-real-but-operationally-meaningless gap like 98.0% vs 98.3% at huge volume from paging anyone). All three must hold; any one failing means no finding. `sms_route_delivery_divergence_flagged` (a sixth/seventh metric alongside #70/#71's original five, `backends/crates/sms-metrics`) and `deploy/prometheus/alerts.yml`'s `SMSRouteDeliveryDivergence` follow the exact shape those five already established.

**No `GROUP BY` in the delegate API, confirmed by reading `cratestack-sqlx`'s own `Aggregate` builder directly (`count`/`sum`/`avg`/`min`/`max`, none grouped)** — matching §9.1's own note on `dashboardSummary`'s ~26 individual `COUNT` queries for the identical reason. Grouping happens client-side over rows fetched through the ordinary `Message` delegate (still R1-compliant), bounded by `FETCH_LIMIT` (20,000) the same way `reap_outbox`'s own `DELETE_BATCH`/`ALERT_BATCH` bound their per-run work — a deployment with more terminal traffic than that in one 7-day `LOOKBACK` window gets the check computed over its most recent 20,000 messages, not an unbounded scan, and self-corrects on the next daily run regardless.

**The handset-validation half cannot be automated, and this PR does not pretend otherwise.** `RouteValidation` (`schema.cstack`, a new model — 23 models total now, up from 22 — §2.10 regenerated wholesale, id/`performed_at` defaults and one lookup index hand-added the same way `AuditAnchor`'s were) is an append-only evidence record: no `update` `@@allow` at all, matching `DeliveryReceipt`/`ConsentRecord`'s own precedent — correcting a mistaken entry means recording a new one, not editing history. `docs/runbooks/grey-route-validation.adoc` is the human procedure, the same shape `docs/runbooks/36-handset-gate.adoc` already set for "this needs a real phone, not a test suite." `sms-gateway record-route-validation` (a new CLI subcommand, `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/main.rs`, mirroring `provision-user`'s own "operator action, not generated CRUD" precedent) is what the runbook actually runs — writes through the plain delegate under a stand-in `operator`-role context, since `RouteValidation.create`'s own `@@allow` already admits `hasRole('owner') || hasRole('admin') || hasRole('operator')` and needs no dedicated `Procedures` method. `grey_route_watch`'s other half, `check_overdue_validations`, reads every `enabled` `Route`'s most recent `RouteValidation` under `sys` and flags anything with none in the last 30 days (`sms_route_validation_overdue`, `SMSRouteValidationOverdue`) — a staleness signal only, explicit in both the job's own module doc and the runbook's closing section: a route validated yesterday and turned grey today reports exactly as "fine" as one that never had a problem.

**`grey_route_watch` is a new job kind, not one of §7.5's original nine** — named and scheduled (daily, alongside `purge_retention`/`anchor_audit`) the same way every prior job is, registered in `crate::jobs::default_registry()`, but §7.5's own table is left untouched rather than retrofitted, since it documents the original design doc's own job list and this ticket's alert condition was never part of it.

**The fifteenth instance of this file's own repeated `hasRole('system')` gap — flagged in advance, per this PR's own precedent from `Route`/`User`/`Role`/`ConsentRecord`, not found live.** `RouteValidation.read`'s `@@allow` admitted `hasRole('system')` from the moment the model was written, since `check_overdue_validations` needs it from day one; `backends/crates/sms-api/tests/system_context_golden_list_live_postgres.rs` classifies it into `SYSTEM_READABLE_MODELS` in the same PR. Verified to actually guard something: with `hasRole('system')` temporarily removed from `RouteValidation`'s `read` clause, the live half of that test failed with `a system context could not read back the RouteValidation row it (or an owner, on its behalf) just seeded. RouteValidation's current schema.cstack read/list/detail policy is: @@allow("read", auth().kind == "user" || hasRole('system'))`; restoring the clause restored a pass.

**A real, upstream-documented trap hit while writing the schema, not a new one:** `RouteValidation.notes String? @length(max: 1000)` reproduced cratestack#537 verbatim — `@length` on a nullable `String?` breaks the generated `Update{Model}Input::validate()` at compile time (`E0308`, `&str` expected where `&Option<String>` is passed). `AuditAnchor.prevChainHash` worked around this with a non-null sentinel; a sentinel doesn't fit free-text notes, so `notes` simply carries no `@length` bound at all, matching `SenderId.notes`/`ConsentRecord.evidenceRef`'s own precedent for a nullable free-text field. `expectedSenderId`/`observedSenderId` are non-nullable and keep their `@length`, unaffected — only a nullable field triggers the bug.

**Guard-failure proof, the ticket's own named priority: a large, genuinely divergent sample is flagged; a tiny sample with a worse ratio is not — and the two gates that make that true were each shown to be load-bearing independently, not merely redundant with each other.** At `n=4` vs `n=4` (the issue's own named noise case, "4 messages and 50% delivery"), the z-test alone already refuses to flag regardless of `MIN_SAMPLE` — the largest possible z-score at that sample size (~2.83) never clears `Z_THRESHOLD` (3.0). That made `MIN_SAMPLE`'s own contribution provably real only at a size where the z-test *would* pass on its own: a second test at `n=20` vs `n=20` (100% vs 50%, z≈3.65 — past `Z_THRESHOLD`) with `MIN_SAMPLE`'s own filter temporarily bypassed reproduced a genuine `DivergenceFinding` that must never surface:

```
below MIN_SAMPLE must never be flagged even when the z-test alone would allow it: [DivergenceFinding { operator: orange, class: otp, reference_route_id: "route-a", reference_rate: 1.0, reference_n: 20, route_id: "route-b", rate: 0.5, n: 20, delta: 0.5, z_score: 3.651483716701107 }]
```

Restoring the real filter restored a pass. A second break proved `uncertain`-exclusion the identical way: temporarily folding `MessageState::uncertain` into the failure-count arm of `aggregate_outcomes` reproduced `assertion left == right failed: uncertain must not count as failed / left: 30 / right: 0` against a fixture of 30 delivered + 30 uncertain messages; restoring the real match arm restored a pass. All 15 of this file's own unit tests, plus five live-Postgres tests (`backends/crates/sms-worker/tests/grey_route_watch_live_postgres.rs`) proving the same DB-backed wiring — a genuinely divergent pair flagged through the real `Message` delegate, `RouteValidation`'s own system-context read, `run_at`'s two gauges always set, and the `JobHandler` entry point itself — pass stably.

## Milestone 5's own gate: the automated half of "kill Orange in staging" (#65) — landed 2026-08-13

§12's own M5 gate — "Kill Orange in staging; MTN unaffected, Orange fails over cleanly" — has an automatable half and a half that genuinely needs a real staging deployment, the same split M2's own `#36` gate already established as this repo's model. `backends/crates/sms-worker/tests/kill_orange_gate_live_postgres.rs` is the automatable half; `docs/runbooks/65-kill-orange-gate.adoc` is the rest, and does **not** claim the gate has passed — it hasn't; nobody has run it against a real staging deployment with real Orange/aggregator credentials yet.

**The whole point of this suite, versus every earlier #63 test: a real transport-level failure, through the real adapters, not a synthetic `ProviderError`.** `dispatch_live_postgres.rs`'s own `a_permanent_failure_fails_over_...`/`an_open_circuit_routes_...` tests (above) use hand-rolled `AlwaysErr`/`AlwaysOk` `SmsProvider` impls that return a chosen `ProviderError` directly — real coverage of the state-machine reaction, never of the transport failure itself. This suite instead talks to Orange through the real `sms_provider_orange_cm::OrangeCmProvider` and to MTN through the real `sms_provider_mtn::MtnAggregatorProvider` (#61), and "kills" Orange by **dropping a real, running `sms_fake_orange::FakeOrange` HTTP server** — the very next submit gets a genuine OS-level `ECONNREFUSED` through `reqwest`, exercising `classify_transport_error`'s real connect-vs-read distinction rather than asserting it separately in isolation (as `sms-provider-orange-cm`'s own `a_connect_refusal_is_still_unavailable` already does).

**Reviving Orange on the exact same port needed `SO_REUSEADDR`, and this was verified empirically before writing any test code, not assumed from platform docs.** `OrangeCmProvider::base_url` is fixed at construction, exactly like every other adapter in this workspace — so "Orange comes back" has to mean a *new* listener on the *same* address the already-constructed adapter still points at, not a second adapter instance. A throwaway Rust program (kept out of this repo) reproduced the actual failure mode first: bind an ephemeral port, complete one real HTTP round trip through it (so the client side leaves a `TIME_WAIT` entry), drop the listener, then a plain `std::net::TcpListener::bind` on that same port fails `EADDRINUSE` — even though *nothing* is currently listening there — because `TIME_WAIT` blocks a fresh *listening* bind on that port regardless of remote endpoint, on both macOS and (per general POSIX socket behaviour) Linux. A socket built via `socket2` with `SO_REUSEADDR` set before `bind()` rebinds immediately. `backends/crates/sms-worker/tests/kill_orange_gate_live_postgres.rs::reuseaddr_listener` is the resulting helper — a new `socket2` dev-dependency (already resolved transitively at 0.6.5 via `tokio`, so this names it directly rather than adding a second version to the tree). `wiremock`'s own `run_server` (`hyper.rs`) already calls `listener.set_nonblocking(true)` itself before wrapping the std listener, so the helper doesn't need to.

**"Nothing double-sends" is checked against the providers' own request logs — `FakeOrange::ledger()` for Orange, `MockServer::received_requests()` for MTN — never against this system's `Message` rows,** matching the issue's own explicit instruction: the database records what this system *believes* it did; a provider's own ledger records what it actually received. Since Orange is genuinely unreachable throughout the outage (nothing ever listens on that port), its own ledger staying at exactly the pre-kill baseline count (not growing at all) is a stronger proof than counting `Message.attempts` could ever be — it proves no connection ever *reached* Orange's request handler during the outage, not merely that this system's own bookkeeping says so.

**One scenario, not four separate tests — the four clauses are properties of one realistic timeline (healthy → killed → revived), not four independent mechanisms.** `orange_outage_fails_over_mtn_stays_up_nothing_double_sends_and_the_breaker_reopens` walks phase by phase (baseline, outage, recovery), each phase's own helper function (`run_baseline_phase`/`run_outage_phase`/`run_recovery_phase`, split out for `clippy::too_many_lines`, the same reason `dispatch_live_postgres.rs`'s own `seed_one_active_provider` is pulled to module scope) asserting the clauses reachable from that phase. Fixture: an operator-scoped Orange route at priority 1000, a wildcard (no `matchOperator`) MTN route at priority 500 — the wildcard is what makes Orange-destined failover to MTN possible at all, and also what makes MTN-destined traffic never even consider Orange, the strongest available form of "unaffected" a fixture can express directly through the real routing engine rather than asserted separately.

**All four guard-failure proofs were run for real, against production code, output captured, then reverted — house standard, and for a gate it matters more than usual.** Each sabotage was a single, clearly-labelled `// TEMPORARY SABOTAGE ... DO NOT COMMIT` edit to the exact mechanism the clause depends on, applied to `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/dispatch.rs` or `routing.rs`, verified to reproduce a real failure, then `git checkout --`'d before moving to the next:

- **Clause 1 (MTN unaffected)**: `resolve_provider`'s adapter lookup temporarily forced to always resolve whichever `Provider` row has `kind == orange_cm_http`, regardless of the message's own routed provider — simulating a regression where adapter resolution stops respecting the routing decision. Failed with `left: queued / right: submitted` on an MTN-destined message (it never reached `submitted` because it was silently resubmitted through the dead Orange adapter instead).
- **Clause 2 (Orange fails over cleanly)**: `handle_submit_error`'s `should_attempt_failover` temporarily forced to `false` — failover disabled outright. Failed with the identical `left: queued / right: submitted` shape, on an Orange-destined message this time (it just backed off the same, still-dead provider forever instead of rerouting).
- **Clause 3 (nothing double-sends)**: `write_submitted` temporarily left `state` untouched (`None`, this framework's own "field not present" convention) while also forcing `leaseUntil` into the past — simulating a successful submit that never marks itself terminal before its lease looks abandoned. This is the one guard-failure result worth reading carefully: it did **not** fail at the double-send-specific ledger assertions deep in the outage/recovery phases — it failed immediately, at the suite's own pre-outage baseline sanity check (`sanity: Orange must be genuinely healthy before this gate kills it`, `left: routed / right: submitted`), because a submit that never marks itself done is indistinguishable from a broken send at any point downstream. That is a *stronger* result than the alternative, not a weaker one — worth recording explicitly per this file's own "if a clause turns out to pass even with its mechanism broken, say so" standard: here it did not pass, it failed even earlier than intended, which means the property is foundational enough that it's caught before the code ever reaches the scenario purpose-built to check it.
- **Clause 4 (the breaker reopens)**: `routing.rs::convert_provider`'s `circuit_open` computation temporarily changed from `row.circuitOpenUntil.is_some_and(|until| until > now)` to `row.circuitOpenUntil.is_some()` — ignoring expiry entirely, so a circuit that ever opened never closes again regardless of how much time (real or forced) has passed. Failed with `left: Some("<mtn-provider-id>") / right: Some("<orange-provider-id>")` on a post-recovery message — it stayed routed through MTN instead of returning to Orange, even after the suite forced the cool-down into the past through a real delegate write (see below).

**Forcing the circuit's cool-down forward uses the same "force it for a faster test" technique this codebase already uses elsewhere** (the `#36` runbook's own `UPDATE messages SET lease_until = ...`, `chaos_live_postgres.rs`'s `force_lease_past`) — a real `UpdateProviderInput { circuitOpenUntil: Some(Some(past_time)), .. }.if_match(version)` delegate call (R1), not raw SQL, setting the value into the past rather than to `None` so the exact same `> now` comparison a real 60 seconds elapsing would exercise gets exercised here too.

**No new external dependency beyond `socket2`, already resolved transitively.** `cargo deny check`, `./ci/assert-no-raw-sqlx.sh`, and the full workspace `cargo check`/`cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings` all stayed clean through this change — confirmed by running them, not assumed.

## Milestone 6 started: observability and alerting (#70, #71) — landed 2026-08-11

Two stories, one PR, deliberately — #70 names five alert conditions, and every one of them needs a metric #71 was the ticket to build; #70 without #71 would be alerting on nothing, #71 without #70 would be instrumentation nobody consumes.

**Backend: Prometheus text exposition (`backends/crates/sms-metrics`, a new pure library crate below both `sms-api` and `sms-worker`), not OTLP.** No collector infrastructure exists anywhere in `deploy/`, and a pull-based `/metrics` needed nothing new to receive it. The `prometheus` crate, `=0.14`, `default-features = false` (drops the `protobuf` feature — the older binary scrape format nothing here speaks; `TextEncoder`/`Registry`/`*Vec` need none of it), Apache-2.0, checked against `deny.toml`'s allowed-license list and against `cargo deny check` directly (`advisories ok, bans ok, licenses ok, sources ok`, confirmed by running it, not assumed from the license string alone).

**Every metric's actual writer, and why:**

- `sms_sm001_total{entity,from_state,to_state}` — incremented inside `backends/crates/sms-api/src/errors.rs::map_database_error`, the one place this workspace already translates a raw `SM001` into something a caller can act on. Labels are parsed out of the guard trigger's own `RAISE EXCEPTION` text (`illegal <entity> transition <from> -> <to> on <id>`) by `sms_metrics::record_sm001` — matching §9.1's own "SM001 rejection count by from/to pair" line, not a plain unlabelled counter.
- `sms_worker_singleton_lease_held{role}` — `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/lib.rs::run_singleton`, the only writer: `1` the instant a lease is acquired, `0` the instant this process is standing by, released, or fails to attempt the lock.
- `sms_dispatch_in_flight_submits{provider}` — `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/dispatch.rs::submit_one`, incremented immediately before `SmsProvider::submit` and decremented immediately after.
- `sms_webhook_outbox_oldest_undelivered_age_seconds` — `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/drain.rs::tick`, reusing the exact `oldest_undelivered_age` that module's own doc already said exists "so alerting could reach it."
- `sms_event_outbox_poison_rows` — `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/jobs/reap_outbox.rs::ReapOutbox::run_at`, reusing `alert_poison_rows`'s own returned count, the same reuse principle.

**The absent-vs-zero problem (#70's own "subtlest requirement") is solved structurally, not with extra bookkeeping.** `run_singleton` only ever calls a role's real body (`run(role, ...)`) from inside the branch that already holds the lease — so `drain::tick` and `dispatch::submit_one` are *only ever reachable* on the process currently in charge. A standby process never touches those two gauges at all, so they are genuinely *absent* from its own `/metrics`, not present-and-zero; only `sms_worker_singleton_lease_held` itself is deliberately written on every outcome (including standing-by, as `0`), because that's the one gauge #70 needs a process to keep reporting even while it isn't in charge. `deploy/prometheus/alerts.yml`'s singleton-unheld rules check both `sum(...) == 0` **and** `absent(...)`, because only one of the two is true depending on whether the role is merely unheld or nobody is even configured to attempt it.

**Two real, latent bugs found wiring the SM001 counter through `sms-worker`'s write sites, not invented.** `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/jobs.rs::swallow_stale_write` and `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/jobs/expire_stale.rs::expire_matching` both had a `CratestackError::Conflict(reason)` match arm whose own comment claimed it meant "the message moved on before this write landed" — but nothing on either write path ever produced `Conflict` without going through `map_database_error` first, and neither call site did. That made the arm dead code, and — the actual bug — naively fixing SM001 counting by mapping every error *before* the match (the first attempt) would have made a **genuine** SM001 fall into that same arm and be silently swallowed as a harmless race, exactly the failure mode #70 exists to make loud. `backends/crates/sms-api/src/dlr.rs::ingest_one` had the identical shape, found first, live: its own `Err(CratestackError::Conflict(reason))` arm was equally unreachable before this PR. Fixed the same way in all three: check `sms_api::is_illegal_transition` against the *raw*, unmapped error first (it reads `error.db_sqlstate()` directly, no mapping needed), and only *then* call `map_database_error` (which also records the metric) on the confirmed-illegal case, propagating it loudly rather than swallowing it. A genuine version-race (`PreconditionFailed`) is still swallowed as before.
