**`/metrics` is a second, standalone listener on both binaries — never merged into `sms-gateway`'s public router.** `deploy/Caddyfile`'s `reverse_proxy sms-gateway:8080` is a blanket, no-path-allowlist proxy (confirmed by reading the file, not assumed) — anything mounted on that router is public the moment it exists. `sms_api::metrics::router()` binds to its own address instead (`--metrics-listen`, default `127.0.0.1:9090` on the gateway, `127.0.0.1:9091` on the worker — loopback by default, `0.0.0.0` only inside the container via each `Dockerfile`'s own `ENV` override, same pattern `SMS_LISTEN_ADDR` already used), never published to the host and never a path Caddy's config names. `backends/apps/sms-worker` had **no HTTP surface at all before this PR** ("six poll-loop roles, never a listener" — `main.rs`'s own module doc, now corrected) — it gained `axum` as a real dependency (not dev-only) specifically for this, reusing `sms_api::metrics::router()` directly rather than a second near-identical implementation.

**Correlation is `Message.id` across processes, `cratestack_request_id` within one.** `cratestack-macros` already logs `cratestack_request_id = ctx.request_id().unwrap_or("")` on every generated CRUD/procedure route — found live, by reading the vendored macro source, that nothing in this deployment had ever called `CratestackContext::with_request_id`, so that field had been empty on every log line since the router first existed. `backends/crates/sms-api/src/auth.rs::GatewayAuth::authenticate` now honours an inbound `X-Request-Id` verbatim or mints a fresh one — the one place a `CratestackContext` is constructed per HTTP request. That ties together every `cratestack_*` log line for one request, within `sms-gateway` — it does **not** cross into `sms-worker` or back through a DLR, which are separate processes (or, for the DLR case, an unauthenticated route with no bearer token and therefore no per-request `CratestackContext` at all — `dlr.rs`'s own module doc). `Message.id` is the join key across that boundary instead: `procedures.rs::send`, `dispatch.rs::submit_one`, and `dlr.rs::ingest_one` each log a `message_id`-carrying event at `info`. `docs/runbooks/alerting.adoc`'s "Correlating a message end to end" section is explicit that this is grep-able log correlation, not OpenTelemetry-shaped distributed tracing — no span tree, no exporter, three log lines sharing one field.

**"Alerting" stops at the rules evaluating — no Alertmanager, no receiver, nothing in this tree can page anyone.** `deploy/prometheus/alerts.yml` holds the five rules; a real `prometheus` service in `deploy/docker-compose.yml` (stood up unconditionally, not behind a compose profile — this repo's own delivery convention is to build a thing and make it live) evaluates them against `sms-gateway:9090`/`sms-worker:9091` and shows firing state on its own `/alerts` page. Wiring a receiver on top is left to whoever operates a real deployment. §9.1 of the design doc is corrected to match what actually shipped (five real metrics/alerts; everything else in the original prose — submit rate/latency, delivery rate by provider×operator, time-to-delivery percentiles, provider balance, DLR silence, webhook dead-letter rate — is still aspirational) rather than left silently stale, and the design doc's own 60s outbox-staleness figure is flagged as not matching `drain.rs`'s real, already-implemented 2-minute `STALLED_THRESHOLD` — the alert rule matches the code that actually runs.

**The Helm chart (`deploy/charts/vsms/values.yaml`) does not get a `/metrics` Service or ServiceMonitor in this PR** — a deliberate, named scope cut, not an oversight: Docker Compose is the deployment path this repo's own §9.2 documents as real ("Kubernetes only when you have a reason that isn't résumé-driven"), and a ServiceMonitor assumes a Prometheus Operator install this chart doesn't otherwise depend on. The chart's own stale "sms-worker has no HTTP surface" comments are corrected regardless, since this PR's own code changes made them false.

**One metric proven to fail its own guard, live, not just reasoned about.** `backends/crates/sms-api/src/errors.rs::sm001_increments_the_labelled_prometheus_counter` was deliberately broken (the `sms_metrics::record_sm001` call commented out of `map_database_error`) and confirmed to fail with `assertion left == right failed: map_database_error must record exactly one SM001 observation / left: 0 / right: 1` before being restored. `backends/crates/sms-worker/tests/live_postgres.rs::run_singleton_reports_held_then_released_on_the_lease_gauge` drives the real `run_singleton` (not `RoleLease` directly) against a live Postgres and asserts the gauge reads `1` while held and `0` again after a graceful shutdown releases it — proof the absent-vs-zero design in `backends/crates/sms-metrics`'s own module doc is real, not just documented.

## Milestone 6 started: retention purge (#67) — decision #5 resolved

**Decision #5 (10-year traffic retention vs 90-day minimisation) was resolved by the maintainer on 2026-08-11: 90-day minimisation, no split ledger.** §10's own split-ledger proposal — a second, parallel ten-year traffic-metadata table alongside a 90-day content/MSISDN purge — was a real, considered option, not a strawman, and is now a documented alternative that was considered and not taken, not the plan of record. vsms purges *content and plaintext MSISDN both* at 90 days and carries no second, longer-horizon ledger anywhere in `schema.cstack`. Long-horizon retention, if it is ever required, is an infrastructure concern for whoever operates the deployment (backup/archival retention configured outside this application), and nothing in this repository fails if nobody configures that — see issue #5's own resolution comment for the tradeoff stated in those terms, and `docs/architecture.md` §10 (updated in the same PR). This unblocked #67, and shrank its scope: implement the 90-day purge, not the split ledger the issue's own text originally described.

`#67` landed as `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/jobs/purge_retention.rs`, the `purge_retention` job — one of the eight §7.5 job kinds `#35`'s own status paragraph named but didn't build, now down to six. Registered in `jobs::default_registry()`, scheduled daily (§7.5's own cadence) in `scheduler::schedule()`, alongside `expire_stale` and `reap_outbox`.

**What gets purged, and the reasoning per column — read the job's own module doc for the full version, this is the summary:**

- **Purged**: `msisdn` (overwritten with a fixed placeholder, not nulled — see below), `body`, `clientRef`, `idempotencyKey` (a verbatim copy of `clientRef` at creation, per `procedures.rs` — purging one and not the other would purge nothing), `stateReason` (free text straight from a provider's API response, via `dispatch.rs`'s `classify()`, not a closed vsms-authored set).
- **Survives, deliberately**: `msisdnHash` (required by the decision's own text — "is what any post-purge correlation runs on"), `bodyHash` (also `NOT NULL`; write-only per `#134`'s own audit, left as a known, considered gap rather than solved here), `senderIdValue` (identifies the sending brand, not the recipient; §2.5's own "linkage" argument needed the plaintext `msisdn` to be meaningful, and that's gone).
- Only **terminal** `Message` rows (`delivered`/`failed`/`expired`/`rejected`/`cancelled` — §7.4's own terminal set) past 90 days are touched. A non-terminal row past 90 days would mean something else is already broken (§7.4's expiry machinery guarantees termination well inside 90 days), so this job trusts that guarantee rather than forcing a still-live row over it.
- `DeliveryReceipt` gets deleted, not redacted — the model carries no correlation-key column worth preserving, and this PR is the first thing that has ever removed one (§2.5 used to call receipts "append-only"; that line is now qualified). Eligibility is the receipt's own `receivedAt`, independent of its parent message's age.

**`Message.msisdn` stays `NOT NULL` — it is overwritten with a fixed 13-character placeholder (`"purged-msisdn"`), not nulled, and this was a deliberate rejection of the more "obvious" fix.** Making the column nullable was considered and rejected: the only two production readers of `Message.msisdn` (`dispatch.rs`'s submit path, `webhooks.rs`'s `Message.created`/`updated` subscriber) structurally can never see a message old enough to be purged — dispatch only touches `accepted`/`queued`/`routed`/`undelivered` rows, and the subscriber fires synchronously inside the mutation that created the row. Making the type `Option<String>` would have forced `.expect()`-shaped defensive handling into two heavily-tested, already-correct hot paths for a branch that can never actually be hit, for no privacy benefit the placeholder doesn't already give (`UpdateMessageInput::validate()` still runs `@length(min: 12, max: 15)` against the placeholder on every write — update-input validators treat every field as present-or-absent, not nullable-or-not, so the placeholder has to satisfy the same length bound a real MSISDN would).

**A `purgedAt DateTime?` column was added to `Message` — a real DDL change, done wholesale per this file's own regeneration workflow, verified against a real Postgres before being trusted.** Without a dedicated marker, "purged" and "never had a body" are indistinguishable, and relying on `body IS NULL` as an implicit signal is exactly the kind of silent contract this codebase keeps getting bitten by — nothing else in this codebase nulls `body` today (the #183 redact-at-terminal-state idea was built and rejected, see §2.5), but that could change. `purgedAt` is also the job's own idempotency guard (`purgedAt IS NULL` is part of the candidate filter). `cratestack --version` matched the pin (`0.7.10` = `0.7.10`) at the time, so the regeneration was trusted without the CLI-skew workaround this file's own "Regenerating migrations" section warns about — confirmed, not assumed. `cratestack migrate diff`'s output diffed to exactly one line against the committed `0001_init` (`purged_at TIMESTAMPTZ,`), and `0002_bootstrap` gained two hand-written indexes (`messages_purge_idx`, a partial index on `(created_at) WHERE purged_at IS NULL AND state IN (...)`; `receipts_received_at_idx` on `delivery_receipts (received_at)`) added to §2.10 of the doc first, then regenerated — never hand-edited into the migration file directly. Both migrations were applied to a real, disposable Postgres and `ci/test-state-machine.sql` passed before either was trusted.

**`DeliveryReceipt` gained its first-ever `delete` capability — the tenth instance of this file's own repeated `hasRole('system')`-shaped gap, but on the `delete` action rather than `list`/`detail`, and an absent clause rather than a broken one.** The model had no `@@allow("delete", ...)` at all before this PR; deny-by-default made that indistinguishable from "deliberately unsupported" until this job actually needed it. `schema.cstack` only, no DDL consequence — confirmed the same way every prior instance in this file was, a byte-identical `cratestack migrate diff` before and after the policy-only change.

**Seeding a genuinely old dataset — the acceptance criterion this issue names explicitly, and the part most likely to be faked.** `Message.createdAt` and `DeliveryReceipt.receivedAt` both carry `@default(dbgenerated())`, excluding them from their own create input (§2.0) — but they are not symmetric past that point. `Message.createdAt` genuinely can be backdated through a delegate: a `@default` excludes a field from `CreateXInput` but not `UpdateXInput` (this file's own framework-constraints table already says so), and — unlike `updatedAt` — no trigger touches `created_at` on write, confirmed by reading `touch_updated_at` itself rather than assumed from the mixin's name. So `backends/crates/sms-worker/tests/purge_retention_live_postgres.rs` seeds a message, drives it to `cancelled` (a direct, legal `accepted -> cancelled` edge), and backdates `createdAt` to a real past `DateTime<Utc>` in the same CAS write — genuinely old data in the database, not a virtual clock. `DeliveryReceipt.receivedAt` has no such seam at all: the model has no `@@allow("update", ...)` clause whatsoever (append-only by design — `dlr.rs` only ever `.create()`s one), so its own tests fall back to the virtual-`cutoff` trick `reap_outbox_live_postgres.rs` already established for exactly this shape of column (framework-stamped, no delegate seam to backdate through) — shift the `cutoff` argument forward instead of the row's own timestamp.

Five boundary points proven against real data, not inferred: comfortably past, just past, exactly at (the `<=` cutoff comparison is inclusive — a message created *exactly* 90 days before the run is purged), just inside, comfortably inside. Verified to actually catch a regression, not just pass by construction: temporarily loosening `RETENTION` from 90 days to 1 hour reproduced a real failure (`just_inside: purgedAt.is_some() expected false ... left: true`) before being restored.

Explicitly out of scope, matching this milestone's own precedent for naming a scope cut rather than leaving it implicit: `Job` (`@@retain(days: 14)`) and `WebhookAttempt` (`@@retain(days: 30)`) also carry `@@retain` in `schema.cstack`, but §7.5's own `purge_retention` table row only ever specified "null `body`/`msisdn` past 90 days, delete receipts past 90" — `Job` and `WebhookAttempt` enforcement, if wanted, is a separate decision this PR doesn't make.

**Found in the coordinator's review of this PR, not by its own tests: a purge would have re-fired a customer's own webhook about a message reported on three months earlier.** The chain, real and confirmed against the tree, not hypothetical: `Message` carries `@@emit(created, updated)`; `purge_messages`'s own `.update()` is a real delegate write — R1 gives no other option; `backends/apps/sms-worker`'s `main` registers `backends/crates/sms-api/src/webhooks.rs`'s subscribers unconditionally, regardless of `--roles`, per that module's own documented reason (an unregistered writer *loses* events, not merely stalls them); and `message_event_type` maps four of this job's five terminal candidate states (`delivered`/`failed`/`expired`/`cancelled` — every one but `rejected`) to a catalogued event. Un-guarded, every purge therefore attempted to enqueue — and `hooks` would then have signed and POSTed — a live webhook carrying `to: "purged-msisdn"` and an already-nulled `clientRef`. `webhook_attempts_dedupe` (`endpoint_id`, `aggregate_id`, `event_type`) happened to swallow the common case, where an attempt from the message's original delivery already exists to collide with — a coincidence of an unrelated unique index, not a guard, and it does not cover an endpoint registered *after* the original event.

Fixed at the subscriber, not the write site (there is no seam at the write site to suppress an emit from — R1 forbids going around the delegate): `enqueue_message_webhook_attempts` (`backends/crates/sms-api/src/webhooks.rs`) now returns `Ok(())` immediately on `message.purgedAt.is_some()`, before it ever reads `msisdn` for the payload. Also corrected a wrong claim the first version of `purge_retention.rs`'s own module doc made while justifying `msisdn`'s `NOT NULL`-placeholder design: it asserted `webhooks.rs`'s subscriber "structurally can never see" a purged message, true of `dispatch.rs` and false of `webhooks.rs` — the doc now records why that reasoning was wrong, not just the corrected conclusion, matching this file's own convention of recording found mistakes rather than silently fixing them. `backends/crates/sms-worker/tests/purge_retention_live_postgres.rs`'s `a_purge_never_re_fires_a_webhook_to_a_registered_endpoint` deliberately registers the `WebhookEndpoint` *after* driving the message to `delivered`, the one case dedupe cannot mask, and was confirmed to actually fail without the fix (`left: 1, right: 0` — a real `WebhookAttempt` row) before being trusted.

## Milestone 4: `@version`/ETag/If-Match threaded through operator-editable models (#59) — landed 2026-08-11

`#59`'s own example ("two operators editing the same route") didn't compile against the schema as written: only `Message`, `Job`, `WebhookAttempt` carried `@version` before this PR — the three models the *worker* claims via CAS, not the ones a human edits. `Route` (the ticket's own example) had no `@version` at all. Two honest options existed: thread ETag/If-Match only for the three already-versioned models (cheap, leaves the ticket's actual motivating scenario unsolved), or add `@version` to the models a human actually edits from the console (a real schema change, but the one that makes the ticket's own stated goal true).

**Took the second option, and went through every model's `@@allow("update", ...)` clause rather than trusting the ticket's own illustrative list.** Every model whose update policy admits at least one human role (`hasRole('owner'|'admin'|'operator'|'developer')`) gained `@version`: `App`, `AppClient`, `SenderId`, `SenderIdRegistration`, `Provider`, `Route`, `OperatorPrefixRule`, `WebhookEndpoint`, `User`, `Role` — ten models, not the seven or eight the ticket's own prose named (`User`/`Role` — RBAC/account administration — weren't mentioned there at all, but their update policies are exactly as human-gated as `Provider`'s, and a stale-role-reassignment race is the textbook version of this ticket's own worked example). Left alone, deliberately: the three already-versioned worker-claimed models (no human update policy to begin with), and every model whose update policy is `hasRole('system')`-only or absent entirely (`OauthClient`, `OauthSigningKey`, `Message`, `MessagePart`, `Job`, `WebhookAttempt`, `DeliveryReceipt`, `ClientAssertion`, `OptOut`) — nothing for a human to race there.

**Blast radius was smaller than it looked, and the search for it is worth recording.** `grep`ing for every `Update<Model>Input` construction across `crates/`/`app/` found real internal `.update()` call sites on exactly two of the ten: `Provider` (three sites: `backends/crates/sms-api/examples/send_test_message.rs`, `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/main.rs`'s `seed-provider` command) and `WebhookEndpoint` (`backends/crates/sms-api/src/procedures.rs`'s `rotateWebhookSecret`/`replayWebhookAttempt`, `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/hooks.rs`'s three endpoint-bookkeeping writes) and `SenderId` (one, inside `send_test_message.rs`'s own sender-activation helper). The other seven models — `App`, `Route`, `OperatorPrefixRule`, `SenderIdRegistration`, `User`, `Role` — have **zero** internal `.update()` call sites anywhere in this codebase, not even in a test fixture: every write to them today happens exclusively through the generated REST route, which needs no application-code change at all. Seventeen files in total needed a `.if_match(...)` added once live-test fixtures were counted too (every one of those two models' own `*_live_postgres.rs` seed helpers that activate a `Provider`/`SenderId` row after creating it).

**The framework fact this PR corrects, found live, not assumed: `if_match` is a runtime requirement, not a compile-time one.** This file's own "Verified toolchain API" comment (`// required on @version models`) reads as though a compile error results from omitting it — checked directly against `cratestack-sqlx-0.7.10/src/query/write/update.rs`: `if_match` is a plain `Option<i64>` builder field, defaulting to `None`; `run`/`run_in_tx` compiles fine without it and instead returns `Err(CratestackError::PreconditionFailed("If-Match header required for versioned model"))` the first time the write actually executes. Proof this isn't hypothetical: `cargo check --workspace --all-targets` stayed fully green through the entire `@version` addition, on every one of the seventeen files that needed a real `.if_match(...)` fix — the compiler cannot see this gap, the same way it cannot see a misspelled `@@allow` action (§2.0's own already-documented blind spot, now joined by a second one of the identical shape). The only way to find every real call site was `grep`ing for the generated `Update<Model>Input` type names and reading each one by hand, then confirming with the live suites — never trust `cargo check` alone here.

**The REST ETag/If-Match wiring itself needed zero application code.** Read directly, not assumed: `cratestack-macros-0.7.10/src/axum/model/prep/etag.rs` generates, per model, entirely from whether it has a `@version` field — a `PATCH` handler that parses `If-Match` into `Option<i64>` (a missing header is `CratestackError::PreconditionFailed("If-Match header required")`, matching the builder-level message above almost verbatim), calls `.if_match(version)` on the same `UpdateRecordSet` this file's own delegate code calls, and stamps the response `ETag` header from the returned row's `version` on success; a `GET`/detail handler stamps the same header from whatever it read. `CratestackError::PreconditionFailed` maps to HTTP `412`, code `"PRECONDITION_FAILED"` (`cratestack-core-0.7.10/src/error.rs`, read directly). So the ten new `@version` fields are the entire "put it in the data layer once" mechanism for the REST surface — nothing in `backends/crates/sms-api/src/router.rs` needed to change for this ticket at all.

**A structural finding that changed the shape of the proof this ticket needed: `GatewayAuth::authenticate` (`backends/crates/sms-api/src/auth.rs`) hardcodes `role: "app"` for every real token this deployment ever mints — not read from any JWT claim.** `backends/crates/sms-auth/tests/rbac_layer2_live_postgres.rs`'s own module doc already said "no live success case" exists for `PATCH /providers/{id}` because no human-login flow can mint an `owner`/`admin`/`operator`-role token; reading `authenticate`'s body line by line resolves *why* more strongly than that note states it: `role` is a literal constant in the function, not derived from `scope`/`perms`/anything else the token carries, so even the hand-signed-stand-in-token technique `m1_acceptance_gate_live_postgres.rs`'s `sign_developer_stand_in_token` uses to reach a Layer-2 *denial* cannot reach a Layer-1 *allow* here either — forging claims changes what a real token would have carried into `GatewayAuth`, not what `GatewayAuth` derives from it. Consequence: **no write route any of #59's ten models exposes is reachable over real HTTP by any token this deployment can currently issue, full stop** — including this console's own service-account credential (`frontends/packages/gateway/src/token.ts`), which is exactly such a real, `kind: "app"` token. This is not a gap #59 introduced; it already existed for every one of these models' write policies. It does mean the live end-to-end proof for this ticket has to live one layer down.

`backends/crates/sms-api/tests/if_match_live_postgres.rs` is that proof: real Postgres, `Provider` (the ticket's own PATCH-route anchor, `router::PROVIDER_WRITE_ROUTES`), the real generated delegate call the axum handler makes internally (`db.provider().update(id).set(...).if_match(v).run(ctx)`), not a hand-rolled reimplementation. Three tests: two operators loading the same row, one saving successfully, the second's now-stale write rejected with a genuine `CratestackError::PreconditionFailed` (asserted directly against `.status_code().as_u16() == 412` and `.code() == "PRECONDITION_FAILED"` — the framework's own HTTP-shape fact, not this crate's own `errors.rs`, which only maps *database*-level SQLSTATEs and never touches a builder-level `PreconditionFailed` at all) and the row left holding operator A's write, never B's; a correct `if_match` in isolation; omitting `if_match` entirely on a versioned model. Verified to actually fail, not just pass by construction: temporarily pointing operator B's write at the *fresh* version instead of the stale one reproduced a real assertion panic naming the exact silent-overwrite this ticket exists to prevent — the panic message printed back the row with `displayName: "Renamed by operator B"` and `version: 2`, i.e. B's write had gone through and clobbered A's, exactly what a genuine `if_match` bug would produce — restored afterward, all three tests passing again.

**The web half (`frontends/packages/gateway`) hits the identical structural wall — the console's own credential cannot call any of these routes for real either — so its own proof is mechanism-level, not a live round trip.** `frontends/packages/gateway/src/rest.ts` is the new generic seam: `fetchWithEtag`/`updateWithIfMatch`, following `messages.ts`'s existing `getJson` shape (Bearer + one 401 retry) but capturing the response `ETag` header on the way in and sending it back as `If-Match` on the way out — the one piece `messages.ts` never needed, since `Message` has no human edit action. `errors.ts` already mapped `412 → CONFLICT` before this PR (its own module doc names the mapping, clearly written in anticipation of this ticket); `isStaleWriteError` is the one new addition — checks `httpStatus === 412` so a screen can show "someone else changed this, reload" instead of a generic conflict toast, distinct from a duplicate-key `409` landing in the same `trpcCode` bucket. `frontends/packages/gateway/src/rest.test.ts` proves the mechanism against a fake upstream (an injected fetcher, the same dependency-injection shape `message-stream.ts`'s own `fetchWindow` already uses) — real header parsing, real `If-Match` attachment, real status-based branching, `token.ts` mocked since key-loading has nothing to do with what this file tests. `vitest.config.ts` gained a real (if fake) `SMS_API_URL` for the first time, needed because `restUrl()` resolves every path against it before the injected fetcher ever runs.

**Migrations were regenerated wholesale, following this file's own documented workflow, and it caught a real trap before it reached CI.** `cratestack --version` matched the pin exactly (`0.7.10` = `0.7.10`, corrected AGENTS.md's own stale claim about this — see the "Regenerating migrations" section above) so regeneration was safe for the first time in this repo's history; the diff against the unmodified schema was byte-for-byte the ten new `version BIGINT NOT NULL` columns and nothing else. Applying the regenerated `0002_bootstrap` to a real scratch Postgres immediately failed: `null value in column "version" of relation "operator_prefix_rules" violates not-null constraint` — §2.10's hand-written seed `INSERT INTO operator_prefix_rules` (14 rows) predates `@version` existing on that model and, being raw SQL rather than a CrateStack `create()` call, never got the framework's own server-side `version = 0` seed (`cratestack-sqlx-0.7.10/src/query/write/create_exec.rs`'s `create_record_with_executor`, which the bootstrap SQL bypasses entirely). Fixed by adding `version` (literal `0`) to every row in `docs/architecture.md` §2.10's own seed table and regenerating; `ci/test-state-machine.sql`'s own hand-written `apps`/`webhook_endpoints` fixture inserts needed the identical fix for the identical reason. Both migration paths — `ci/apply-migrations.sh` and the production `backends/apps/sms-migrate` binary — were verified against a real, freshly created Postgres before trusting the regeneration, and `ci/test-state-machine.sql`'s full assertion suite (and `just parity`) passed against the result. `sdks/rust/vsms-sdk-rust/schema.cstack` was re-vendored (`vendor-schema.sh`) and its own `cargo check --all-targets` confirmed clean — the exact step whose omission made #185 red.

Explicit scope cuts, named rather than left implicit: no edit screens (#52–#58's own job, unblocked by this PR rather than built by it); no human-login `AuthProvider` (the structural gap this PR's own finding sharpens, not closes — tracked the same way #24/#25 already tracked it); `WebhookEndpoint`'s hot-path worker writes keep their existing best-effort semantics (a detected-but-still-dropped race, not a CAS-retry loop) rather than being redesigned as part of a ticket about console edits.

## Milestone 4: the Jobs and Workers screens landed (#56/#57) — the diagnostic core, alongside #50

`docs/roadmap.md` names #50 (messages detail), #56, and #57 as "the diagnostic core" of M4's own gate — *"an operator can diagnose a failed message without touching SQL."* #56/#57 landed together, in one PR, because they share a data layer the issue text itself points at: `pg_locks` for #57, `Job` for #56, both read through the same admin console credential.

**#57's data has no delegate — `pg_locks` isn't a schema model** — the same shape this file's own R1 exceptions table already has six entries for (`drain.rs`, `reap_outbox.rs`, the readiness probe, `lease.rs` itself, migrations, `LISTEN`/`NOTIFY`). `backends/crates/sms-api/src/worker_locks.rs` is the seventh: a `pg_locks` ⋈ `pg_stat_activity` query, keyed on the same `(classid, objid)` `lease.rs` takes locks under. Verified live, not assumed, against a real Postgres 16 with a real held lease: a granted two-key advisory lock is exactly one row (`objsubid = 2`), and a losing `pg_try_advisory_lock` leaves **no row at all** — unlike the blocking form, there is nothing to queue. The consequence for #57's own framing ("two `dispatch` workers means a blocked Orange account"): Postgres's own exclusivity guarantee means `pg_locks` can *never* show two granted rows for the same role — if that ever happened for real, it would mean a bug bypassing `run_singleton`'s lease check entirely, not something this table could surface directly. The Workers screen states this plainly rather than implying it would catch that shape of bug.

To make "which node" answerable at all, `RoleLease::try_acquire` (`backends/crates/sms-worker/src/lease.rs`) now takes a `worker_id` and stamps it onto the lease connection's own `application_name` via `PgConnectOptions` — the dedicated, never-pooled connection this module's own doc already explains exists for nothing but holding one lock, so its `pg_stat_activity.application_name` is, for the lease's lifetime, that worker's identity. The `NS`/lock-key table itself moved to `sms_api::worker_roles` (a new, tiny, pure module) rather than staying duplicated: `sms-api` cannot depend on `sms-worker` (the dependency runs the other way), so the one fact both sides need — role name to `objid` — lives on the `sms-api` side of that one-directional edge, and `lease.rs` now looks it up there instead of keeping its own copy. `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/lease.rs`'s own test module cross-checks `sms_api::worker_roles::is_singleton` against `Role::cardinality` for all six roles, so the two tables can't quietly drift apart.

**#56's re-enqueue needed a new state-machine edge.** `dead -> pending` didn't exist — `dead` was a true dead end, on purpose (`0002_bootstrap`'s own comment). Added the same way #43 added the identical edge for `WebhookAttempt`: one row in `job_state_transitions` (§2.10, `0002_bootstrap` regenerated, `ci/test-state-machine.sql` gained a block walking a job to `dead` and back), a `requeueJob` procedure (`backends/crates/sms-api/src/procedures.rs`) that proposes only that edge and returns a named `409 Conflict` for anything else — `failed -> pending` already exists for `jobs::apply_failure`'s own automatic backoff, but `failed` is a same-tick transient state no operator poll can realistically observe, so `requeueJob` doesn't accept it as a starting state at all. `Job`'s own list/detail `@@allow` gained `auth().kind == "app"` (schema-policy-only, no DDL) — the same unscoped shape `SenderId`/`OptOut` already use, since `Job` has no `appId` to scope by the way `Message` does.

**That last point is the real finding of this PR, not a footnote: for `Job`, Layer 2 is the actual perimeter, not defense in depth.** Every prior Layer-2 example in this file (`PROVIDER_WRITE_ROUTES`, `sendMessage`) is provably redundant today, because `GatewayAuth` never mints anything but `role: "app"`/`"system"` and the underlying model's own `@@allow` already excludes `"app"`. `Job` breaks that pattern on purpose: its `@@allow` *does* admit `auth().kind == "app"`, so any provisioned app client — not just the admin console's own credential — passes Layer 1 and reaches the whole system's job backlog unless Layer 2 stops it. New scopes `job:read`/`job:enqueue`/`worker:read` (§5.2, the same literals `operator`'s own `perms` already used for the first two) close that: `router::JOB_READ_ROUTES` gates `GET /jobs`/`GET /jobs/{id}`, and `requeueJob`/`workerLocks` each call `require_permission` directly. Proven with a real, live, end-to-end HTTP success case — `backends/crates/sms-auth/tests/rbac_layer2_live_postgres.rs`'s new `job_read_route_succeeds_for_a_token_carrying_job_read` is the first test in that file to reach a genuine `200` through both layers with a real token, something `PROVIDER_WRITE_ROUTES`'s own tests there explicitly cannot do (that route's Layer 1 already excludes every issuable token). The admin console's own client needs the three new scopes granted at provisioning time — `scripts/demo.sh` and `docs/runbooks/getting-started.adoc` updated to request them, `@vsms/env`'s `SMS_CONSOLE_SCOPE` default extended to match.

Two Rust guard failures reproduced on purpose, not just asserted: `requeueJob`'s own `.if_match(existing.version)` removed entirely first — cratestack's own runtime refused the write outright (`PreconditionFailed("If-Match header required for versioned model")`), before any race could even matter; restored, then a *wrong* version substituted (`existing.version + 999`) to reach the actual race-losing path — `PreconditionFailed("version mismatch: expected 1002, found 3")`. Both restored, both confirmed passing again.

The admin console gained two screens (`frontends/apps/admin/app/jobs`, `frontends/apps/admin/app/workers`) — plain `refetchInterval` polling, not a live stream hub like the messages list (§6.5's live-list rules are specific to that flagship screen, and a six-role or job-backlog diagnostics table doesn't warrant a second server-side hub). `@vsms/ui`'s `status-tokens.ts` closed its own long-standing "Job states deliberately are NOT mapped here yet" gap — a `JobStatusPill` alongside `StatusPill`, sharing the same glyph geometry (`StateMarkFromMeta`, extracted from `StateMark` without changing its existing public API) but its own `JOB_STATUS_META`: a job's `failed` is retryable, not terminal, and is deliberately not styled the way a message's own terminal `failed` is.

## Milestone 4 started: the human login flow (#194)

`#97`/`#98` cut the authorization-code/human-login path from the OP because no admin console existed yet to need it — M4 is that console, so the cut came due. #194 closes it: real humans can now log in, hold a real role, and reach a real `hasRole('operator')`-gated policy.

**`authkestra-op` 0.3.3 does support `authorization_code` + PKCE, fully — read from the vendored source, not assumed.** `authkestra_op::handlers::authorize::handle_authorize` enforces S256-only PKCE (rejects `plain`, rejects a `code_challenge_method` with no `code_challenge`), exact `redirect_uri` matching, `response_type == "code"`, and grant-type admission; `authkestra_op::handlers::token::handle_authorization_code` enforces the matching verifier check (`sha256(code_verifier) == code_challenge`, base64url, `S256` only — anything else is a hard `server_error` rather than a silent bypass) and issues a real `id_token` when `openid` is in scope. All of this was already exercised by the library's own test suite (`test_missing_pkce_redirect_error`, `test_plain_pkce_redirect_error`, `test_successful_exchange_with_pkce`, `test_invalid_pkce_verifier`, ...) before this PR ever ran it for real. The `MachineOnlyOpStore` this codebase already assembled for #20 turns out to need **zero changes** for this: its authorization-code and refresh-token slots were always real, correct `MemoryStore<T>` — never exercised, because nothing ever mounted `/authorize` or a login route, not because they were placeholders. In-memory is the *right* choice for both, not a shortcut: this deployment runs exactly one `serve` process, an authorization code lives at most 60s, and a lost refresh token on restart just forces a re-login.

**What the library does *not* give you: a login form, or any way to establish the `Identity` `handle_authorize` requires before it will run.** That's the real design decision #194 had to make, and the issue explicitly left it open: authenticate humans against `authkestra-op` itself, or federate to an external OIDC IdP with this OP acting as a broker? **Chosen: local password authentication, entirely within this system, backed by Argon2id — not a federated external IdP.** Full reasoning is in `backends/crates/sms-auth/src/login.rs`'s own module doc; the short version is that federation needs a second, equally security-sensitive OIDC *client* implementation and an actual external IdP this deployment can point at (none decided — see the "Open questions" list below, unchanged by this PR), plus nothing in this sandbox to prove it against end to end the way this repo's fake-for-every-external-dependency convention (`sms-fake-orange`) demands. Local password auth needs nothing this deployment doesn't already have and is fully testable against a real Postgres with no external dependency.

**Say it as loudly in this file as the PR that introduced it says it in its own doc comments: this system now stores password hashes, which it did not before.** New security surface, not a footnote. `backends/crates/sms-auth/src/login.rs` — Argon2id (crate defaults: 19 MiB, 2 iterations, 1 lane), a fresh random salt per hash, and a **timing-safe "no such user" path**: `authenticate_user` always runs a real Argon2 verify, against a lazily-computed dummy hash when no matching active account exists, so "wrong password" and "no such account" cost the same wall-clock time and return the identical `LoginError::InvalidCredentials`. The password hash itself lives in a **new model, `UserCredential`, not a field on `User`** — because §2.0's own grammar table already answers the question of whether that's safe: **"No field-level read masking. Model-level access only."** `@sensitive` redacts audit snapshots, never an HTTP response body — a password hash on `User` would come back verbatim from `GET /users/{id}` to every role `User.read` already admits (owner/frontends/apps/admin/auditor). `UserCredential` is `hasRole('system')` on every action, the same isolation `OauthSigningKey.privateKeyPem` already uses for the OP's own signing key, and no console screen — present or future — may ever read or write it directly. This is a deliberate, flagged deviation from the issue's own "the schema is already shaped for this and needs no new models" framing: the field-masking constraint made the new model unavoidable, not optional.

**The real `authkestra-op` library shape ruled out baking role/perms into the access token at issuance, which §5.3's own worked example assumed.** Found live, not by reading the design doc: `handle_authorization_code` calls plain `TokenManager::issue_user_token`, never `issue_user_token_with_extra` — the only method that can stamp `extra` claims onto a token at mint time. Forking that handler to add one was considered and rejected: it would mean re-implementing PKCE/redirect-uri/client-binding validation alongside it, exactly the security-critical duplication this codebase avoids everywhere else (the jwks/discovery hand-rolling in `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/op.rs` is the precedent, and even those don't touch security-decision logic the way a token-issuance fork would). Instead `sms_api::auth::GatewayAuth`'s new human path (`authenticate_human`, `backends/crates/sms-api/src/auth.rs`) resolves role/perms with a per-request, 60s-TTL-cached `User`/`Role` lookup — the same cache shape `app_cache` already uses for `AppClient`. This is not a consolation: a role change or account deactivation now takes effect within one cache TTL, strictly *more* responsive than "up to 15 minutes" §5.3 originally described for the baked-in-at-issuance design.

**A human token's `aud` is real and must be checked — unlike a service-account token's self-referential one.** `GatewayAuth`'s existing `Validation.validate_aud = false` (§4.2's own reasoning: `aud == sub == client_id` for `client_credentials`, nothing fixed to check) still applies globally, since one `Validation` decodes both realms. The human-only audience check (`claims.aud == Some(human_client_id)`, `human_client_id` a `GatewayAuth::new` parameter, `sms_api::DEFAULT_CONSOLE_CLIENT_ID = "sms-console"`) is a manual, post-decode comparison inside `authenticate_human` — the exact "revisit this line before wiring `identity`-bearing tokens up to anything" the previous revision of this type's own doc comment flagged.

**`POST /login`, a raw axum route in `backends/apps/sms-gateway/src/login.rs`, is the login form's server-side leg** — not `GET /authorize` (never mounted; nothing needs it, since `sms-console` is the only OIDC client this deployment will ever have, and a spec-compliant redirect-to-a-hosted-login-page dance buys nothing a first-party BFF needs). It collapses "authenticate the human" and "run the real `handle_authorize`" into one call: `sms_auth::login::authenticate_user`, then a real `authkestra_op::handlers::authorize::handle_authorize` — unmodified library code enforcing every PKCE/redirect-uri/state property #194 requires — with the resulting `Identity`. `admin`'s own `POST /api/auth/login` route handler is the only intended caller; the browser never talks to `sms-gateway` directly for this step.

**`frontends/apps/admin/middleware.ts`'s Basic-auth gate (#48) is gone — a hard cutover, not a parallel path**, matching this repo's own standing convention. No `DASHBOARD_AUTH`/`DASHBOARD_BASIC_USERS`/`DASHBOARD_BASIC_REALM` env vars exist any more; `@vsms/env`'s cross-field validation now requires `ADMIN_BASE_URL` to be `https://` in production instead. The full flow: `GET /login` (middleware mints and encrypts a PKCE/state/nonce transaction into a short-lived `vsms_oidc_txn` cookie — has to happen in middleware, not the page component, because Next.js only allows `cookies().set()` in a Server Action/Route Handler/Middleware, never a plain Server Component render) → the login page's plain `<form method="post">` (no client JS) → `POST /api/auth/login` (reads the txn cookie server-side, never trusts the form for `state`/`codeChallenge`; calls `sms-gateway`'s `/login`; on success, redirects the *browser* to the URL that returned — a genuine HTTP redirect, not a JSON response, so `state` is verified on a real callback the way #194 requires) → `GET /api/auth/callback` (**verifies `state`** against the txn cookie, exchanges `code` at the real `/token` with the real `code_verifier`, **verifies the `id_token`** — signature via `sms-gateway`'s own `/jwks.json`, `iss`, `aud`, `exp`, and **`nonce`** against the same txn cookie — then encrypts a `vsms_session` cookie and redirects to `/`). Both cookies are `jose` JWE (`EncryptJWT`/`jwtDecrypt`, direct `A256GCM`, key = SHA-256 of `SMS_CONSOLE_SESSION_SECRET`), `HttpOnly`/`Secure` (in production)/`SameSite=Lax`. `frontends/apps/admin/lib/oidc.ts` is deliberately Edge-and-Node-portable (`jose` runs identically under both) because `middleware.ts` — Edge runtime, cannot opt into Node — needs the identical decrypt/verify logic route handlers use, or the two would silently drift.

**Not wired up in this PR, flagged rather than silently assumed done:** every `admin` → `sms-gateway` call still authenticates as the shared `SMS_CONSOLE_CLIENT_ID` machine credential (`frontends/packages/gateway/src/token.ts`), not the logged-in human's own access token — #194 built the login mechanism and the session, not the rewiring of every existing tRPC route to use a per-request human token. That's what makes `#50` (message detail, app-scoped) buildable now rather than done. Also unwired: rate limiting on `/login` itself (the same class of gap `#156`/`#168` closed for `/token`, not yet closed here), and `admin`'s own display of "which role is this" (`Session.role` is stamped `""` — the `id_token` carries no role claim by design, and there's no cheap way to fetch one without a dedicated endpoint; cosmetic only, Layer 3, never a security boundary).

**Provisioning tooling, mirroring `provision-client`'s own conventions:** `sms-gateway seed-console-client` registers the `sms-console` `OauthClient` row (`tokenEndpointAuthMethod: none` — a public BFF client, PKCE is the protection, no column could hold a shared secret anyway), idempotent via the same create-then-catch-`23505` shape `create_or_find_provider` uses. `sms-gateway provision-user` creates a `User` + `UserCredential`, generates a random 24-character password (never an operator-supplied `--password` flag — that would land in shell history and the process list, the exact exposure `provision-client`'s own `--key-out` file exists to avoid), and prints it exactly once. Neither seeds `Role` rows: §5.2's six built-in roles are not seeded by any migration, matching this repo's "no seed data assumed" posture — the very first `owner` account has to be bootstrapped by hand against a fresh database (see the deploy runbook), the same bootstrapping problem `rotate-signing-key` already has for the OP's own signing key.

**Every one of the four required guard-failure proofs was run for real, output captured, not merely asserted:**

- **PKCE verifier mismatch** — `backends/apps/sms-gateway/tests/login_flow_live_postgres.rs`'s `a_wrong_pkce_code_verifier_is_refused_at_the_token_exchange` spawns the real `sms-gateway` binary, completes a real `/login`, and presents a `code_verifier` that doesn't hash to the original `code_challenge` at `/token` — refused with `invalid_grant`, real HTTP, real Postgres, unmodified library code doing the check.
- **State mismatch** — `frontends/apps/admin/lib/oidc.test.ts`'s `a_mismatched_state_is_rejected` (Vitest). Broken for real: `verifyState` temporarily changed to `return true` unconditionally past the empty-string guard, which made this test fail with `expected true to be false`; restoring the real `timingSafeEqual` comparison restored a pass.
- **Inactive-user rejection** — `backends/crates/sms-auth/tests/login_live_postgres.rs`'s `a_correct_password_against_a_deactivated_account_is_refused`. Broken for real: `find_active_user`'s `.and(user::active().is_true())` clause temporarily removed, which made a deactivated account's exactly-correct password authenticate successfully (`Ok(AuthenticatedUser { ... })` where an `Err` was expected); restoring the clause restored the refusal.
- **`User`'s system-read policy** — `backends/crates/sms-api/tests/system_context_golden_list_live_postgres.rs`'s `every_system_readable_model_actually_admits_a_system_read`, same mechanism as every prior instance of this file's own repeated `hasRole('system')` gap: `hasRole('system')` pulled from `User`'s `read` clause made the test fail naming `User` and its broken clause; restored, it passed again.

**Found in review, closed in the same PR: a `Role` keyed literally `"system"` escalated any human assigned to it to full system privilege.** `Role.key`'s own `@regex` (`^[a-z][a-z0-9_]{2,31}$`) has no way to exclude specific literals, and `@db_enforce` is a silent no-op on `@regex` fields regardless (§2.0) — so nothing in the schema stopped an `owner` from creating a `Role` keyed `"system"` through ordinary generated CRUD (`Role.create`'s own `@@allow` is `hasRole('owner')`) and assigning a human `User` to it. `load_human_principal` (`backends/crates/sms-api/src/auth.rs`) put `Role.key` straight into `Principal.role` with no filtering, and `hasRole('system')` matches on that claim verbatim — so that human's next login would read `OauthSigningKey.privateKeyPem` (the key that signs every token this system issues) and every `UserCredential.passwordHash`, both `hasRole('system')`-gated, through generated CRUD. `owner` is the top of the *human* vocabulary; §5.2 is explicit that `system` is a different, synthetic thing, "not a row in roles... constructible only inside a process" — this bug collapsed that distinction.

Closed two independent ways, deliberately, not one — this is the one invariant in this system worth defence in depth for: **(1)** `backends/crates/sms-api/src/auth.rs`'s `load_human_principal` refuses a `role_key` of `"system"` or `"app"` (`RESERVED_ROLE_KEYS`) before ever constructing a `Principal` from it, with the same non-distinguishing `CratestackError::Unauthorized("unknown or inactive user")` an unknown/inactive user already gets; **(2)** `roles_key_not_reserved_check` (§2.10, `CHECK (key NOT IN ('system', 'app'))`) refuses the row at the database level, so the first guard is never the only thing standing between a human and `hasRole('system')`. `"app"` is reserved alongside `"system"` defensively — `GatewayAuth`'s own machine-caller sentinel `role: "app"` matches no `hasRole(...)` clause today, so a human `Role` keyed `"app"` is confusing rather than exploitable, but reserving it costs nothing.

Both guards were proven to actually fail, not just asserted to pass — the fifth and sixth instances of this file's own "prove your guards can fail" standard in this PR alone:

- **The database guard**: `backends/crates/sms-auth/tests/login_live_postgres.rs`'s `a_role_keyed_system_is_rejected_by_the_database_check` / `a_role_keyed_app_is_also_rejected_by_the_database_check` — a plain `db.role().create()` with `key: "system"` (or `"app"`) through the real delegate, asserting `db_sqlstate() == Some("23514")` (Postgres `check_violation`). Verified live against a real Postgres 16: `ERROR: new row for relation "roles" violates check constraint "roles_key_not_reserved_check"`.
- **The point-of-use guard**: `backends/apps/sms-gateway/tests/login_flow_live_postgres.rs`'s `a_role_keyed_system_never_authenticates_even_past_the_database_check` — the harder proof, since the database guard makes the exploit row unreachable through any real path. `seed_reserved_role_login_account` briefly `ALTER TABLE ... DROP CONSTRAINT`s `roles_key_not_reserved_check` (the R1 exception named in `CONTRIBUTING.md` for this file — schema DDL to build an otherwise-unreachable fixture, not a production data-access bypass; every actual row write still goes through a real delegate) to construct a `User` whose `roleKey` genuinely resolves to `"system"`, then restores the constraint once the fixture no longer needs to exist (`ReservedRoleFixture::cleanup` — reassigns `User.roleKey` off the reserved `Role` first, since `User`'s own `@@soft_delete` means a plain `delete()` leaves the FK reference in place and blocks re-adding the constraint; found live, not assumed, the first version of this test tried deleting `User` first and failed with `violates foreign key constraint "users_role_key_fkey"`). Both password auth and the OAuth token exchange succeed — neither has any concept of role to refuse — so the refusal has to happen, and does, the moment the resulting access token is presented to any real route: with `RESERVED_ROLE_KEYS.contains(...)` temporarily commented out in `load_human_principal`, this test failed with `left: 200, right: 401` (the reserved-role token successfully listed `Role` rows); restoring the check restored the `401`.

## Milestone 4: Providers, Routes, and a route simulator landed (#54)

The engine already existed (#62): `sms_routing::select_route` computes the whole answer, including the explanation trail, and injects its random draw rather than generating it internally specifically so a replay can be deterministic. #54's actual job was exposing that `Decision`, not recomputing one — a client-side reimplementation of matching, however careful, could drift from the engine the moment either changed and would then confidently show the wrong answer.

**`backends/crates/sms-api/src/route_simulator.rs` is a second, necessarily separate copy of `backends/crates/sms-worker/src/routing.rs`'s own fetch-and-convert glue, not a shared call.** `sms-api` cannot depend on `sms-worker` — the dependency runs the other way, confirmed by `sms-worker.workspace = true` sitting in `sms-api`'s own `[dev-dependencies]` (test-only). The root `Cargo.toml`'s own comment on the `sms-routing` dependency edge had already named this exact situation as expected ("a future #54 simulator procedure in sms-api is expected to depend on this directly too"). What's shared is the pure engine crate itself; the ~60 lines of `Route`/`Provider` row conversion around it are duplicated, the same tradeoff `sms-provider-mtn`'s own `classify_transport_error` duplication already accepted for a two-crate case. `Procedures::simulate` (`procedures.rs`) reuses `classify_operator` — the same `OperatorPrefixRule`-backed classification `sendMessage` uses — rather than a third copy, and applies the identical `Msisdn::parse_mobile` validation bar a real send would.

**Two schema policy changes, the same shape #56/#57 already established for `Job`.** `Provider.read` and `Route.read` both gained `auth().kind == "app"` — neither model carries an `appId` to scope a row-level predicate by, so this is the "unscoped but Layer 2-gated" shape, not a narrower one. `router::PROVIDER_ROUTE_READ_ROUTES` (a third, independent `enforce_route_permission` instance, same reasoning `job_rbac_state` already gives for not merging into one slice) gates `GET /providers`/`GET /providers/{id}`/`GET /routes`/`GET /routes/{id}` on `provider:read`/`route:read` — real perimeter, not defense in depth, for the identical reason `JOB_READ_ROUTES` is: an `auth().kind == "app"` caller passes Layer 1 unconditionally now, so only a granted scope stands between a provisioned app client and every row in either table. `simulateRoute` itself never needed the `@@allow` change — it already read under `sys()`, admitted by the pre-existing `hasRole('system')` clause — but gates itself on the same `route:read` permission via `require_permission`, matching `workerLocks`/`requeueJob`'s own precedent for a procedure rather than a generated route.

**Write access is untouched — and #194 landed mid-PR, which changes *why* it's still unreachable, not *whether*.** `Provider.update` and `Route.create`/`update`/`delete` carry no `auth().kind == "app"` clause, unchanged by this PR. #194 (human login, PR #206) merged into `main` while this PR was in progress — rebasing onto it, not around it, was the correct response, and re-checking every "cannot succeed until #194 lands" claim this PR had written before that landing was the point of doing so; leaving them as-is would have been exactly the kind of stale doc-drift this file exists to prevent. **`GatewayAuth` genuinely does mint a real `hasRole('owner')`-meaningful context for a real human token now** — #194's own section above is the source of truth for that mechanism. What #194's own section *also* already says, in its own "Not wired up in this PR" paragraph: "every `admin` → `sms-gateway` call still authenticates as the shared `SMS_CONSOLE_CLIENT_ID` machine credential..., not the logged-in human's own access token." Confirmed directly against the diff, not inferred from that sentence alone: `frontends/packages/gateway/` (`token.ts` included) is entirely absent from #194's own changed-file list — every function this PR's `providers.ts`/`routes.ts` calls, and every other screen's own gateway module, still resolves its bearer token exclusively through the pre-existing `client_credentials` machine seam, `role: "app"` always. `GatewayAuth::human_client_id` (default `"sms-console"`, `DEFAULT_CONSOLE_CLIENT_ID`) is a *different* `OauthClient` than `SMS_CONSOLE_CLIENT_ID` — the browser session's own real, role-bearing `accessToken` (`frontends/apps/admin/lib/oidc.ts::Session.accessToken`) sits in an encrypted cookie, read by `frontends/apps/admin/middleware.ts` to gate which pages the browser may open, and reaches nothing this PR's screens call. So `Provider.update`/`Route.create`/`update`/`delete` remain exactly as unreachable through these screens as before #194 landed — the fix is a separate, larger piece of work (forwarding a per-session human token through `frontends/packages/gateway`, replacing or supplementing its single static credential for every screen, not just these three) that #54's own scope doesn't include on its own. The Providers/Routes screens' edit/create/delete forms are real, wired code regardless — `frontends/packages/gateway/src/providers.ts`/`routes.ts`, going through #59's own `fetchWithEtag`/`updateWithIfMatch`, plus two new `rest.ts` siblings (`postJson`/`deleteResource` — a `DELETE` needs no `If-Match` at all, confirmed by reading `cratestack-macros-0.7.10/src/axum/model/prep/etag.rs` directly: `EtagTokens` only ever wires tokens for `update`/`get`, nothing for delete) — not hidden behind a flag, stated on screen instead, matching this file's own "no dormant code" convention. Proven live, the same rigor this file's own "prove your guards can fail" standard asks for elsewhere: `Save`/`Delete` clicked for real against a real gateway (`just demo`) returned a real `403`, body `missing required permission "provider:update"` — the exact, specific denial this reasoning predicts, not a generic failure.

**A live-data finding, not assumed: `GET /providers`/`GET /routes` return a bare JSON array, not `{ items, totalCount, pageInfo }`.** `messages.ts`/`jobs.ts`'s own envelope only applies to `@@paged` models; `Provider`/`Route` aren't. Confirmed by reading `cratestack-macros-0.7.10/src/axum/model/prep.rs`'s own `list_response_type` (`Vec<ProjectedValue>` when `!paged`) directly, not inferred from the two existing examples.

**The guard this ticket asked for — proven to actually fail, not just pass by construction.** `route_simulator.rs`'s `the_wire_result_matches_the_engines_own_decision` asserts every field of the wire result against `sms_routing::select_route`'s own `Decision` for the identical inputs. Temporarily hardcoding `decision_to_wire`'s `Eligible` arm to always report `winning_band: false` reproduced a real failure (`assertion left == right failed / left: false / right: true`) before being reverted.

**Two real bugs found live, in this PR's own new test file, not in production code — worth recording because both are the same "verify against live execution" shape this file keeps rediscovering.** `backends/crates/sms-api/tests/simulate_route_live_postgres.rs` first asserted `result.evaluations.len() == 1` for a single seeded route, reasoning that `matchAppId`-scoping a route makes it invisible to another candidate — wrong: `Decision.evaluations` covers *every* `Route` row in the table unconditionally by design (so a caller never has to re-derive "why wasn't route X picked" from anything else), and running the suite together (not just this file's own DB-free unit tests) surfaced another test's leftover, correctly-`predicate_failed` route sitting in the same list. Second, a determinism test asserting "the heavier-weighted route always wins at draw=0.9" failed intermittently — not because replay was non-deterministic (it wasn't: the same draw against the same route set reliably reproduced the same winner, the actual property under test), but because the test's own assumption that creation order determines which route gets the low vs. high share of `[0.0, 1.0)` was wrong. The band is ordered by `id` ascending (matching production, `fetch_routes_and_providers`'s own `.order_by(route::id().asc())`), and `Cuid` generation isn't correlated with creation order — confirmed by rerunning the same test repeatedly and watching which of two named routes sorted first vary. Both fixed by deriving expectations from the engine's own reported evaluations/tie-break ranges rather than from an assumption about row identity.

The admin console gained three screens (`frontends/apps/admin/app/providers`, `frontends/apps/admin/app/routes`, `frontends/apps/admin/app/simulator`) — the first screens in this console to mount `@vsms/ui`'s `Toaster` (added to `frontends/apps/admin/app/providers.tsx`'s own provider tree) and the first to exercise a `PATCH`/`POST`/`DELETE` write path at all. `scripts/demo.sh` and `docs/runbooks/getting-started.adoc` both gained `provider:read`/`route:read` in the console's provisioned scopes; touching `getting-started.md` also surfaced and fixed a pre-existing drift its own `SMS_CONSOLE_SCOPE=sms:send sms:read` example line had carried since #56/#57 landed — three scopes short of what that same doc's own `provision-client` command two paragraphs above it already requested.

**A third real bug, found only by actually running `just demo` against a real gateway — TypeScript's own compiler cannot see this one at all.** A nullable optional column serialises as an explicit JSON `null`, not an omitted key — confirmed live, and a direct contradiction of `messages-screen.tsx`'s own documented claim for `Message.stateReason` ("a message with no `stateReason` simply has no `stateReason` key... all of them are, confirmed live"). `routes-screen.tsx`'s first draft rendered every wildcard route's predicates as `"operator=null, class=null, app-scoped, prefix=null"` instead of `"matches anything"`, because its own `!== undefined` check is `true` for a JSON `null`. Worse, `simulator-screen.tsx` crashed outright — `Cannot read properties of null (reading 'priority')` — the instant a single-route candidate (nothing to tie-break) sent `"tieBreak": null` and the screen's own `result.tieBreak !== undefined` guard let it through to `result.tieBreak.priority`. Neither `tsc` nor this repo's own `field?: T | undefined` convention catches this class of bug: the type annotations were internally consistent, just wrong about what the wire actually sends. Fixed at the one seam each affected value crosses — `frontends/packages/gateway/src/routes.ts::normalizeRoute`, `providers.ts::normalizeProvider`, `route-simulator.ts::normalizeResult` — each converting `null` to `undefined` exactly once, so every downstream consumer keeps the one convention this codebase already uses everywhere else, rather than every call site needing its own `!= null` check. Both crashes were reproduced for real (a genuine Next.js error overlay, a genuine mis-rendered predicate summary) before being fixed, not merely inferred from reading the code — the same "verify against live execution" discipline this file's own `#87` section is the reference example for, applied here to the TypeScript side of the seam rather than the Rust side for the first time.
## Milestone 6: audit log anchoring landed (#68)

`backends/crates/sms-worker/src/jobs/anchor_audit.rs` — the last unbuilt §7.5 job kind besides `verify_backup`/`poll_balance`/`probe_providers`/`reconcile_clients`/`cleanup_secrets`. The issue itself named no implementation; the design question ("hash chain vs a bare periodic digest vs external anchoring") had to be answered and justified, not assumed — full reasoning lives in that file's own module doc, not repeated here in full, but the load-bearing parts:

**A plain, unkeyed SHA-256 hash chain, not a Merkle snapshot and not external anchoring.** Each daily anchor folds every `cratestack_audit` row in its own period into a `rangeHash`, and chains that onto the *previous anchor's own* `chainHash` — so deleting or editing a covered audit row, or an entire past anchor row, both break a recomputation somewhere in the chain. A bare periodic snapshot (the design doc's own §4.7 suggestion, "hash the day's rows into a Merkle root") was rejected for exactly the gap a chain closes: without linking to what came before, deleting an entire past period's rows *and its own anchor* leaves every remaining anchor internally self-consistent — nothing contradicts it. External anchoring (publishing the digest somewhere this deployment cannot itself rewrite) is the strongest of the three and was rejected only for scope: no such service exists anywhere in `deploy/`, and standing one up is real infrastructure, not a job-kind PR.

**Be honest about what this proves — the module doc's own section, restated here because it's the point of the ticket.** An anchor stored in the same Postgres instance an attacker already has write access to proves less than "tamper-evident" implies: editing a covered audit row is caught (the next run's own re-verification, or a manual one); editing or deleting an entire past anchor row is caught too, as long as a *later* anchor still references its `chainHash`. **Deleting the single most-recent anchor before anything else references it is not caught by anything in this database** — an attacker with the same write access this job itself has, who can also recompute a fresh chain forward from that point, leaves no contradiction. Closing that needs real external anchoring or an offsite copy — named as follow-up, not built here, and not silently implied by the issue title.

**A keyed HMAC chain under `backends/crates/sms-api/src/pepper.rs`'s existing `HashPepper` was considered and rejected — not because it's weaker, but because it's a second, separate secret-management problem this ticket doesn't ask for.** It would raise the bar from "an attacker with only `psql` access can recompute a valid chain" to "...cannot, without the pepper too" — a real improvement. Rejected because `pepper.rs`'s own doc is explicit a pepper is designed to *rotate*, and rotation doesn't retroactively rehash anything: a keyed chain would need every historical pepper value kept around indefinitely just to keep re-verifying anchors signed under it, or every rotation would make every past anchor look like tampering. A plain unkeyed chain has no such coupling.

**A real framework bug, filed upstream, not worked around silently: `@length` on a nullable `String?` field breaks the *generated* `UpdateXInput::validate()`, unconditionally, regardless of whether `@@allow("update", ...)` exists to route to it.** `AuditAnchor.prevChainHash` was originally `String? @length(min: 64, max: 64)` — `cargo check -p sms-api` failed `E0308`, `&Option<String>` passed where `validate_length` needs `&str`. Read from the vendored source, not guessed: `cratestack-macros-0.7.10/src/model/inputs.rs`'s `generate_update_input_struct` unconditionally emits `Update{Model}Input` for every model (no gate on whether any `@@allow("update", ...)` clause exists to ever construct one), and its own `validate_impl_tokens(&fields, true)` wraps *every* field in one `Option<...>` for "present or absent" regardless of the field's own nullability — so a `String?` field becomes `Option<Option<String>>` there. `emit_field_validators`'s own optionality check (`cratestack-macros-0.7.10/src/validators/emit.rs`) only unwraps one layer, binding `value: &Option<String>` and passing it straight to `validate_length`, which wants `&str`. Fixed by *not* making `prevChainHash` nullable at all: the very first anchor uses a fixed 64-`'0'`-character genesis sentinel instead of `NULL` — a cleaner design in its own right (the field is never actually "absent" in the cryptographic sense; "no predecessor" is itself a fixed, known value), and it sidesteps the bug entirely rather than waiting on a fix. `rangeHash`/`chainHash` (both non-nullable) never hit this.

**The DDL is genuinely additive** — one new table, `audit_anchors` (`id`, `period_start` nullable, `period_end`, `row_count`, `range_hash`/`prev_chain_hash`/`chain_hash` each `@length(64,64) @db_enforce`, a real `CHECK`, `chain_hash @unique`, `created_at`), a `cs_cuid()` default and a `created_at` default (hand-added to §2.10's own lists — `AuditAnchor` doesn't `@use(Timestamps)`, no `updatedAt` at all, since nothing ever updates an anchor row, the same shape `DeliveryReceipt.receivedAt` already has), and one hand-written index (`audit_anchors_period_end_idx`, the "find the most recent anchor" lookup every run makes, same reasoning `oauth_signing_keys_active_idx` already documents for an analogous singleton-lookup index). `cratestack migrate diff` at an isolated, pin-matching `=0.7.10` CLI diffed to exactly that against the unmodified `0001_init` — confirmed, and both migrations applied to a real scratch Postgres with `ci/test-state-machine.sql` passing before being trusted.

**The eleventh instance of this file's own repeated `hasRole('system')` gap — flagged in advance, not found live**, the same discipline `Route`/`WebhookAttempt` established: `anchor_audit.rs`'s own `latest_anchor`/`verify_chain_linkage` read `AuditAnchor` under `sys` every run (the job needs the previous anchor's `chainHash` to chain the next one, and needs every prior anchor to re-verify linkage), so `AuditAnchor` shipped with `hasRole('system')` on `read` from the start and was added to `SYSTEM_READABLE_MODELS` in the same PR, not discovered broken afterward.

**R1 exceptions, the seventh and an eighth, test-only one.** `cratestack_audit` has no schema model — same reasoning `drain.rs`/`reap_outbox.rs` already establish for `cratestack_event_outbox`: no delegate, no row-level policy to bypass, no audit trail to skip (a `SELECT` isn't a mutation, and this *is* the audit table besides). The eighth, `backends/crates/sms-worker/tests/anchor_audit_live_postgres.rs`, is the one genuinely new shape: proving tamper-evidence means the test itself has to tamper with a raw `cratestack_audit` row (and, separately, a raw `audit_anchors` row — `AuditAnchor` has no update capability at all, legitimate or otherwise) the same way a real attacker would, simulating the exact threat model this job defends against.

**A live-test-methodology trap worth internalising for the next job whose own `now` has to be pushed forward, not backward, to prove coverage.** `cratestack_audit.occurred_at` has no delegate seam to backdate — it's `chrono::Utc::now()`, stamped by the framework itself. The only way to prove a freshly seeded row falls inside a period is to push the *anchor's* `now` argument forward past `ANCHOR_LAG` instead. The first cut of `anchor_audit_live_postgres.rs` did that independently in three separate test functions sharing one binary's database (`sms_test_support` gives one database per binary, not per test) and two of them started failing `rowCount >= 1` — reproduced live: an anchor's `periodStart` inherits the *previous* anchor's `periodEnd`, which only ever moves forward, never back. Once any call pushes it minutes ahead of real time — which covering a just-now row against a fixed 5-minute lag necessarily requires — every subsequent call's own freshly-seeded row, stamped at genuine unshifted wall-clock time only milliseconds later, is already older than that inherited floor, permanently, for the rest of the process; there is no way to walk it back down without real time actually elapsing. Fixed by confining the forward shift to exactly one test function in the whole file; every other test's own `run_at` call uses real, unshifted `Utc::now()` (which only ever anchors up to the past, never poisoning the floor for whoever runs next) and doesn't depend on covering a specific fresh row.

**Tamper detection was proven to actually detect tampering, twice, the same discipline this file already applies elsewhere: break the guard, watch the test fail, restore, watch it pass.** `verify_period_content` temporarily replaced with `Ok(true)` unconditionally — `anchoring_covers_new_rows_chains_correctly_and_detects_a_tampered_row` failed with `TAMPER TEST FAILED TO DETECT TAMPERING: verify_period_content still reported true...`, restored, passed again. `verify_chain_linkage`'s own `chainHash` recomputation check temporarily short-circuited (`if false && recomputed != anchor.chainHash`) — `a_tampered_anchor_row_breaks_chain_linkage` failed with the equivalent message naming `verify_chain_linkage`, restored, passed again.

## Milestone 6: consent records, quiet hours, and the classification exemption (#72) — landed 2026-08-12

The last M6 compliance story before audit anchoring (#68). Three pieces, and the second is the one that matters: `docs/architecture.md` §10 already said "keep the audit trail proving the classification" — the honest work here was deciding what that sentence can and cannot mean, then wiring the two checks whose asymmetry (`otp`/`transactional` exempt, `marketing`/`notification` not) is what makes a mislabelled class *consequential* in the first place.

**Consent is a new model, `ConsentRecord`, not `OptOut`'s inverse.** `OptOut` is the enforcement primitive — its presence is the entire mechanism `sendMessage` already checked. `ConsentRecord` is append-only evidence with provenance (who, when, channel, scope) that a caller produces *because* the classification or the consent itself gets challenged, and it has no `revokedAt` on purpose: withdrawing consent is a new `OptOut` row, the one thing `sendMessage` already treats as authoritative, so "does this recipient currently receive X" never has two disagreeing sources of truth to reconcile. `appId`-scoped, unlike `OptOut` (a recipient's opt-out has to bind everywhere in this deployment; a consent grant is a specific app's own customer relationship — App A's marketing consent doesn't authorise App B's). `scope MessageClass` reuses the existing enum rather than a second free-form string the way `OptOut.scope` is, so `ensure_consent_on_file` matches it against `Message.class` directly and a typo'd scope string can never silently drift from the class it was meant to describe.

**What the classification audit trail actually proves, said as plainly as the module doc says it — this is the load-bearing finding of this PR, not a footnote.** `Message.class` already carries `@@audit` (present before this PR), so every `sendMessage` call writes a framework audit row, in the same transaction as the `Message` insert, capturing the declared class and the authenticated caller's identity — a real, contemporaneous, tamper-resistant-absent-DB-compromise record of *who declared what, when*. It does **not** prove the declaration was *true*: content classification from free text is not machine-verifiable the way a state transition is, so a caller willing to mislabel marketing traffic as `transactional` bypasses every check this PR adds, and the audit trail records that they did so accurately, not honestly. `backends/crates/sms-api/src/consent.rs`'s own module doc carries the full reasoning; recording it here too because this is exactly the kind of claim that reads as solved from a section heading ("keep the audit trail") and isn't, the same shape this file's own doc-drift corrections keep finding elsewhere. Closing the actual gap needs either a manual compliance-review process reading the audit trail (this system's real answer today) or an independent content classifier — not built, not small, and not attempted here.

**Enforcement scope is derived from `docs/architecture.md` §10's own words, not invented.** Two exhaustively-matched pure functions in `backends/crates/sms-api/src/consent.rs`: `requires_recipient_consent_controls` (governs *both* opt-out honouring and the new consent check — §10 names the identical class pair for both, "`marketing` and `notification`. OTP and transactional are exempt") and `subject_to_quiet_hours` (narrower — `marketing` only; §10's quiet-hours bullet never names `notification`). Exhaustive `match`, not `matches!` with a wildcard, so a fifth `MessageClass` variant fails to compile here rather than silently defaulting either way — the same discipline this file's other "single source of truth" functions (`default_validity`, `message_event_type`) already use.

**A real, pre-existing gap found while wiring the first of those two functions in: opt-out honouring was unconditional for every class, not class-gated at all.** Before this PR, `ensure_not_opted_out` ran on every `sendMessage` call regardless of `class` — more restrictive than §10 ever specified (an opted-out recipient couldn't receive an OTP either), accidentally safe rather than a compliance hole, but not what was asked for. Fixed by gating the existing call site on `requires_recipient_consent_controls(class)`; `an_opted_out_recipient_is_refused_before_persistence` (pre-existing test) had to move from the default `transactional` class to `notification` to keep testing what it always meant to, and a new `an_opted_out_recipient_still_receives_an_otp_or_transactional_message` proves the exemption half directly.

**Quiet hours are a policy knob, deliberately not a hardcoded legal constant, and deliberately not runtime-configurable either.** `MARKETING_QUIET_HOURS_START_WAT`/`_END_WAT` are `pub const`, named and doc-commented as self-imposed best practice ("no Cameroon-specific statutory rule was found") rather than buried inline — the next reader should see a value to reconsider, not a rule to trust. Not wired to an env var or a `Settings` model: no such model exists yet, and this repo's own standing preference is a visible hard-coded decision over a half-built configurability seam nobody asked for. Enforced at `sendMessage` (accept time), not `dispatch` (hold-and-release) — both are defensible, and the choice here follows `send`'s own existing framing ("this procedure's job is the decision, not delivery"): a message accepted at 19:59 and held server-side until 08:00 the next day would sit silently in `queued` for up to twelve hours with no caller-visible signal, where an accept-time rejection gives the caller a synchronous, actionable answer immediately. The real cost: no built-in "schedule this campaign for the next quiet-hours window" — `SendMessageInput.scheduledAt` already exists for a caller to do that itself, but nothing here holds a message across the boundary automatically.

**Tests are structured around one real trap: quiet hours and consent share `marketing`, so a naive test suite would be flaky by time of day.** `consent.rs`'s own unit tests are deterministic and boundary-exact (07:59/08:00/19:59/20:00 WAT, both sides of each edge) with an injected clock, not `Utc::now()`. The live suite's consent-specific tests (`a_notification_send_with_no_consent_record_is_refused`, `a_notification_send_with_a_matching_consent_record_succeeds`, `a_consent_record_scoped_to_a_different_class_does_not_authorise_this_one`) deliberately use `notification`, not `marketing`, so quiet hours can never be the confounding reason for a pass or a failure. The one test that does exercise `marketing` end to end (`a_marketing_send_matches_the_real_clocks_current_quiet_hours_state`) is live-clock-adaptive by construction — it asks `consent::is_within_marketing_quiet_hours(Utc::now())`, the exact function `send()` itself calls, what today's expected outcome is, and asserts against that rather than against a fixed assumption about time of day. Correct and non-flaky at any hour this test happens to run, and it can never silently stop testing the real boundary the way a fixed "assume it's daytime" assumption could.

**The guard-failure proof this ticket asked for, run for real, output captured.** `a_marketing_send_is_refused_but_a_transactional_send_to_the_same_recipient_is_not` sends a `marketing` message with no consent on file (refused) and a `transactional` message to the *same* recipient (accepted) — the literal classification-exemption asymmetry #72 exists to prove. With `Self::ensure_consent_on_file(...)` temporarily short-circuited (`if false && ...`) in `Procedures::send`, both this test and `a_notification_send_with_no_consent_record_is_refused` failed with:

```
called `Result::unwrap_err()` on an `Ok` value: SendMessageResult { messageId: "c555dbfe772717725082f0c", state: accepted, encoding: gsm7, segments: 1, operator: mtn, estimatedCostXaf: 0 }
```
